My DUI Case — The Record

THE SUBJECT'S POSITION — HIS OWN WORDS
"The final verdict is whatever it is. As long as it is understood, I'm willing to fight for myself to the highest possible degree." What is asked is that the case be understood.
"Let it be known: I am willing to offer any and all information that they need from me for this case, truthfully."
"And there's also information that they don't need to know for this case. Is irrelevant. Only the facts that are needed to know — and they know what those are and I know what those are."
The line is relevance: what is needed for this case is within the scope of this declaration; what is not relevant to this case sits outside it, and is not part of this case.
"There is really no reason for us to ever need to speak. Other than an account of the incident."
Apart from his account of the incident, communication runs through counsel and the written record.
What is volunteered, and when, is provided through his attorney, at his attorney's direction — the declaration records his willingness; the channel is counsel.
"Let it also be known — as a statement of intent, and not as a threat: should the record in this matter disclose corruption or misconduct, I will bring it forward through lawful and respectful means, at the highest level available to me. I cannot be bought, persuaded, or settled into silence. Nothing concludes this effort except the lawful resolution of this case itself."

In summary — in his words and by his direction:
  • "I am only asking that the law be followed ethically."
  • Start as nice and friendly as they want it to be. (His direction for the packet's manner — courtesy governs the manner, never the contents.)
  • The law be followed; the record stays clean — no profanity in any legal material, including inside his own quoted words (vulgarity omitted, substance preserved, marked where cleaned).
  • "If they want to use loopholes to snag people, people can use loopholes to get out." (Rendered clean under the rule above.)
  • "No case big or small, is above the law."
  • Pertinence, not favor, decides what enters: "Include all this information, and have the info there to support it."
  • "Please add all this into my docket since this pertains directly to my case. It is my case."

Side A — Why I should legally be completely let off

The case for complete dismissal, stated door by door. Each door states the law, what the record currently shows, and what is still needed. Where the current record does not support a door, the door is marked CLOSED.

Door 1 · The Stop
Everything after the stop depends on the stop itself being lawful.
AWAITING INFORMATION

THE LAW

A traffic stop is a seizure. The officer needs of a traffic violation or a crime — specific facts, not a hunch — or . If the stop was unlawful, the evidence gathered from it can be suppressed, and a case built on that evidence can fall with it.

Source: U.S. Constitution, Fourth Amendment; Texas Code of Criminal Procedure.

WHAT THE RECORD SHOWS NOW — HIS ACCOUNT

Labeled HIS ACCOUNT — his words, October 29, 2026, until records confirm them:

  • The stop was for speeding. That is the reason for the stop as he states it.
  • There was no harm: no crash, no injury, no damage.

These two facts are entered as his account. The date and the paperwork record of the stop are still to come, below. The county is now supplied — Tarrant County (§00A).

THE SPEED ALLEGATION — THE STATE'S NUMBER BESIDE HIS ACCOUNT, AT THE SAME SIZE

Entered October 29, 2026, in his words: "I would like to see on the radar where it says I was going 57 and a 40. Because I'm pretty sure I wasn't. I could be wrong, but the video should prove it."

THE STATE'S ALLEGATIONHIS ACCOUNT
57 in a 40. The State alleges he was traveling 57 mph in a 40 mph zone — the radar figure behind the speeding stop, per his account of what he was told. Printed as THE STATE'S ALLEGATION: it is the State's number, not a finding on this page. He believes he was not going 57. In his own words: "I'm pretty sure I wasn't. I could be wrong." His belief is printed as UNVERIFIED. His actual speed: NOT SUPPLIED — he does not claim a different number, and none is printed for him.

THE ARBITER HE NAMED — IN HIS WORDS: "THE VIDEO SHOULD PROVE IT." The speed question is item one of the Video Audit (Door 7): when the footage of his own arrest is in hand, the first check run against it is this one — the recording, including any speed overlay or metadata the patrol system captured, held up against the alleged 57. The records that settle it are listed at Door 7, to be obtained through counsel.

WHAT'S STILL NEEDED

  • The date of the stop, and where the stop happened.
  • His fuller account of the stop, in his own words.
Door 2 · The Arrest
A lawful stop is not, by itself, a lawful arrest.
AWAITING INFORMATION

THE LAW

An arrest requires of its own: facts and circumstances enough for a prudent person to believe an offense was committed. The question is what the officer knew, and when he knew it.

Source: U.S. Constitution, Fourth Amendment; Texas Code of Criminal Procedure art. 14.01.

WHAT THE RECORD SHOWS NOW

Awaiting Tristan's account and the offense reports, which come through counsel.

WHAT'S STILL NEEDED

  • His account of the arrest: what was said, what was done, in what order.
  • The offense reports, through his lawyer.
Door 3 · The Tests as Run
The field tests only count if they were run the way they were designed to be run.
LAW: DOCUMENTED · RECORD: AWAITING INFORMATION

THE LAW — LAW LAYER: DOCUMENTED

The battery. The are the three-test battery: (1) , (2) Walk-and-Turn, and (3) One-Leg Stand — each administered and scored against the standardized clues in the NHTSA protocol.

The accuracy figure, with its conditions attached. The combined battery's figure — approximately 91% correct decisions at BAC ≥ 0.08 — comes from a 1998 validation study conducted under standardized conditions: trained officers, the standardized instructions, demonstration, surface, and scoring. The conditions are part of the claim. The figure describes decisions under those conditions; it is not a figure for the battery administered off-protocol.

Source: NHTSA 1998 SFST validation study, as reported in NHTSA training materials.

The Texas admissibility standard: substantial compliance, not perfection. In Texas, SFST results stand on with the NHTSA protocol. Plouff v. State, 192 S.W.3d 213, 219–221 (Tex. App.—Houston [14th Dist.] 2006), citing Compton v. State, 120 S.W.3d 375, 378 (Tex. App.—Texarkana 2003): slight variations in administration "may affect the weight to be given the testimony" but do not render the evidence inadmissible.

Sources: Plouff v. State, 192 S.W.3d 213, 219–221 (Tex. App.—Houston [14th Dist.] 2006); Compton v. State, 120 S.W.3d 375, 378 (Tex. App.—Texarkana 2003).

The standing structure, labeled as such. Even where standardized SFST results are excluded, an officer may still testify to plain observations — what the officer saw, heard, and smelled — as lay evidence. Exclusion of the standardized opinion does not erase lay observation testimony; the two travel on different foundations, and this page records them separately wherever they appear.

NHTSA's own manual concedes the physical tests were not designed for people over 65, people with back, leg, or inner-ear problems, or people 50 or more pounds overweight. It requires a dry, hard, level, non-slippery surface; heels over two inches come off; and officers are to ask first about disabilities, illness, injuries, and medications. (Stated in full at Door 4.)

Source: NHTSA DWI Detection and Standardized Field Sobriety Testing manual.

THE FLAWS EXHIBIT — THE VALIDATION RECORD

Researched October 29, 2026. Every claim below is labeled by source type, and every headline number is printed beside the conditions it was measured under — who was tested, at what average BAC, and by whom. A number without its conditions is not the record.

StudyHeadline number, as reportedConditions printed beside itSource type
1977 — Burns & Moskowitz, laboratory (DOT HS 802-424) The combined three-test battery correctly discriminated subjects below 0.10 from subjects at or above 0.10 83% of the time. The per-test figures officers are still taught trace to this programme: HGN 77%, Walk-and-Turn 68%, One-Leg Stand 65% — at the 0.10 criterion. Laboratory, dosed volunteers, 0.10 threshold — not roadside, and not the 0.08 limit the battery is now used at. [Court opinion — State v. Lasworth, summarising the NHTSA report] · [NHTSA's own documents — 2023 Participant Manual, Session 8]
1981 — Tharp, Burns & Moskowitz, laboratory + limited field (DOT HS 805-864) Officers classified 81% of volunteers correctly against the 0.10 line. Officers' BAC estimates differed from actual BAC by 0.03 on average. Interrater and test-retest reliabilities for the battery ranged 0.60 to 0.80. Laboratory: 10 police officers testing 297 drinking volunteers with BACs from 0 to 0.18. A limited field phase collected 3,128 data forms. [NHTSA's own documents — the report's own abstract]
1995 — Colorado validation (Burns & Anderson), field 86% correct arrest/release decisions overall; 93% of arrested drivers had a BAC of 0.05 or higher — and only 64% of decisions to release were correct. First full field study using experienced SFST officers. The criterion was 0.05 BAC (Colorado's Driving While Ability Impaired threshold) — not 0.08 and not 0.10. On this study's own numbers, errors were concentrated in the release direction — the opposite pattern from San Diego, below. [NHTSA's own documents — 2023 Participant Manual, Session 8, and the San Diego report's summary of it]
1997 — Florida validation (Burns & Dioquino), field “Correct decisions to arrest were made 95% of the time based on the three SFSTs.” The manual calls this the first study conducted at the 0.08 limit. What the manual's own wording claims is arrest-decision accuracy — the share of arrests that were correct — not 95% of all decisions, and the manual states no release-decision or below-limit failure rate alongside it. [NHTSA's own documents — 2023 Participant Manual, Session 8]
1998 — San Diego validation (Stuster & Burns, DOT HS 808 839) — the study behind the 91% figure at the top of this door Officers' estimates of above/below 0.08 were “accurate in 91 percent of the cases, or as high as 94 percent if explanations for some of the false positives are accepted.” Component accuracies: HGN 88%, Walk-and-Turn 79%, One-Leg Stand 83%. Correlations with measured BAC: HGN r = 0.65; the three tests combined r = 0.69. Seven officers of the San Diego Police Department's alcohol enforcement unit, all previously NHTSA-trained, given refresher training by Dr. Burns herself, all with at least three years in the Traffic Division. 297 motorists, each already stopped on reasonable suspicion of DWI. The sample was heavily skewed high: mean measured BAC of all 297 was 0.122; of the 217 arrested, 0.150; only 83 of 297 were below 0.08 at all. Scoring was modified for the study: 4 HGN clues were redefined to indicate 0.08. The report contains no accuracy figure isolated to subjects near the 0.08 threshold. [NHTSA's own documents — the report itself]

Sources: Stuster & Burns (1998), Validation of the Standardized Field Sobriety Test Battery at BACs Below 0.10 Percent, DOT HS 808 839: ojp.gov — San Diego validation report; NHTSA/IACP Participant Manual, 2023 curriculum, edited 4/2025: SFST Participant Manual (PDF). Read October 29, 2026.

THE FLAWS EXHIBIT — THE SAN DIEGO MATRICES

How this layer is labeled, exactly: the counts below are arithmetic on the study's own published figures (Figure 4, Figure 5, and Table 6 of the San Diego report), popularized by defense experts — they are not a headline the study itself printed. The framing built on them is labeled advocacy where it appears. Both halves of that sentence are part of the record.

  • False positives vs. false negatives, overall decisions: 24 vs. 4. Of the 83 drivers actually below 0.08, 24 (29%) were estimated by officers to be at or above 0.08. Of 234 arrest estimates, 24 (10%) were wrong. The 94% headline is reached by reclassifying 10 of the 24 false positives as correct — nine had BACs of 0.07–0.079, plus one juvenile at 0.069 in a zero-tolerance jurisdiction.
  • Per-test failure rates among drivers below the limit (drivers below 0.08 who met the “failing” criterion): HGN — 30 of 81 (37%); Walk-and-Turn — 40 of 76 (53%); One-Leg Stand — 31 of 75 (41%). These rates are not stated in the report's conclusions; they are computed from its Figure 5 matrices.
  • The false positives were not all near-misses: measured BACs among them include 0.020, 0.028, 0.030, 0.037, 0.043, 0.048, and 0.050. One false-positive subject (estimated 0.08, measured 0.050) “was found to be a psychiatric patient, which helped to explain her erratic behavior, poor SFST performance, and apparent impairment” — the report's own example of a person impaired for a non-alcohol reason failing the battery.
  • The decision step was not mechanical: in 7 of the 24 false-positive cases, officers' BAC estimates were inconsistent with the HGN clues they themselves recorded — the report speculates other observations or possible drug use influenced the estimates.
  • At the 0.04 criterion, officers' decisions not to arrest were only 52% accurate — a figure the report acknowledges while still concluding the battery “strongly suggests” accurate discrimination at 0.04.

Source type: [NHTSA's own documents — San Diego report, Figures 4, 5, 7 and Table 6; percentages are arithmetic on those tables, as reproduced in defense-expert literature (Ott / International Association of Forensic Toxicology Consultants) and checked against the primary tables].

THE FLAWS EXHIBIT — THE MANUAL'S OWN WORDS

All quotations in this layer are [NHTSA's own documents] — the current Participant Manual (2023 curriculum, edited 4/2025), unless an earlier edition is named.

  • Not a pass/fail test: “Remember the SFSTs are a tool to assist you in seeing visible signs of impairment and are not a pass/fail test.” (Walk-and-Turn section.)
  • Clue counts alone are not enough: “it is not enough to report the number of clues on the three tests. The numbers are important to the police officer in the field because they help determine whether there is probable cause to arrest. But to secure a conviction, more descriptive evidence is needed.”
  • The standardization warning — with its documented history: the current manual states validation applies only when the tests are administered in the prescribed standardized manner, and: “If any one of the SFST elements is changed, the validity may be compromised.” The 2000 edition of the same passage ended categorically — “the validity is compromised” — as quoted by courts (State v. Homan, Ohio; United States v. Horn). The change from “is” to “may” is a documented softening in NHTSA's own text over time; which edition an officer was trained under is a checkable fact in a given case.
  • What the tests indicate: the San Diego report itself states the developers pursued tests that would give “statistically valid and reliable indications of a driver's BAC, rather than indications of driving impairment,” and that “the objective of the test is to discriminate between drivers above and below the statutory BAC limit, not to measure driving impairment.” The link between BAC and impairment “is a separate issue.”
  • Reliability in the field: “This type of ‘inter-rater’ reliability was impossible to measure directly during this study, due to the constraints imposed by field conditions.” The report substitutes the 1981 laboratory inter-rater figure (r = .72, rising to .80) — no field measurement exists.
  • The subject-pool facts — fewer than 1.5% of the original subjects over 65, and no weight data recorded in the final report behind the 50-lb caveat — are printed in full at Door 4, from the same manual, and are not duplicated here.

THE FLAWS EXHIBIT — SOBER PEOPLE FAILING, WITH LIMITS PRINTED AT THE SAME SIZE

  • 46% of stone-sober subjects judged too impaired. [Peer-reviewed study — Cole & Nowaczyk, Perceptual and Motor Skills 79:99–104 (1994).] 21 volunteers, all at 0.00 BAC, were videotaped performing SFST-type tests and “normal abilities” tests. 14 experienced officers viewing the tapes judged on average 46% of the sober subjects “too impaired to drive” on the SFSTs, vs. 15% on the normal-abilities tests. Its limits, at the same size: a small study (21 subjects); judgements made from video, not live administration; HGN was not included, because eye movements could not be videotaped adequately; and officers were asked about impairment (“too impaired to drive”), not the validated BAC criterion.
  • 26% of sober subjects failed the battery. [Peer-reviewed study — Yoshizuka et al., J. Forensic Toxicology & Pharmacology 3:2 (2014).] Pooled across drug-comparison studies, 49 of 185 (26%) drug-naïve subjects failed the SFST battery overall; per-test failures were concentrated in Walk-and-Turn (~22%), vs. HGN ~2% and One-Leg Stand ~7% — a distribution notably different from San Diego's below-limit drivers. The paper's own methodological point: the original validation research “did not utilize a standard control group to validate that the failure of the SFST was not a characteristic of the population at large.” Its limits, at the same size: subjects were pooled across heterogeneous small studies, tested in a research setting, not roadside, and there was no standard control group; the journal is a low-profile open-access forensic journal. The same paper recomputes San Diego's 24 of 83 below-0.08 subjects (29%) — matching the matrices layer above.

What these two studies establish, stated plainly: a non-trivial sober failure rate exists. What they do not establish: a precise roadside sober-failure rate.

Sources: Cole & Nowaczyk (1994), abstract: psycnet.apa.org — Cole & Nowaczyk; Yoshizuka et al. (2014): touroscholar.touro.edu — Yoshizuka et al. Read October 29, 2026.

THE FLAWS EXHIBIT — THE STRUCTURE, AS DOCUMENTED FACT

  • The tests come last, after suspicion. [NHTSA's own documents — the manual's three-phase model.] The SFSTs are “Phase Three: Pre-Arrest Screening.” Phase Two ends with the decision to order the driver out of the vehicle, and the manual states that decision “usually is made after you have developed a suspicion the driver is impaired,” and that an officer “may have developed a strong suspicion the driver is impaired prior to the face-to-face observation.” By design, the battery is never administered to a neutral sample.
  • The scorer is never blind. The same officer who stopped the driver, observed the driving, and noted odour, speech, or admissions then scores the borderline clues — whether an arm rose six inches, whether pursuit was “smooth.” There is no blinding at any point. This is visible in San Diego's design itself: subjects entered only on reasonable suspicion, and officers' estimates explicitly combined SFST results with “observations of a driver's general appearance and speech” — so the 91% figure is the accuracy of officers' decisions in context, not of clue counts in isolation. [NHTSA's own documents; the isolation point is made independently by [peer-reviewed review — Rubenzer 2008] and [independent re-analysis — Kane & Kane 2021, not clearly peer-reviewed].]
  • The validation concentration — documented fact: Marcelline Burns authored or co-authored every validation study in the table above (1977, 1981, Colorado 1995, Florida 1997, San Diego 1998 as co-author, and the 2007 Robustness study), conducted the San Diego officers' refresher training herself, and co-analysed the San Diego data. That the programme was substantially designed, executed, analysed, and defended by the same small SCRI/Anacapa group under NHTSA contract is verifiable from the reports' title pages. The inference that this biased the results is [defense-bar / advocacy claim] — it is labeled as advocacy here, not printed as a finding. The absence of a large, fully independent replication is a statement about the literature that Rubenzer 2008 also makes from within peer review.
  • A counterweight, printed at the same size: the record does not show a battery with no signal. Every validation study, including the critical re-analyses, finds a real correlation between SFST clues and BAC — in San Diego, r = 0.65 for HGN alone and r = 0.69 for the three tests combined. [NHTSA's own documents.]

THE FLAWS EXHIBIT — THE HONEST LIMITS, PRINTED AT THE EXHIBIT

No document states the tests were designed to fail anyone. No NHTSA document, validation report, or peer-reviewed study states a design intent to fail sober people. Intent is NOT documented, and this page does not claim it. (Cole & Nowaczyk's title is a question — “Are they designed for failure?” — and their data address officers' judgements of sober performance, not designers' intent.) What IS documented is the structure printed above: the validation conditions, the matrices, the manual's own words, the sober-subject studies with their limits, the never-blind scorer, and the Texas structure at Door 5.

The genuine unknowns, printed as unknowns:

  • Accuracy near 0.08 — the validation samples averaged 0.12–0.15 BAC; no study isolates accuracy for a driver at 0.075 vs. one at 0.085.
  • Age, weight, and medical effects as measured variables — the manual's caveats rest on variables that were barely sampled (age) or not recorded at all (weight).
  • Live roadside agreement between officers — inter-rater reliability was never measured in the field; the only figures are from the 1981 laboratory.
  • Independent blinded replication at 0.08 — none exists on this record.
  • Specific roadside conditions as controlled variables — surface, lighting, weather, and footwear have not been tested as controlled variables on this record.

These are listed as open questions. They are not filled in from either side's advocacy.

THE SFST DIVIDING LINE — WHERE THE DOOR OPENED, AND WHERE IT DID NOT

Researched October 29, 2026. This layer is the case-law record of the smallest administration failures courts have acted on — and the ones they have refused to act on. Only the HGN test receives scientific gatekeeping in Texas: under Emerson v. State, 880 S.W.2d 759 (Tex. Crim. App. 1994), the theory and technique are judicially noticed as valid, but the State must still prove the technique was applied properly on the occasion, following the NHTSA standardized procedures — three clues per eye, including screening for equal pupil size, equal tracking, and resting nystagmus or medical causes. Walk-and-Turn and One-Leg Stand testimony is treated as lay observation, not Emerson science.

The Texas dividing line, on this record: the door opens on an officer's admission of invalidity, an omitted test component, or a total absence of foundation. It does not open on a stopwatch dispute — timing deviations go to weight, not admissibility. And even when the door opens, the recurring Texas outcome is error held harmless with the conviction affirmed — not dismissal. This layer doubles as the checklist for the Video Audit of Tristan's own arrest footage (Door 7): every item below is a question the footage will be asked, frame by frame, when it is in hand.

THE DIVIDING LINE — THE DOOR OPENED (TEXAS)

CaseWhat brokeWhat the court didOutcome category
McRae v. State, 152 S.W.3d 739 (Tex. App.—Houston [1st Dist.] 2004, pet. ref'd) The officer admitted at the ALR hearing he performed only 2 of 3 HGN components (never tested onset before 45°), made one pass per eye instead of two, and gave conflicting timing testimony; confronted at trial, he agreed there was no “valid HGN test.” The trial court abused its discretion admitting the six-clue HGN testimony; not a “slight variation” under Emerson. ERROR HELD HARMLESS — CONVICTION AFFIRMED (Intoxilyzer evidence and driving facts). Not a dismissal, not a reversal. Printed with that limit, at full size.
Smothers v. State, No. 02-03-00056-CR (Tex. App.—Fort Worth 2004, no pet., unpublished; as described in Bryant) The officer's admission he did not complete the minimum number of passes. HGN failed Emerson. Exclusion-side ruling (described secondhand; posture as in Bryant).
Tillinghast v. State, No. 02-04-00553-CR (Tex. App.—Fort Worth 2005, no pet.; as described in Bryant) No foundation at all — the officer said only that she performed the HGN and tried to give clear instructions. The trial court abused its discretion admitting HGN without proof of NHTSA compliance. Appellate exclusion ruling.
State v. Rudd, 255 S.W.3d 293 (Tex. App.—Waco 2008, pet. ref'd) The State failed to prove proper HGN administration (later briefing emphasized the HGN was administered off-camera while other tests were recorded — a factor, not the holding). HGN exclusion AFFIRMED on the State's appeal; the separate unlawful-detention suppression was reversed; remanded. EVIDENCE EXCLUDED ONLY — NOT A DISMISSAL.
Kirby v. State (Tex. App.—Houston [1st Dist.] 2008) The One-Leg Stand was not properly administered or demonstrated. The trial court excluded the OLS testimony; the Walk-and-Turn was admitted on substantial compliance despite non-verbatim instructions. Partial exclusion; conviction posture affirmed.

Sources: McRae: caselaw.findlaw.com — McRae; Rudd: law.justia.com — Rudd; Bryant (reciting Smothers and Tillinghast): law.justia.com — Bryant; Kirby: law.justia.com — Kirby. Read October 29, 2026.

THE DIVIDING LINE — THE DOOR DID NOT OPEN (TEXAS): STOPWATCH DISPUTES GO TO WEIGHT

  • Compton v. State, 120 S.W.3d 375 (Tex. App.—Texarkana 2003, pet. ref'd): smooth pursuit run in ~11 seconds instead of the recommended 16 (≈2.5 seconds fast per eye) — did not invalidate the HGN; the manual gives approximations. Separately, the officer failed to instruct the defendant to keep his arms at his side on the One-Leg Stand: admitting that test was error, but harmless — the omission made the test easier, and he failed anyway. Conviction affirmed.
  • Plouff v. State, 192 S.W.3d 213 (Tex. App.—Houston [14th Dist.] 2006, no pet.): the whole HGN took ~53 seconds against an alleged 82-second minimum — “not a meaningful variation.” It is unreasonable, the court held, that “any variation… no matter how slight” automatically undermines admissibility.
  • McCarthy v. State, No. 01-12-00240-CR (Tex. App.—Houston [1st Dist.] 2013, no pet.): alleged omitted onset passes, maximum-deviation holds of 2–3 seconds instead of 4, and vertical movement of the stimulus — all held to go to weight, not admissibility, precisely because, unlike McRae, the officer never admitted the test was invalid and testified he screened pupils and medical conditions.
  • York v. State, No. 14-16-00994-CR (Tex. App.—Houston [14th Dist.] 2018): on station video, the stimulus was held 3 seconds instead of the prescribed 4 on each maximum-deviation pass; the technician conceded 3 seconds would be improper but said he counted 4; the trial court held the difference “not sufficiently significant to render results invalid.” BAC .116/.113. Conviction posture affirmed.
  • Bryant v. State, No. 02-08-00294-CR (Tex. App.—Fort Worth 2010): HGN administered with the defendant seated instead of standing — presumed a variation, held not to undermine admissibility automatically.
  • Taylor v. State, No. 03-03-00624-CR (Tex. App.—Austin 2006, pet. ref'd): one pass per eye for smooth pursuit instead of two; 3-second instead of 4-second holds — “negligible,” no showing the result would change.
  • Ellis v. State, 86 S.W.3d 759 (Tex. App.—Waco 2002, pet. ref'd): the officer admitted he never completed the 30 practice cases required by his NHTSA course and was not certified to perform HGN — he should not have testified; error harmless.

Sources: TDCAA DWI case-law update: tdcaa.com — Field Sobriety Tests; McCarthy: law.justia.com — McCarthy. Read October 29, 2026.

THE DIVIDING LINE — OUT OF STATE, AT THE SAME SIZE: SMALLER DEVIATIONS DID WORK

  • Ohio — State v. Homan, 89 Ohio St.3d 421 (2000): strict compliance. For SFSTs to support probable cause, they had to be administered in strict compliance with standardized procedures, because “the small margins of error that characterize field sobriety tests make strict compliance critical.” The trooper had deviated in two of three tests — yet probable cause was upheld on the remaining facts and the conviction was affirmed; the tests were excluded only from the probable-cause calculus. Ohio's legislature later replaced strict compliance with substantial compliance, R.C. 4511.19(D)(4)(b).
  • Ohio — State v. Stewart, Butler App. No. CA2000-07-148 (2001): the Walk-and-Turn should have been excluded: the officer never instructed the defendant how to stand during the instruction phase (itself a scored divided-attention clue), never told him which direction to turn after nine steps, and used no designated straight line.
  • Oregon — State v. Ingram, 238 Or. App. 720 (2010): the trooper, after reviewing his own patrol video, admitted he made the wrong number of passes, moved the stimulus at an improper speed, and held it higher than trained. Conviction REVERSED and remanded — foundation failure is an admissibility question, not mere weight.
  • Oregon — State v. Rodriguez-Delao, 335 Or. App. 700 (2024) (Lane County; DUII; BAC .12): the trooper admitted on cross, after watching bodycam, that he combined equal-tracking with lack-of-smooth-pursuit, missed or did not delineate one pass, and — though trained to ask about medical conditions first — never asked, calling it “a procedural mistake.” The Court of Appeals held the six-clue HGN was admitted without adequate foundation, the error was not harmless, and reversed and remanded. Outcome category: reversal — not a dismissal.

Oregon's foundation rule (State v. O'Key, 321 Or. 285 (1995)): qualified officer, proper administration, accurate recording; HGN may show influence but not a specific BAC. Read at the same size as the Texas line above: Oregon courts reversed convictions on deviations Texas courts would likely call weight-not-admissibility. Two related records, labeled for what they are: in Abyo v. State, 166 P.3d 55 (Alaska App. 2007), video forced the officer to concede her clue testimony was inaccurate — Abyo is not an acquittal or dismissal and is not cited as one; and in Upper Arlington v. Wissinger, 2014-Ohio-1601, a trial court need not credit officer clue testimony the video contradicts.

Sources: Stewart: cases.justia.com — Stewart (PDF); Ingram: case.law — Ingram (PDF); Rodriguez-Delao: cases.justia.com — Rodriguez-Delao (PDF). Read October 29, 2026.

THE DIVIDING LINE — WHAT EXISTS ONLY AS MARKETING, LABELED AND NEVER COUNTED

The conditions fact patterns — gravel, slopes, heels — exist in the research only as defense-firm self-reports: a Saratoga Springs, NY gravel-patch/heels case said to have ended in adjournment in contemplation of dismissal, and a Boone County, IL video case said to have ended in dismissal. Neither carries a case caption or citation. They are printed here as what they are — MARKETING, never counted — leads, not holdings. The NHTSA manual conditions themselves (a “reasonably dry, hard, level, non-slippery surface”; heels over 2 inches offered removal; documented difficulty for people over 65, with back, leg, or inner-ear problems, or 50+ lbs overweight) are documented at Door 4 from the manual; what is marketing is the claim that these fact patterns have thrown out cases. Defense-firm claims that substituting a non-standardized test (alphabet, finger count, Romberg) “throws out” Texas cases are likewise MARKETING: no Texas appellate case excluding a DWI solely on that ground was found; in Texas those are treated as observations going to probable cause and weight.

Door 4 · His Body vs. the Tests
The tests were validated on a general population. They do not measure an individual's own baseline.
LAW: DOCUMENTED · RECORD: NOT YET PROVEN

THE LAW — LAW LAYER: DOCUMENTED (FROM NHTSA'S OWN MATERIALS)

The standardized tests were validated on a general population. Conditions that affect the eyes, balance, legs, or ankles bear directly on what a test result can show about this person. A score against the general-population standard is not a measurement of this person's baseline. What follows is from NHTSA's own materials:

  • Who may have difficulty regardless of sobriety: people over 65, people with back, leg, or inner-ear problems, and people 50 or more pounds overweight may have difficulty with the Walk-and-Turn and the One-Leg Stand regardless of sobriety. The manual recommends officers skip the physical tests for such persons, or administer only HGN.
  • The surface the tests were designed for: both physical tests were designed for a dry, hard, level, non-slippery surface. Deviations from that surface affect the tests' evidentiary weight. (NHTSA Refresher Manual.)
  • Footwear: a subject wearing heels over 2 inches should be offered the opportunity to remove their shoes.
  • Questions officers are supposed to ask first: before administering the tests, officers are supposed to ask about physical disabilities, illness, injuries, diabetes, epilepsy, and medications.
  • The validation population: the original validation studies included fewer than 1.5% subjects over 65 and recorded no subject-weight data — so the standardized figures rest on a population in which older subjects were barely represented and weight was not measured at all.

Sources: NHTSA DWI Detection and Standardized Field Sobriety Testing manual; NHTSA SFST Refresher Manual; NHTSA validation-study materials.

  • He broke his leg in 5th grade.
  • Multiple ankle injuries since.
  • In his words: "clumsy all my life."

These are documented as his account. Turning them into proof requires records or an evaluation.

WHAT'S STILL NEEDED

  • Whether an eye evaluation would help is his lawyer's call. It is already on the binder question list for counsel.
TOLERANCE — WHAT THE RECORD SHOWS
What tolerance is, what recovers and what does not, what has actually been tested — and what is not claimed about this driver.
UNVERIFIED

WHAT TOLERANCE IS

The documented kinds:

  • Acute tolerance (within one session): at the same blood alcohol concentration, effects are stronger while the level is rising than while it is falling — the Mellanby effect.
  • Chronic tolerance (between sessions): after continued consumption, a higher dose is required to elicit the same effects.

Sources: the Mellanby effect, as documented in the alcohol literature; National Institute on Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism (NIAAA), definition of tolerance.

WHAT RECOVERS AND WHAT DOES NOT

The documented record, each item with its source:

  • A person's feeling of intoxication recovers on the falling limb faster than their function does.
  • In controlled comparisons at identical concentrations, visuomotor speed showed partial recovery, while executive function and short-term memory showed NO recovery. (Experimental and Clinical Psychopharmacology, 2010.)
  • Divided attention is the most sensitive measure of alcohol impairment in the reviewed literature; 60% of studies detected impairment at or below 0.05. (Moskowitz & Robinson, review of 158 studies for NIAAA.)
  • In simulator testing, sober drivers slowed down when their attention was divided; drivers under alcohol did not — the compensating caution itself was impaired. (PMC simulator study.)
  • A systematic review of the driving literature concludes that acute tolerance to motor-coordination impairment is insufficient to restore driving performance, and that alcohol-induced disinhibition persists on the descending limb. (Harrison & Fillmore.)

HELPS OR HINDERS — AS THE RECORD STATES IT

On some simple, single-task measures, tolerance is associated with better performance at the same concentration than a novice shows.

On the measures driving actually consists of — divided attention, executive function, inhibition, and self-assessment — the record shows tolerance does not restore performance, and the felt recovery is itself documented as a risk factor for the decision to drive.

WHAT HAS BEEN TESTED ON TELEVISION — DEMONSTRATION, NOT A STUDY

DEMONSTRATION — NOT A STUDY. Small samples, entertainment production, entered as demonstration only:

  • MythBusters, "Cell Phones vs. Drunk Driving" (2005) — both testers failed a road-safety course while talking on a phone and while intoxicated just below 0.08.
  • MythBusters, "Tipsy vs. Tired" (2010) — at just below 0.08, both testers made errors and left their lanes; after 30 hours awake, worse.
  • MythBusters, "Driving in Heels" (2012) — a driving course scored 70 under bladder strain and 25 under alcohol just below the limit.

HONEST NEGATIVE: no MythBusters head-to-head test comparing a tolerant drinker against a novice at the same concentration was found in the record checked for this door. If one is located, it enters by the same rule.

THE LAW NOTE

Texas defines intoxication two ways: a concentration of 0.08 or more, or not having the normal use of mental or physical faculties. Tolerance, whatever it shows, does not change a measured concentration or the first definition.

Source: Texas Penal Code § 49.01.

HIS OWN TOLERANCE

STATUS: UNVERIFIED — NOT TESTED, NOT DOCUMENTED. Nothing about this driver's individual tolerance is asserted anywhere on this page. If his history becomes relevant, it enters through counsel, with documents, by the same rule as every other fact here.

Door 5 · The HGN Foundation
Texas treats HGN as scientific evidence. A foundation must be laid before the opinion reaches a jury.
LAW: DOCUMENTED · RECORD: AWAITING INFORMATION

THE LAW — LAW LAYER: DOCUMENTED

Texas treats as scientific evidence. Under Emerson v. State, 880 S.W.2d 759, 768–769 (Tex. Crim. App. 1994), HGN testimony is admissible under Texas Rule of Evidence 702 only when the test is performed by an officer certified in its administration who applies the proper NHTSA technique. Foundation failure is the door to exclusion of the HGN opinion — without that foundation, the eye-test opinion can be kept from the jury entirely.

Source: Emerson v. State, 880 S.W.2d 759, 768–769 (Tex. Crim. App. 1994); Texas Rule of Evidence 702.

Non-alcohol causes of nystagmus, sourced. Nystagmus also arises from causes that are not alcohol, including: neurological conditions (multiple sclerosis, brain lesions, cerebellar disorders), medications (anti-seizure drugs, benzodiazepines), head injury, fatigue, and eye conditions. Roadside officers are not trained to distinguish pathological nystagmus from alcohol-induced nystagmus.

Source: National Center for Biotechnology Information (NCBI), clinical literature on nystagmus and horizontal gaze nystagmus.

THE TEXAS STRUCTURE — HOW THE FLAWS ARE TREATED IN A TEXAS COURTROOM

Researched October 29, 2026. All items in this layer are [court opinions], stated as the courts stated them. Door 3 carries the substantial-compliance summary; this layer prints the structure underneath it, without repeating that summary.

  • Emerson's ceiling. A qualified witness “may testify concerning a defendant's performance on the HGN test, but may not correlate the defendant's performance on the HGN test to a precise BAC.” The reason the court itself gave: NHTSA's lab work produced an average margin of error of .03 — too high to take judicial notice of precise-BAC reliability — and the State has more reliable means of quantification (blood, breath, urine). The court itself recorded the accuracy figures in the literature it judicially noticed: 77% most commonly, “as high as 88% under optimal conditions,” and ~80% for HGN + Walk-and-Turn combined. An officer therefore may not tie SFST performance to a precise BAC number in a Texas courtroom.
  • Plouff's rule — the physical tests are lay testimony. Walk-and-Turn and One-Leg Stand testimony is lay witness testimony under Rule 701, not expert testimony under Rule 702, because it rests on “common knowledge that excessive alcohol consumption can cause problems with coordination, balance, and mental agility.” Using the words “test,” “standardized clues,” or “divided attention” does not convert it into expert testimony (Plouff; McRae, for One-Leg Stand). The consequence, stated plainly: the Kelly scientific-reliability gate — valid theory, valid technique, proper application — does not apply at all to two of the three tests, even though their clue counts and decision points come from the same NHTSA validation programme as HGN.
  • Deviations go to weight, not admissibility. Under Plouff/Compton, “slight variations” in administration may affect the weight of the testimony; they do not render it inadmissible. Compton's own facts show how much variation counts as “slight”: the officer performed the smooth-pursuit portion in 11 seconds instead of 16 and the maximum-deviation portion in 19 instead of 32 — and the result was admitted, the court reasoning that the manual's times are approximations. The NHTSA manual's own warning (Door 3) therefore operates in Texas courtrooms mainly as impeachment text, not as an admissibility rule.
  • The rare hard line — McRae. McRae v. State, 152 S.W.3d 739 (Tex. App.—Houston [1st Dist.] 2004): exclusion is possible at the extreme. Where the officer admitted he did not administer all three parts of HGN, made only one pass per eye instead of two, did not comply with the manual, and conceded his own test “was not valid,” the trial court abused its discretion in admitting the HGN testimony. The practical threshold, printed at the same size: exclusion followed the officer's own admission of invalidity, not merely a defense showing of deviation.
  • The rare hard line — Smith. Smith v. State, 65 S.W.3d 332 (Tex. App.—Waco 2001), as summarised in later Texas opinions: an officer's testimony that 3 of 4 One-Leg Stand clues meant an “83 percent probability” of intoxication crossed from lay into expert testimony and gave the test an “imprimatur of scientific accuracy” without the Emerson/Kelly reliability proof. Texas courts thus police the percentage claim, while admitting the underlying clue observations as lay testimony.
  • What the structure means in practice (analysis assembled from the holdings above, not a holding itself): the State never has to re-prove HGN science (Emerson judicial notice) and never has to prove scientific validity at all for the physical tests (Plouff); administration defects are ordinarily cross-examination material about weight, short of a McRae-type admission; and the one hard ceiling is quantification — no witness may convert clues into a precise BAC number (Emerson), though the same information can reach the jury in qualitative form (“six clues,” “lost the normal use of his faculties”).

Sources: Emerson v. State, 880 S.W.2d 759 (Tex. Crim. App. 1994): law.justia.com — Emerson opinion; Compton v. State, 120 S.W.3d 375 (Tex. App.—Texarkana 2003): law.justia.com — Compton opinion; Plouff v. State, 192 S.W.3d 213 (Tex. App.—Houston [14th Dist.] 2006), McRae and Smith as cited in the text. Read October 29, 2026.

  • The video, to check the technique against the protocol (Door 7).
THE TWO RECORDS — CREDIBILITY, COUNTED
The arresting officer's documented record on truthfulness and the law, beside Tristan's own — counted the same way, at the same size.
AWAITING INFORMATION

THE LAW

The State must disclose evidence that impeaches its witnesses' credibility. Under Brady v. Maryland (1963), the prosecution must turn over evidence favorable to the defense; under Giglio v. United States (1972), that duty includes evidence that bears on a government witness's credibility. In Texas, the Michael Morton Act — Code of Criminal Procedure art. 39.14 — requires prosecutors to turn over exculpatory and impeachment material in their possession. An officer's documented dishonesty is impeachment material that these authorities may require the State to disclose.

Sources: Brady v. Maryland, 373 U.S. 83 (1963); Giglio v. United States, 405 U.S. 150 (1972); Texas Code of Criminal Procedure art. 39.14 (Michael Morton Act).

A related record addresses what each side may lawfully state during questioning:

THE POLYGRAPH RECORD — THE SCIENCE LAYER

No physiological response is unique to deception. The polygraph measures physiological responses — respiration, cardiovascular activity, and electrodermal response — none of which is uniquely produced by lying. That is the starting finding of the scientific record, stated before any accuracy figure.

The National Research Council's review, The Polygraph and Lie Detection (2003), concluded that for specific-incident testing, polygraph sorting performs "well above chance, though well below perfection"; and that for screening — testing without a specific incident — accuracy is "insufficient to justify reliance" on the results.

The American Psychological Association's position is to the same effect: there is little evidence that polygraph tests accurately detect lies.

Evidentiary status and real-world weight. Polygraph results are generally inadmissible in court. Their real-world weight runs through other channels — investigations, plea discussions, and supervision conditions — which is where a result can affect a person's life without ever reaching a jury. This page records the science and the status together, at the same size.

Sources: National Research Council, The Polygraph and Lie Detection (National Academies Press, 2003); American Psychological Association, statement on polygraph accuracy.

WHERE THIS DOOR APPLIES — UNRECORDED MOMENTS

This door applies where a disputed point was not captured by any camera or recording, and the evidence consists of the accounts of the people present. In that circumstance, each account is testimony, and the fact-finder weighs it. The documented credibility records of each speaker are among the materials relevant to that assessment. Where the officer's account and Tristan's account conflict about an unrecorded moment, the counts on this door record the documented findings for each speaker.

As Tristan's account supplies them, disputed points that no recording captured will be marked across this page — in every door, not just this one — with this label, styled the same as every other status label on the page: UNRECORDED — WORD AGAINST WORD — so every place where the evidence consists of conflicting accounts is identified.

THE JUDGE'S OWN RECORD — RULINGS IN THIS COURTROOM
How this judge has ruled, in this courtroom, on the same doors this case raises — counted as pattern, never inflated into precedent.
AWAITING INFORMATION

THE LAW — PATTERN, NOT PRECEDENT

A trial judge's past rulings are not binding precedent the way appellate law is. A judge's earlier ruling in another case does not bind this court the way a court of appeals' published opinion does. What past rulings are is the judge's own pattern. A prior ruling on similar facts may be cited to the court as part of an argument for consistency: "in this courtroom, on these facts, the ruling was X."

Trial rulings are subject to appellate review, so this door tracks not only what this judge ruled, but how those rulings fared on review — affirmed, or reversed, and on what issue. A pattern of rulings reversed on the same point is a documented fact about this courtroom, and so is a pattern of rulings affirmed.

Stated at full size: this door records pattern only. It does not present a prior ruling as binding precedent.

WHAT GETS COUNTED — LEGALLY OPEN SOURCES ONLY, EACH ENTRY SOURCED

Once the judge is identified, each class below is checked only through legally open sources, and each entry is printed with its source:

  • Rulings on the same doors this case raises — suppression of stops, SFST/HGN foundation, breath-test foundation, and Brady/Morton Act disputes — granted vs. denied, with the documented facts of each case, so like is compared with like.
  • Appellate outcomes of this judge's rulings — affirmed or reversed, and on what issue.
  • Sentencing pattern in like DUI cases — what this judge has imposed on comparable facts. This door feeds, and is fed by, Side B's comparison: the judge is one of the documented variables that can account for differences in outcomes between cases with similar facts.
  • The judge's public record — prior career (public biography), and any public actions by the State Commission on Judicial Conduct. Campaign filings are already the Money Layer's territory (Side B, Door 4) — cross-referenced there, not duplicated here.

HONEST LIMITS — PRINTED AT FULL SIZE

Misdemeanor courts generate few written opinions. Most rulings are oral, or survive only as docket entries. So this record is built from docket outcomes, hearing results, appellate opinions, and public filings — not from a library of reasoned opinions that does not exist.

Where the trail is thin, this door prints THIN RECORD rather than a pattern it cannot support. And a pattern assembled from oral rulings and docket outcomes is labeled as exactly that — an oral-ruling pattern, built from outcomes — never presented as if it were quoted from written opinions.

PUBLIC RECORD ONLY · ALLEGATIONS LABELED

THE FRAME

Researched October 29, 2026. Public records only: court records, judicial-conduct orders, licensing actions, official reports, reputable reporting. Allegations are labeled ALLEGATION; material the law seals is printed as DOOR CLOSED BY LAW; no motive is imputed anywhere. Where the same conduct produced two different outcomes, the documented cases stand next to each other and the record speaks.

JUDGES

  • Sharon Keller, Presiding Judge, Texas Court of Criminal Appeals. On September 25, 2007, with Michael Richard's execution-eve filing being prepared, Keller declined to keep the clerk's office open past closing — “We close at 5” — and Richard was executed that night. The State Commission on Judicial Conduct (SCJC) issued a public warning (July 16, 2010), finding “willful or persistent conduct that is clearly inconsistent with the proper performance of her duties” and that “casts public discredit on the judiciary.” A three-judge Special Court of Review vacated the warning (October 11, 2010; In re Keller, 357 S.W.3d 413) — not on the merits, but because after formal proceedings the Commission's only lawful options were dismissal, public censure, or recommending removal/retirement; a warning was not an authorized sanction. Authority: continued — Keller REMAINED presiding judge. In 2013 voters approved Proposition 9 (>84%), expanding SCJC sanctions — nicknamed in the press the “Keller amendment” — the later rule change, noted. Separately, the Texas Ethics Commission fined Keller $100,000 over omissions in her personal financial statements (Prison Legal News, secondary).
  • Judge David Glickler, Hays County Court at Law No. 2 — a court whose docket included DWI cases. Stopped May 26, 2015; the SCJC's own findings record that on dashcam he repeatedly identified himself — “I am a judge in this county” — after refusing a field sobriety test (the deputy replied: “You are a judge but you are also a citizen”), and was arrested for DWI. He pleaded no contest on May 4, 2016. SCJC sanction (June 16, 2016): public admonition + order of additional education. The order did not remove or suspend him — NO removal.
  • Judge Raymond Angelini, Bexar County district judge — ALLEGATION posture. Arrested on a DUI charge in March 2008 (police reported weaving; he refused a breath test and said he had “maybe three drinks” — AP). The DA asked that he be recused from all criminal cases; Administrative Judge Peeples ruled he could stay on the bench with a limited docket — no DUI cases, and no criminal cases involving the arresting officers. The criminal case's final disposition was not verified in this research and is printed UNVERIFIED; the charge is an allegation, and the docket ruling is the documented outcome.

Sources: abajournal.com — Keller warning tossed; deathpenaltyinfo.org — Keller; SCJC Glickler order: scjc.texas.gov — Glickler order (PDF); abajournal.com — Angelini limited docket. Read October 29, 2026.

PROSECUTORS

  • Ken Anderson — Williamson County District Attorney, later district judge. As DA he withheld exculpatory evidence in the murder prosecution of Michael Morton. A rare Court of Inquiry (2013) ordered Anderson's arrest, writing it could not “think of a more intentionally harmful act than a prosecutor's conscious choice to hide mitigating evidence.” Anderson invoked the statute of limitations against more serious charges. He resigned the judgeship (September 23, 2013) and on November 8, 2013 pleaded no contest to criminal contempt: a 10-day sentence — he served 5 — a $500 fine, and 500 hours of community service; evidence-tampering charges were dropped in exchange for surrender of his law license, with eligibility to apply for reinstatement after five years. Authority: ended — by negotiated plea, the first documented U.S. instance of a prosecutor jailed for a Brady-based wrongful conviction (Innocence Project).

The same case, both numbers, at the same size: Prosecutor Ken Anderson, for hiding the evidence — 10 days sentenced, 5 served. Michael Morton, the man the evidence would have freed — 8,989 days in custody before exoneration (Prison Legal News count).

  • Charles Sebesta — former Burleson County District Attorney. He withheld a co-defendant's confession that exculpated Anthony Graves, who spent 18 years in prison before exoneration. On June 11, 2015, the Board of Disciplinary Appeals disbarred Sebesta. Authority: ended.
  • Contrast (federal): in the Ted Stevens prosecution, the conviction was set aside and the indictment dismissed in 2009 after DOJ admitted Brady failures, and a special prosecutor was appointed to investigate contempt — the documented record contains no criminal conviction of any prosecutor in that matter. Outcome categories differ and are not blended: dismissal of the defendant's case ≠ sanction of the prosecutor.

Source: Sebesta: criminallegalnews.org — prosecutorial misconduct. Read October 29, 2026.

POLICE OFFICERS — THE WANDERING-OFFICERS RECORD

  • The research. Grunwald & Rappaport, The Wandering Officer, Yale Law Journal (2020), using two decades of Florida records (~98,000 officers): officers fired and rehired elsewhere are more likely to be fired again and to receive misconduct complaints than peers, and they migrate to smaller agencies with fewer resources.
  • The Texas numbers. A Texas House bill analysis (HB 4966, 88th Leg., 2023), citing the Texas 2036 / Benchmark Analytics 2022 Texas Law Enforcement Data Landscape report: between 2012 and 2022, at least 1,400 dishonorably discharged Texas peace officers were rehired at another Texas agency, and thousands more with documented performance or disciplinary problems were rehired. The same analysis records that TCOLE itself recognizes the F-5 separation-report system is “deeply flawed and nontransparent.” SB 24 (87th Leg.) had already tightened pre-employment checks, including review of prior agencies' personnel files.
  • How the record is built. Under Tex. Occ. Code §1701.452, an agency head must file an F-5 termination report rating each separation Honorable / General / Dishonorable (dishonorable = terminated, or resigned/retired in lieu of termination, over allegations of criminal misconduct, or for insubordination or untruthfulness). Only the dishonorable rating triggers the license-consequence process; a general discharge leaves the officer employable, and the officer may petition to correct the report (§1701.4525).
  • Brady/Giglio status. Prosecutors' offices maintain Brady/Giglio lists of officers with sustained credibility findings; as a matter of doctrine, placement does not by itself bar an officer from testifying — the prosecutor decides case by case (law-review discussion, secondary). No Texas-named Brady-list officer case was verified in this research, and none is invented here.

Sources: Grunwald & Rappaport: yalelawjournal.org — The Wandering Officer; HB 4966 analysis: app.legiplex.com — HB 4966; Texas 2036 / Benchmark report: texas2036.org — 2022 Data Landscape (PDF). Read October 29, 2026.

THE INSIDER RULES — QUOTED, BESIDE THE OUTSIDER RULES

  • Tex. Occ. Code §1701.502(a): TCOLE shall immediately revoke the license of an officer convicted of a felony.
  • §1701.502(b)–(c): TCOLE shall immediately suspend the license of an officer charged with a felony and placed on community supervision (including deferred adjudication), with reinstatement possible after release from supervision.
  • A misdemeanor conviction — including a first-offense Class B DWI — triggers NO mandatory revocation. Consequences, if any, sit with the employing agency and the F-5 rating it chooses to file. (§1701.501(a) allows revoke/suspend/probation/reprimand for chapter or rule violations; §1701.501(d): for constitutionally elected officers — sheriffs, constables — revocation only on a felony conviction or an offense directly involving the officer's duties.)
  • DOOR CLOSED BY LAW — §1701.454 / §1701.457: §1701.454 makes all F-5-submitted information confidential and exempt from disclosure under Gov't Code ch. 552 (narrow exceptions); §1701.455 allows subpoena only in a judicial proceeding; and §1701.457 provides that TCOLE is not authorized to review an agency's disciplinary action or subpoena agency disciplinary documents. The Benchmark researchers themselves could not obtain TCOLE separation data because of these bars.
  • Judges. Judicial discipline in Texas is not criminal process: the SCJC (Tex. Const. art. V, §1-a; Gov't Code ch. 33) imposes sanctions — admonitions, warnings, reprimands, censure, education orders — and removal or forced retirement only through recommendation and a review tribunal. Keller documents the system's pre-2013 structural limit: after formal proceedings, the Commission literally had no lawful middle sanction to give, so the one it gave was vacated. A judge's criminal case runs in ordinary court, but the seat is governed by the conduct process and the voters.
  • Lawyers and prosecutors. Discipline runs through the State Bar to the Board of Disciplinary Appeals, up to disbarment (Sebesta). Anderson documents the negotiated variant: license surrender as a plea term, with reapplication eligibility after five years.
  • The outsider column, same conduct categories. A non-insider's first Texas DWI is a Class B misdemeanor conviction that cannot be expunged, carries a 90-day to 1-year license suspension (plus 180 days administratively for refusal), possible interlock, and counts for life under Texas's lifetime lookback. A non-insider who hides exculpatory evidence in a murder case faces the tampering charges Anderson's plea dropped. The comparison printed here is confined to what the cited sources document, and this record draws no conclusion from it.

Sources: Tex. Occ. Code §§1701.452–1701.502: law.justia.com — Occupations Code ch. 1701; §1701.501: texas.public.law — §1701.501. Read October 29, 2026.

THE INCENTIVE LEDGER — WHO IS PAID WHILE A CASE STAYS OPEN
The fees, costs, and institutional benefits that exist while a case continues — each with its legal source and its recipient.
LAW: DOCUMENTED · AMOUNTS: PARTIALLY DOCUMENTED

THE FRAME

Every stage of a case carries fees, costs, and institutional benefits that exist only while the case continues. This door lists them, with the statute or schedule each comes from and the recipient each goes to. Where money flows is a documented fact.

WHAT THE LEDGER RECORDS

Each entry records the charge or benefit, its legal source, who receives it, and the amount where a schedule or his own receipts state one. Amounts are tied to a receipt or a schedule, never estimated.

  • Court costs and fines, and the funds Texas law allocates them to — printed in full, including allocations that fund victim services, trauma care, and road safety, because destinations are facts and the ledger prints all of them, not a selection.
  • Community-supervision (probation) monthly fees — the monthly supervision fee, its authorizing source, and the receiving supervision department.
  • Ignition-interlock installation, monthly service, and calibration fees — paid to the interlock provider. His own installation, calibration, and payment are documented in Door 9, The Record Since.
  • License reinstatement and administrative license revocation (ALR) fees — the fees stated in the applicable schedule and the agency that receives them.
  • Bond fees and their statutory split — the bond amount and fee as stated on the bond receipt, and the division the statute or schedule states.
  • Towing and impound charges — the charges as stated on the towing and storage receipts, and the company or facility that received them.
  • The class fee for the required victim-impact panel — the fee as stated by the program, and the program that receives it.
  • Program-level institutional benefits — documented at the program level and labeled as such: enforcement statistics, and the federal and state grant funding that is allocated with reference to them. These entries are recorded at the program level; they are not entries about any individual case or person.

For each entry, the ledger also records what, if anything, is documented as flowing to the person inside the case.

THE TEXAS STATUTORY ALLOCATIONS — WHERE THE MONEY GOES BY LAW

Court costs on a Texas DWI conviction are not one pot. Texas law allocates them, by statute, to named funds and purposes. The ledger prints all of the destinations the statutes name — including destinations that fund public goods — because destinations are facts:

  • Consolidated court cost (Local Government Code § 133.102) — allocated by percentage among funds including comprehensive rehabilitation, abused children's counseling, crime stoppers assistance, the breath alcohol testing account, the Bill Blackwood Law Enforcement Management Institute, law enforcement officers standards and education, law enforcement management statistics, and the correctional management institute, among the statute's named accounts.
  • Crime Victims' Compensation Fund (Code of Criminal Procedure art. 102.011 and related cost provisions; Crime Victims' Compensation Act) — funded in part by court costs assessed on conviction.
  • Emergency medical services, trauma facilities, and trauma care systems — funded in part through the Driver Responsibility Program's successor allocations and the fines and fees Transportation Code ch. 708 and Health and Safety Code ch. 780 direct to trauma care.
  • State highway and road-safety purposes — allocations Transportation Code and Government Code provisions direct to transportation and enforcement programs.
  • Ignition-interlock and supervision fees — paid to the interlock provider and the community supervision department under Code of Criminal Procedure arts. 42A and 102 provisions authorizing supervision fees and program costs.
  • ALR and license fees — reinstatement fees to the Department of Public Safety under Transportation Code §§ 524.051 and 724.046.

Amounts for any entry print only where a statute's schedule or his own receipts state one — the allocations above are the structure the statutes establish, recorded as structure.

Sources: Texas Local Government Code § 133.102; Texas Code of Criminal Procedure arts. 102.011, 42A; Texas Transportation Code §§ 524.051, 708, 724.046; Texas Health and Safety Code ch. 780.

THE RAIL — PRINTED AT FULL SIZE

An incentive is a documented structure, not a documented motive. This ledger shows who is paid while a case stays open and what, if anything, is documented as flowing to the person inside the case. It does not state or imply that any individual acted because of these amounts; no entry names a private individual as a recipient of anything beyond what a public schedule states.

THE MONEY EXHIBIT — THE DEFINITION, AT THE DOOR

EXTORTION: obtaining something of value by threat — threatening to harm a person, accuse them of a crime, or ruin them, so they pay to make it stop. In Texas it is prosecuted as theft by coercion (Penal Code § 31.03); federally, the Hobbs Act and the blackmail statute cover the same shape. Below, that definition is cross-referenced element by element against how the legal system runs. This is a definition with its break point printed — not a slogan.

ElementExtortionThe legal system, as documentedThe shape
The threat Pay, or I hurt you. Jail, your license, your record, your livelihood — real threats, communicated in advance, with the punishment range printed on the charge like a price list for refusing. Same shape.
The thing of value Money or property for the threatener. Fines, fees, court costs, forfeited property — and the guilty plea itself, the most valuable item of all, because it closes the case. Same shape.
Manufactured consent The victim “agrees” because refusing costs more. Plead and take the discount, or refuse and face the full range — the trial penalty — which is why the overwhelming majority of cases end in pleas. Same shape.
The one element that differs — printed at the same size Extortion is a crime because the threat is wrongful when a private person makes it. The identical threat, issued by the process, is authorized. The difference is the badge, not the shape.
The asymmetry A private extortion victim can call the police. A person inside the process has no outside to call — every appeal routes back into the same system. Asymmetric.

Sources: Texas Penal Code § 31.03 (theft by coercion); the system's elements are documented in the layers below — the stack (Part A of the research), the plea and bail findings (Part D), each with its own source.

The headline fine is a minority of the bill. Every line below is a documented line item, with its source.
