The total, printed exactly as the research prints it. Fines alone, maximum lawful: $4,000 + $6,000 = $10,000, plus $370 in chart costs.

Constructed range from the documented line items above: ≈ $9,700–$15,300 before attorney and insurance; ≈ $15,200–$32,800 with them (attorney $2,500–$10,000; insurance increase $3,000–$7,500; the court-imposed fine assumed at $1,000–$4,000 — the one discretionary input, flagged as such). This is constructed arithmetic from documented components, NOT a surveyed average — no surveyed average exists: no study or agency publication reporting the mean or median amount actually paid in Texas DWI cases was found.

Beside it, the official anchor: TxDOT estimates the total financial cost of a DWI can exceed $17,000 (as restated in a Texas DPS news release; components in TxDOT campaign material include towing, bail, attorney, court costs, license fees, probation, and insurance hikes). Published [law-firm] all-in estimates cluster at $10,000–$20,000 over several years.

Flagged, not repeated as current: many attorney sites still list “DPS surcharges $1,000/yr × 3 years ($2,000/yr if BAC > 0.15).” Those were Driver Responsibility Program surcharges, repealed September 1, 2019 and replaced by the one-time § 709.001 fine (see the Knowledge Trail below). Any total containing annual DPS surcharges for a post-2019 case is stale.

Why totals vary: county (filing/ODL fees, reimbursement fees, testing fees, and program prices are local); judge (fine within the cap, probation length, IID imposition and length); disposition (deferred adjudication avoids the $6,000 state fine and conviction suspensions but not court costs or probation fees); and indigence findings (the § 709.001 waiver; fee waivers under the Knowledge Trail law).

Sources: Texas DPS news release restating the TxDOT estimate: dps.texas.gov; OCA court-cost charts; statutes and schedules as cited per line. Researched October 29, 2026.

THE MONEY EXHIBIT — THE TIME DIMENSION, AND WHAT NONPAYMENT TRIGGERS

The money runs on three clocks at once: (i) the months-to-years clocks above — supervision 12–24 months, interlock typically 6–12 months, SR-22 for 2 years, insurance re-rating for 3–5 years in market summaries; (ii) the fine and costs, payable at sentencing unless the court orders a later date or installments after the ability-to-pay inquiry; and (iii) license consequences that persist until each separate DPS action is paid and cleared.

  • Capias pro fine / warrant: a court may not issue a capias pro fine for failure to satisfy a judgment without first holding a hearing on the ability to satisfy it. Issuance then requires failure to appear at that hearing, or findings that the defendant is not indigent and failed to make a good-faith effort, or is indigent but failed to make a good-faith effort via community service and could have done so without undue hardship. (SB 1913 framework; see the Knowledge Trail.)
  • License-renewal hold (OmniBase / Failure to Appear–Failure to Pay Program): under Transp. Code ch. 706, a participating court reports failure to appear or failure to pay to DPS, which denies renewal of the license until each reporting court clears it; reimbursement fee $10 per reported offense for post-January 1, 2020 reports (waivable for indigence). Note: some secondary summaries still quote $30 — that was the pre-2020 amount. Driving after expiry then risks a new Driving While License Invalid charge — the documented cascade mechanism.
  • Jail credit and community-service credit — a rate conflict, flagged as a conflict, not resolved: SB 1913 (2017) raised the floor from $50 to $100/day of confinement or labor and $100 per 8 hours of community service in the then-codification. The current Ch. 45A text (eff. January 1, 2025) states jail credit at not less than $150/day for fine-only misdemeanors, while community-service credit in secondary summaries remains $100 per 8 hours, and TexasLawHelp still tells the public “typically $100 for every 24 hours.” Record-safe statement: credit is at least $100, and $150/day for jail in the current fine-only statute; the article applicable to the case type must be verified before quoting one number.
  • Probation revocation for nonpayment: governed by Bearden (Knowledge Trail) plus Texas art. 42A.751's ability-to-pay consideration — the State must show the failure was willful or that alternatives were inadequate; nonpayment alone, if genuinely involuntary, is not a lawful basis to revoke and imprison.

Sources: SB 1913 bill analysis, 85R: capitol.texas.gov — SB 1913 analysis; Code Crim. Proc. ch. 45A text: tcss.legis.texas.gov — Ch. 45A; Transp. Code ch. 706 summaries and OCA chart.

THE MONEY EXHIBIT — THE PURPOSE TEST: WHAT THE CODE SAYS IT IS FOR

The Code's own objectives, quoted verbatim — Texas Penal Code § 1.02:

“The general purposes of this code are to establish a system of prohibitions, penalties, and correctional measures to deal with conduct that unjustifiably and inexcusably causes or threatens harm to those individual or public interests for which state protection is appropriate. To this end, the provisions of this code are intended, and shall be construed, to achieve the following objectives:

(1) to insure the public safety through: (A) the deterrent influence of the penalties hereinafter provided; (B) the rehabilitation of those convicted of violations of this code; and (C) such punishment as may be necessary to prevent likely recurrence of criminal behavior;

(2) by definition and grading of offenses to give fair warning of what is prohibited and of the consequences of violation;

(3) to prescribe penalties that are proportionate to the seriousness of offenses and that permit recognition of differences in rehabilitation possibilities among individual offenders;

(4) to safeguard conduct that is without guilt from condemnation as criminal;

(5) to guide and limit the exercise of official discretion in law enforcement to prevent arbitrary or oppressive treatment of persons suspected, accused, or convicted of offenses; and

(6) to define the scope of state interest in law enforcement against specific offenses and to systematize the exercise of state criminal jurisdiction.”

Source: Texas Penal Code § 1.02 — law.justia.com — § 1.02 (verified live, October 29, 2026). The test below is therefore: deterrence (1)(A), rehabilitation (1)(B), non-recurrence (1)(C), proportionality and individualization (3), and non-arbitrary treatment (5).

THE PURPOSE TEST — THE EVIDENCE AGAINST SEVERITY AND ADD-ON FEES

  • Certainty deters; severity largely does not. The National Institute of Justice's Five Things About Deterrence (summarizing Nagin and others): “Increasing the severity of punishment does little to deter crime”; certainty of being caught is a far more powerful deterrent than severity; there is no evidence for a “chastening” effect of harsher sanctions; prison may exacerbate recidivism. nij.ojp.gov — Five Things About Deterrence
  • Harsher sanctions and recidivism. Gendreau et al. meta-analysis (summarized by Public Safety Canada): harsher sanctions had no deterrent effect on recidivism and were associated with a ~3% increase; in the “intermediate sanctions” category — which expressly included fines — no relationship with recidivism was found.
  • Add-on sanctions fail deterrence theory on its own terms. Harris, Evans & Beckett, Monetary Sanctions as Misguided Policy (Criminology & Public Policy): U.S.-style supplementary monetary sanctions are imposed with neither swiftness nor certainty (amounts vary arbitrarily by county and judge), so on deterrence theory itself they are unlikely to deter; the authors find no convincing penological rationale (deterrence, rehabilitation, incapacitation, or proportionate retribution) for non-income-based add-on sanctions — expressly not extending the critique to European day fines.
  • The Wisconsin $200 surcharge natural experiment (Milwaukee misdemeanors, court records 2001–2019; summarized by the Fines & Fees Justice Center [ADVOCACY clearinghouse, reporting a peer-track working study]): a $200 increase in misdemeanor fees was associated with no change in overall recidivism but an increase in felony-level recidivism; only $0.28 on the dollar was collected by 5.5 years (46% of collections came from retained bail, not post-sentence payment); +13.6% probability of a civil judgment for unpaid amounts; estimated net social-welfare cost $1,548 per case.
  • Cumulative debt and rearrest. Study of people released from state prison 2009–10 (FFJC summary): median instant-offense debt $1,200, median lifetime debt $4,080; doubling lifetime legal financial obligations raised the hazard of rearrest 10%, reconviction 11%, parole revocation 5%; modeled rearrest probability 30% at $0 debt vs. 52% at $1,000 and 63% at $30,000.
  • Juveniles, Florida (Piquero, Baglivio & Wolff, propensity-matched): youth assessed fees reoffended at 19.4% vs. 15.7% for youth without fees within a year of completing placement; average fees assessed were higher for Black ($709.50) and Hispanic ($633.33) youth than white youth ($426.50).
  • Jailing for nonpayment costs more than it collects. Brennan Center, The Steep Costs of Criminal Justice Fees and Fines (2019) (a policy institute — labeled as such): 10 counties in TX/FL/NM plus statewide data; fees and fines “work against rehabilitation,” and when debt leads to incarceration or license suspension it becomes harder to find a job or housing or pay child support; jailing people unable to pay cost jurisdictions up to 115% of the amount collected and generated no revenue. Texas-specific, via congressional testimony on the same study: Travis County in 2017 spent ~$4.8M on in-court fee/fine compliance proceedings and ~$4.6M jailing people who failed to pay or were earning jail credit. brennancenter.org — The Steep Costs
  • Norway, fixed-penalty increases (null result). Elvik & Christensen, Journal of Safety Research (2007): substantial increases in fixed traffic penalties 1995–2004 produced no detectable effect on speeding compliance generally.
  • The accurate absence statement: no rigorous study was found in this research in which court fees/costs (as opposed to the fact of a ticket, or an interlock) reduced recidivism. That absence is itself the accurate statement; it is not converted here into “fees are proven to have zero effect in all settings” — the Wisconsin and cumulative-debt studies are associational / quasi-experimental, not randomized trials.

THE PURPOSE TEST — THE STRONGEST PRO-SIDE RESULTS, AT THE SAME SIZE

  • The ticket itself, not its size. CESifo working paper using speed-camera data on 1M+ cars (regression discontinuity + event study): receiving a speeding ticket drops the speeding rate by one-third and re-offense rates by 70%, persistently (2 years, no backsliding) — but “higher fines produce only a limited additional effect.” The authors interpret this as learning about enforcement — certainty and salience.
  • Income-based day fines, Finland. Design: units (days) for offense seriousness × a daily unit set as a fraction of the offender's daily disposable income — so the same offense can cost a wealthy Finn €116,000 (a 1999 speeding case) and a low-income offender a small sum, at the same relative burden. Best causal evaluation found (Kaila, Review of Economic Studies, 2026): at the threshold where fines become income-based, fines ~€200 larger reduced reoffending by 15% in the following 6 months, driven by high-income drivers facing the largest increases; the effect was sizable but short-lived. A safety-neutral fixed fine achieving the same deterrence would have had to raise the bottom income quartile's increase by ~300%. Limits documented in the comparative literature: in practice most day-fined defendants are low-income; default can still lead to imprisonment in every system surveyed; income/wealth assessment varies. No Texas day-fine pilot evaluation was found. restud.com — Kaila, income-based fines
  • Ignition interlocks — a paid condition, not a fine — work while imposed. CDC systematic review of 15 high-quality studies (Elder et al. 2011): rearrest rates fell a median 67% while interlocks were installed, vs. license suspension; effects fade after removal unless paired with treatment (Florida treatment-mandate variant: −32% recidivism post-removal). NHTSA/IIHS state-law studies: mandatory/all-offender interlock laws associated with 15% fewer alcohol-involved crash deaths (NHTSA) and a 26% reduction in fatal crashes involving impaired drivers (IIHS) — summarized by the ABA, which advocates interlock adoption [labeled]. It works by incapacitating the specific behavior — the car won't start — not by the deterrent weight of its price.
  • Certainty and salience deterrence (above): a ticket's occurrence deters strongly, and announced enforcement (e.g. DWI checkpoints, publicized patrols) is the NIJ-endorsed lever — this supports enforcement visibility, not larger dollar amounts.
  • Restitution is analytically separate: it compensates an identifiable victim; none of the anti-fee studies above, and not the DOJ 2023 letter (which expressly excludes restitution), treat victim restitution as a “fee.” The fines/fees evidence does not swallow restitution.
  • Ability-to-pay reforms, measured: after SB 1913, Texas OCA data cited by NCSL show people jailed for nonpayment falling from ~620,000 to ~450,000 in a year, and San Francisco's Financial Justice Project (city government program, self-reported — labeled) found ending license suspensions for failure to pay produced no revenue loss; revenue per citation rose. The counterweight, printed with it: reforms work fiscally in part because unpayable debt was largely uncollectible anyway (San Francisco's largest local fee, the monthly probation fee, had a 9% collection rate in 2016). Ability-to-pay scaling is thus best evidenced as revenue-neutral-to-positive and harm-reducing — not as a demonstrated recidivism intervention in adults; no adult recidivism RCT of waiver/scaling was found.

This page does not declare the verdict. The stated purpose (§ 1.02, above) and the measured results (both layers above) sit side by side, at the same size. The reader weighs them.

THE MONEY EXHIBIT — THE KNOWLEDGE TRAIL: WHAT WAS KNOWN, WHEN, AND WHAT CHANGED

This trail answers the question — why a known-flawed system continues — with documents, not motive: what was known, when it was known, and what changed. Incentives print as documented structure under this door's rail, never as imputed motive.

WhenWhat was officially known / saidWhat changed — and what did not
1983 — Bearden v. Georgia, 461 U.S. 660 Danny Bearden, laid off despite repeated job-seeking, with no income or assets, had probation revoked automatically for $550 unpaid of a $500 fine + $250 restitution. Held: a court cannot revoke probation for nonpayment absent evidence and findings that the probationer was responsible for the failure (willful refusal / failure to make sufficient bona fide efforts) or that alternative punishments were inadequate; “if a State determines a fine or restitution to be the appropriate and adequate penalty… it may not thereafter imprison a person solely because he lacked the resources to pay it”; imprisoning a probationer who made sufficient bona fide efforts, without considering alternatives, is “contrary to the fundamental fairness required by the Fourteenth Amendment.” supreme.justia.com — Bearden The constitutional rule has existed for 40+ years. The later record (Ferguson 2015; Texas 2016–17, below) documents routine non-inquiry into ability to pay anyway — the gap is enforcement and practice, not absence of law.
March 4, 2015 — DOJ Investigation of the Ferguson Police Department Findings (DOJ report, as summarized in the FFJC clearinghouse and a Reason Foundation policy brief): “Ferguson's law enforcement practices are shaped by the City's focus on revenue rather than by public safety needs”; fines and fees rose from $1.3M (12% of general revenue, 2010) to a budgeted $3.09M (23%, FY2015); 2010–2014 the city issued ~90,000 citations/summonses in a city of ~21,000; 2012–14, Black residents were 85% of stops, 90% of citations, 93% of arrests vs. 67% of population; a single missed, partial, or late payment could result in incarceration; warrants were used “almost exclusively” to coerce payment; the court refused to reduce or waive even after jailing for inability to pay. City officials evaluated police and court actors on revenue produced. Ferguson entered a federal consent decree (2016); its judge resigned within a week of the report. Nationally, the report is “widely viewed as the start of the movement to reform fines and fees” [FFJC characterization]. It also produced the 2016 DOJ Dear Colleague letter — rescinded December 2017 (see the 2023 row).
2016–2017 — Texas names it, then legislates: SB 1913 / HB 351 (effective September 1, 2017) Texas-specific documentation fed the reform: an ACLU of Texas report (as reported by AP) [ADVOCACY research] found, of 766 Texas municipal courts reviewed, a median community-service grant rate of 0.2%, and more than half waived nothing for people in poverty in the cases reviewed. On February 1, 2017, Chief Justice Hecht's State of the Judiciary address put it on the record of the Texas House Journal: in 2016 Texas courts handled ~7M fine-related cases; defendants were believed indigent and given waiver/community service in ~100,000 cases, but in 640,000 cases (~16%) defendants went to jail for minor offenses; the Judicial Council figure: 95% of arrest warrants issued were for fine-related offenses. Hecht: jailing people who cannot pay — “debtors' prisons” — “is not only illegal under the United States Constitution, [it] keeps them from jobs, hurts their families, makes them dependent on society and costs taxpayers money”; he catalogued fee-stacking (“there is even a fee for making a payment — pay to pay”) and license revocation that “just keeps him from going to work to earn enough to pay.” “The Texas Judicial Council has concluded that the system must be revamped.” SB 1913 then required: (1) citation and court notices must state alternatives to full payment if unable to pay; (2) a judge imposing a fine/costs on a plea in open court must inquire whether the defendant has sufficient resources/income to immediately pay; (3) if not, the judge must choose later/installment payment, community service, waiver in full or part, or a combination; (4) no capias pro fine without a prior ability-to-satisfy hearing; (5) jail/community-service credit floor raised $50 → $100; (6) no monetary bail in a fine-only offense unless the defendant failed to appear and the judge finds ability to post it. Measured: ~170,000 fewer people incarcerated for nonpayment in a year (NCSL/OCA: ~620,000 → ~450,000) and 300,000 fewer Class C warrants [legislator statement citing OCA — labeled as such]. Not changed: the fines and fees themselves mostly remained; later OCA-based research found indigency waivers in under 1% of county-level misdemeanor cases and jail credit satisfying ~8.8% of cases on average (47.2% in one county) — the hearing/waiver machinery was enacted faster than it was used. A 2020 legislative staff presentation [Texas Appleseed / TFDP — ADVOCACY, citing OCA FY2019 data] made the same point in raw counts: 71,308 cases waived for indigency vs. 508,698 cases satisfied by jail credit — jail-credit cases in the hundreds of thousands — and estimated ~$63M in taxpayer cost of jailing people for Class C debt in 2018. In municipal cases (OCA 2019), fines/costs were satisfied at least in part by community service in only 16% of cases and waived in only 8%.
2019 — HB 2048 repeals the Driver Responsibility Program (effective September 1, 2019) The DRP (2003–2019) layered annual surcharges for 3 years on top of the criminal penalty: $100/yr at 6+ points (+$25/point), $250/yr for no license/insurance, $1,000/yr for DWI (up to $2,000/yr if BAC ≥ 0.15 in some summaries); nonpayment → suspension → new DRP-eligible offenses. Documented reasons for repeal, from the Legislature itself: the Senate's own news service — the DRP put drivers in “a cycle of escalating fines,” and suspension for nonpayment created “a never-ending cycle of charges”; it survived 15+ years only because it funded the trauma fund (Fund 5111, ~130,000 patients/yr). As of January 2018, 1.4 million Texans had licenses suspended for DRP nonpayment [FFJC, citing DPS data]; the Texas Tribune: the program “left more than 1 million people unable to keep or renew their licenses,” with surcharges that “grew to thousands of dollars on years-old tickets.” Passed unanimously in both chambers; signed by Gov. Abbott. All outstanding DRP surcharges were waived at repeal (>$2B in surcharges relieved, per later congressional testimony by Texas advocates [ADVOCACY testimony]); DPS figures at enactment: ~630,000 people immediately eligible for reinstatement, ~350,000 more after a reinstatement fee, ~400,000 after resolving other holds; ~1.5M potentially eligible overall. Replacement revenue — the part often omitted: +$20 on state traffic fines ($30 → $50), +$2/yr auto insurance fee, and the new one-time DWI fines of $3,000/$4,500/$6,000 (§ 709.001, in the Stack above) — with the DWI fine waivable for indigence. The money was restructured into new fines, not abolished: a 3-year recurring surcharge became a single, larger, conviction-triggered fine.
April 20, 2023 — DOJ Dear Colleague Letter on fines & fees The letter revived and expanded the guidance, stating 7 constitutional principles — including Eighth Amendment proportionality; the Fourteenth Amendment bar on incarceration for nonpayment without an ability-to-pay determination and a finding of willfulness; the duty to consider alternatives; the bar on conflicts of interest in fee-funded courts; no conditioning court access on prepayment by those unable to pay; notice/counsel protections; and no intentional discrimination. The letter states unjust fines/fees “may erode trust…, increase recidivism, undermine rehabilitation and successful reentry, and generate little or no net revenue.” It expressly updates and supersedes the March 2016 letter, which had been rescinded in December 2017 by AG Sessions. justice.gov — 2023 Dear Colleague Letter (PDF) Current status: NOT CONFIRMED. No formal rescission document for the 2023 letter was found in this research (searches run October 29, 2026). However, the DOJ page as crawled October 7, 2026 now carries the header “This is archived content from the U.S. Department of Justice website. The information here may be outdated,” and DOJ's Associate AG archive lists it among 2021–2025 archive publications. Neither “in force” nor “rescinded” is stated as fact on this page. The underlying Bearden constitutional rule does not depend on the letter's status.

Beyond 2017, the Texas Judicial Council / OCA record continues: OCA is the source of the operative court-cost charts (the Stack above), the Collection Improvement Program reporting, and the data behind every post-reform evaluation in the Purpose Test — the data series is what allows the “enacted vs. used” gap to be documented at all. No later TJC report reversing the 2017 conclusions was found. State Justice Institute summary of Texas's post-2017 position, quoting State Court Administrator David Slayton: courts are “collecting something rather than nothing, and the negative impacts for defendants are much lower… While we have more progress to make…”

Sources: Bearden v. Georgia; DOJ Ferguson investigation findings via finesandfeesjusticecenter.org — Ferguson; Hecht address, Texas House Journal (Feb. 1, 2017): lrl.texas.gov (PDF); SB 1913 bill analysis; NCSL summary of OCA data: ncsl.org — Assessing Fines and Fees; Slavinski & Pettit (RSF, OCA 2019 data); Texas Senate News, DRP repeal: senate.texas.gov; DOJ 2023 letter.

THE KNOWLEDGE QUESTION — HIS QUESTION, IN HIS WORDS

“The way I see it. If they know their system is broken and flawed, yet forcing it in people..that in itself is illegal correct?”

Documented answer: NO — not standing alone. Researched October 29, 2026. No statute found makes “knowingly operating a flawed system” an offense by itself. There is no crime or civil claim whose only elements are (1) the system is broken and (2) they knew. The whole pattern is not a named crime. The specific named pieces of it are — each with its own elements, printed below. Knowledge becomes illegal only inside specific named doctrines — and in one major doctrine, printed plainly below, knowledge is not an element at all. This layer sits beside the Knowledge Trail above because the Trail is the evidence form of the question: dated, sourced knowledge is the only form of “they knew” the law can grip.

THE KNOWLEDGE QUESTION — DELIBERATE INDIFFERENCE: WHERE KNOWLEDGE IS THE WHOLE TEST

  • Estelle v. Gamble, 429 U.S. 97 (1976): deliberate indifference to a convicted prisoner's serious medical needs violates the Eighth Amendment as the “unnecessary and wanton infliction of pain.” The same opinion draws the floor: mere negligence or medical malpractice is not a constitutional violation. A bad system that produces negligent care is, without more, not unconstitutional.
  • Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825 (1994): the test is subjective. An official is liable only if he “knows of and disregards an excessive risk to inmate health or safety”: he must (1) be aware of facts from which an inference could be drawn that a substantial risk of serious harm exists, and (2) actually draw the inference. “An official's failure to alleviate a significant risk that he should have perceived but did not… cannot be condemned as the infliction of punishment.” Should-have-known and negligence are NOT violations. Knowledge may be proved circumstantially — a factfinder may infer an official knew of a substantial risk from its obviousness — but “should have known” alone loses.
  • Pretrial detainees (Fourteenth Amendment) — stated carefully: Kingsley v. Hendrickson, 576 U.S. 389 (2015), adopted an objective reasonableness standard — but only for pretrial detainees' excessive-force claims. The circuits then split on medical-care, conditions, and failure-to-protect claims: the Second (Darnell v. Pineiro), Fourth, Sixth, Seventh, and Ninth Circuits apply an objective standard to at least some detainee claims; the Fifth, Eighth, and Tenth retain the subjective standard. Texas is in the Fifth Circuit, so the subjective standard governs here. Under Hare v. City of Corinth, 74 F.3d 633 (5th Cir. 1996) (en banc): for an episodic act or omission by an individual officer, a pretrial detainee must prove subjective deliberate indifference — actual knowledge of a substantial risk and disregard of it; “knew or should have known” is the wrong test, and even gross negligence is insufficient. For a conditions-of-confinement claim (no individual act interposed), the test is whether the condition is reasonably related to a legitimate governmental objective (Bell v. Wolfish); the Fifth Circuit has called the two tests functionally equivalent (Duvall v. Dallas County, 631 F.3d 203 (5th Cir. 2011)).

Sources: Estelle v. Gamble; Farmer v. Brennan — supreme.justia.com — Farmer v. Brennan; Kingsley v. Hendrickson; circuit split summarized at fisheldowney.com — circuit split; Hare v. City of Corinth; Duvall v. Dallas County. Read October 29, 2026.

THE KNOWLEDGE QUESTION — SYSTEM LIABILITY: THE POLICY MUST BE THE MOVING FORCE

Monell / City of Canton v. Harris, 489 U.S. 378 (1989): a county is liable under §1983 only where an official policy or custom is the “moving force” behind a constitutional violation — there is no vicarious liability for employees' acts. A failure to train counts as such a policy only where it “amounts to deliberate indifference” to the rights of the people employees encounter — i.e., a deliberate or conscious choice by the policymaker. A single incident ordinarily cannot prove it (Connick v. Thompson). This is the closest doctrine to the question at the head of this layer — “they knew and kept doing it” — and its elements show why it usually fails: documented knowledge must be tied to a specific policy choice and to a specific constitutional injury it caused.

Sources: Monell v. Department of Social Services; City of Canton v. Harris, 489 U.S. 378 (1989). Read October 29, 2026.

THE KNOWLEDGE QUESTION — THE CRIMINAL PIECES: WILLFULNESS, AND KNOWLEDGE WRITTEN INTO THE TEXT

  • 18 U.S.C. §242 — deprivation of rights under color of law. Elements: a person acting under color of law willfully subjects another to the deprivation of rights secured by the Constitution or federal law. In Screws v. United States, 325 U.S. 91 (1945), the Supreme Court read “willfully” to require specific intent to deprive a person of a federal right made definite by the Constitution's terms or by decisions interpreting them. The DOJ's own line: “Mistake, fear, misperception, or even poor judgment does not constitute willful conduct prosecutable under the statute.” Penalties escalate with harm: base offense up to 1 year; bodily injury or a weapon, up to 10 years; death, up to life or the death penalty. Prosecutions are relatively rare — multiple sources attribute this directly to the willfulness bar: the government must prove the official knew the conduct was unconstitutional and did it anyway.
  • Texas Penal Code §39.03 — official oppression (the state-law analogue, with knowledge in the text). A public servant acting under color of office commits an offense if he “(1) intentionally subjects another to mistreatment or to arrest, detention, search, seizure, dispossession, assessment, or lien that he knows is unlawful; (2) intentionally denies or impedes another in the exercise or enjoyment of any right, privilege, power, or immunity, knowing his conduct is unlawful; or (3) intentionally subjects another to sexual harassment.” Class A misdemeanor. Note what the elements require: an intentional act against a person, plus knowledge that that act is unlawful. It attaches to acts against persons, not to running a deficient system — running a generally deficient jail is not an element of this offense; knowingly subjecting a person to an unlawful detention or mistreatment is.
  • Ordinary crimes attach to specific acts, not to systems. The Tarrant record in the county-comparison door is the worked example: jailers were prosecuted for murder (indicted) and for tampering with / falsifying government records (convicted) — specific knowing acts by named people — while no one was charged with an offense called “operating a flawed jail,” because no such offense exists.

Sources: 18 U.S.C. §242; Screws v. United States, 325 U.S. 91 (1945) — text at law.cornell.edu — 18 U.S.C. §242; Texas Penal Code §39.03 — texas.public.law — §39.03. Read October 29, 2026.

THE KNOWLEDGE QUESTION — PATTERN-OR-PRACTICE: WHERE DOCUMENTED KNOWLEDGE PRODUCES INSTITUTIONAL REMEDIES, NOT CRIMES

  • 34 U.S.C. §12601: it is unlawful for a law-enforcement agency to engage in a pattern or practice of conduct that deprives persons of constitutional or federal rights, and the Attorney General may sue in federal court for equitable relief to eliminate it. The remedy is a reform decree, not a conviction: knowledge and persistence matter because they are what make a “pattern or practice” provable, but the output is court supervision, not punishment of the knowledge itself.
  • Worked example — Ferguson, Missouri: DOJ opened its investigation in September 2014; its March 4, 2015 findings concluded the Ferguson Police Department and municipal court engaged in a pattern or practice violating the First, Fourth, and Fourteenth Amendments, driven by revenue-focused enforcement and racial discrimination (the fines-and-fees findings are in the Knowledge Trail above). The parties jointly filed a proposed consent decree on March 17, 2016, and the court entered it on April 19, 2016.
  • Jails specifically — CRIPA, 42 U.S.C. §1997 et seq.: for institutions (including county jails), DOJ's vehicle is the Civil Rights of Institutionalized Persons Act. The Texas worked example — Harris County Jail: DOJ notified the county of its investigation on March 7, 2008, and its findings letter of June 4, 2009 concluded the jail failed to provide detainees adequate (1) medical care, (2) mental-health care, (3) protection from serious physical harm, and (4) protection from life-safety hazards — and expressed DOJ's preference for working cooperatively to fix them. That is the documented shape of federal “knowledge” enforcement against a Texas county jail: findings, recommended remedies, negotiation — not criminal charges against the people who knew.

Searched-for negative: no DOJ CRIPA findings letter and no §12601 consent decree for the Tarrant County Jail was found in this research. Its 2024 federal review was an invited technical-assistance visit by the National Institute of Corrections — a different instrument, producing recommendations, not civil-rights findings. Tarrant's state-level analogue of “the system on notice” is TCJS's notice-of-noncompliance / remedial-order process (see the county-comparison door) — administrative enforcement, again not a crime.

Sources: 34 U.S.C. §12601; DOJ, Ferguson — justice.gov — Ferguson consent decree; DOJ CRIPA findings letter on Harris County Jail (June 4, 2009) — criminallegalnews.org — DOJ letter re Harris County Jail; clearinghouse.net — Harris County Jail CRIPA case. Read October 29, 2026.

THE KNOWLEDGE QUESTION — KNOWING FALSE PROCESS: WHERE “THEY KNEW” LOWERS THE BAR, AND WHERE IT IS NOT AN ELEMENT AT ALL

  • Napue v. Illinois, 360 U.S. 264 (1959) (line from Mooney v. Holohan, 294 U.S. 103 (1935)): due process is violated when the prosecution knowingly uses false testimony, or — even without soliciting it — knowingly allows it to go uncorrected, including testimony that goes only to a witness's credibility. Knowledge anywhere in the prosecution team is attributed to the government (Giglio). Remedy: a new trial if there is “any reasonable likelihood” the false testimony could have affected the judgment — a deliberately lower materiality bar than Brady's, and it is lower BECAUSE they knew.
  • Brady v. Maryland, 373 U.S. 83 (1963) — printed plainly: KNOWLEDGE IS NOT AN ELEMENT. Elements: (1) evidence favorable to the accused (exculpatory or impeachment — Giglio v. United States, 405 U.S. 150); (2) suppressed by the State, willfully or inadvertently; (3) prejudice / materiality — a reasonable probability of a different result, assessed cumulatively, sufficient to undermine confidence in the outcome (Bagley; Kyles). The violation exists “irrespective of the good faith or bad faith of the prosecution.” Remedy: vacatur and a new trial. This is the doctrine that freed Brown, Toney, Hawkins, and Nolley (Ties Check, Door 1 of Side B) — in each, people were freed not because someone proved officials knew, but because favorable evidence was withheld and material.
  • Malicious prosecution — Thompson v. Clark, 596 U.S. 36 (2022): a Fourth Amendment §1983 claim lies for prosecution without probable cause. Thompson holds the favorable-termination element requires only that the prosecution ended without a conviction — no “affirmative indication of innocence” is needed. The remaining elements track the 1871 tort: initiation without probable cause and malice. The Court itself noted the claim remains bounded by the probable-cause requirement and by qualified immunity (below). Remedy: civil damages — a suit, not a prosecution.
  • Fabrication of evidence (Fifth Circuit): Cole v. Carson, 802 F.3d 752 (5th Cir. 2015) (reinstated in relevant part, 905 F.3d 334 (2018); en banc, 935 F.3d 444 (2019)) recognizes a due-process right “not to have police deliberately fabricate evidence and use it to frame and bring false charges against a person.” As later cases summarize the elements: (1) the evidence was fabricated, (2) for the purpose of obtaining a charge, and (3) the fabricated evidence influenced the decision to charge. Morgan v. Chapman, 969 F.3d 238 (5th Cir. 2020), holds fabrication used to establish probable cause violates the Fourth Amendment. Fabrication is again a deliberateness doctrine: negligent or mistaken evidence is not fabrication.

Sources: Napue v. Illinois; Brady v. Maryland; Thompson v. Clark, 596 U.S. 36 (2022) — supreme.justia.com — Thompson v. Clark; Cole v. Carson; Morgan v. Chapman. Read October 29, 2026.

THE KNOWLEDGE QUESTION — THE SHIELD: QUALIFIED IMMUNITY, AND ITS PRINTED LIMITS

  • Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800, 818 (1982): government officials performing discretionary functions “generally are shielded from liability for civil damages insofar as their conduct does not violate clearly established statutory or constitutional rights of which a reasonable person would have known.”
  • Anderson v. Creighton, 483 U.S. 635, 640 (1987): the right must be clearly established “in a more particularized… sense: the contours of the right must be sufficiently clear that a reasonable official would understand that what he is doing violates that right.” The precise action need not previously have been held unlawful, but “in the light of pre-existing law the unlawfulness must be apparent.”
  • Why this blunts documented knowledge: even where a plaintiff proves an official actually knew of a risk (Farmer) and disregarded it, an individual-damages suit still fails if no prior decision had particularized the right on materially similar facts — the court asks not “did he know people were being harmed?” but “did existing case law tell a reasonable official this specific conduct was unlawful?”

The shield's printed limits: qualified immunity does not apply to criminal cases (§242, §39.03), does not apply to municipalities (Monell defendants have no qualified immunity), and does not bar injunctions or declaratory relief — it blunts personal civil-damages liability only. That allocation is itself part of the answer to the question at the head of this layer: documented knowledge usually yields institutional remedies and case-specific relief rather than personal liability — which is why the Knowledge Trail above, and the findings in the county-comparison door, matter in the form they take.

Sources: Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800 (1982); Anderson v. Creighton, 483 U.S. 635 (1987). Read October 29, 2026.

THE KNOWLEDGE QUESTION — THE BOTTOM LINE, IN THE RECORD'S FORM

PropositionStatus in law
“They knew the system was broken and kept using it” is, by itself, a crimeNo such offense found — not in federal law, not in Texas law
Knowledge + disregard of a specific substantial risk to a person in custodyDeliberate indifference — constitutional violation (Estelle/Farmer; subjective in the Fifth Circuit per Hare)
A policymaker's conscious choice (e.g., failure to train) that causes a constitutional violationMonell/Canton liability for the county — civil, institutional
Willful (specifically intended) deprivation of a definite constitutional right under color of lawCrime — 18 U.S.C. §242 (Screws willfulness); prosecutions rare because of that bar
Intentionally subjecting a person to a detention/mistreatment known to be unlawfulCrime — Tex. Penal Code §39.03 (Class A misdemeanor)
Knowingly using, or knowingly not correcting, false testimonyDue-process violation (Napue) — new trial on the lowered “any reasonable likelihood” materiality standard
Suppressing favorable material evidence, knowingly or notDue-process violation (Brady) — knowledge expressly irrelevant
Prosecution without probable cause, ending without convictionCivil claim (Thompson v. Clark) — damages, bounded by probable cause and qualified immunity
Deliberately fabricating evidence to obtain a chargeDue-process / Fourth Amendment violation (Cole; Morgan) — civil claim
A documented pattern or practice of violationsFederal institutional enforcement — §12601 (police) / CRIPA (jails): findings letters and consent decrees, as in Ferguson (2015–16) and Harris County Jail (2009)
Documented knowledge, but no prior case particularizing the rightQualified immunity shields the individual from civil damages (Harlow/Anderson)

The pattern the question describes — officials who know and persist — is real in this record (the Ties Check and the county-comparison door document findings, deaths, withheld evidence, and falsified records). The law's answer is that the pattern is not itself a named crime; the specific named pieces of it are, and each piece has elements that decide, case by case, exactly where “they knew” crosses into illegality and where it stops at negligence, administrative noncompliance, or institutional remedy.

THE KNOWLEDGE QUESTION — THE MIRROR, CLOSING THE LAYER

The knowledge element cuts both ways — printed at the same size. Crystal Mason's conviction failed because the State could not prove she knew she was ineligible to vote: the Second Court of Appeals acquitted her on March 28, 2024 because no evidence showed she knew (Ties Check, Door 1 of Side B). The same element that decides whether an official's knowledge becomes deliberate indifference, willfulness, or official oppression also decided whether a citizen's act was a crime at all. Knowledge is the load-bearing element on both sides of the counter — for the person inside the process, and for the people running it.

THE SEEING QUESTION — HIS LINE, IN HIS WORDS

“Just because something has been accepted does not make it acceptable.”

The twin of the Knowledge Question, stated plainly. Researched October 29, 2026. The Knowledge Question above documents what the law does when officials know a practice is flawed. This layer documents the other state: a practice the system itself does not see as wrong. It documents practices, language, and doctrine only. What any living official sees, believes, or intends is CAN'T VERIFY — printed here at the door, and it governs every layer below. No motive is stated or implied for any person.

THE SEEING QUESTION — THE MECHANISM: NORMALIZATION OF DEVIANCE

The documented mechanism. Diane Vaughan, The Challenger Launch Decision: Risky Technology, Culture, and Deviance at NASA (University of Chicago Press, 1996) [scholarship], documented a process in NASA's own records: a deviation from expected performance appears; it is processed through the organization's own formal review; it is redefined as an acceptable risk; that reclassification becomes the baseline; and the next deviation is measured against the new baseline, not the original specification — step by step, until the deviant state is the operating normal. In the documented summary of her account: “damage to the crucial O-rings had been observed after previous shuttle launches. Each observed instance of damage… was followed by a sequence ‘in which the technical deviation of the [O-rings] from performance predictions was redefined as an acceptable risk.’ Repeated over time, this behavior became routinized.” No rule needs to be broken at any step; the standard itself is what moves. Her thesis is expressly not a misconduct story: “No extraordinary actions by individuals explain what happened: no intentional managerial wrongdoing, no rule violations, no conspiracy.”

The concept adopted by an official investigation. The Columbia Accident Investigation Board, Report Vol. I (August 2003) [primary document / official report], applied Vaughan's concept in its own words — Vaughan served as the Board's investigation consultant and research analyst, not as one of its 13 members: “The acceptance of events that are not supposed to happen has been described by sociologist Diane Vaughan as the ‘normalization of deviance.’ The history of foam problem decisions shows how NASA first began and then continued flying with foam losses, so that flying with these deviations from design specifications was viewed as normal and acceptable.” Finding F6.1-2: “Foam shedding, which had initially raised serious safety concerns, evolved into ‘in-family’ or ‘no safety-of-flight’ events or were deemed an ‘accepted risk.’” The Board's Chapter 8 records the same pattern for both losses: “evidence that the design was not performing as expected was reinterpreted as acceptable and nondeviant, which diminished perceptions of risk throughout the agency.”

The critique, at the same size. The safety-science literature raises a documented caution against over-reading the concept [scholarship — Dekker (2004) and Hollnagel (2009), as retrieved in this research via a secondary digest]: procedural deviations precede safe outcomes at least as often as accidents; deviation can be how work actually succeeds under real conditions (“drift to success”); and post-disaster investigation carries a built-in hindsight bias — after a catastrophe, investigators “unearth deviations from procedure and decide that they are causally related to the accident, though in practice they may have been commonplace and did not usually lead to bad outcomes.” No peer-reviewed, dedicated refutation of the concept itself was found in this research; the documented critique is this drift-and-hindsight caution, and it is printed here at the same size as the concept it qualifies.

Precision note, at the same size. The one-sentence “Vaughan definition” most often attributed to her 1996 book could not be page-verified against the book itself in this research, and one scholarly source traces that wording to a 2008 interview rather than to the book. It is therefore not printed on this page as a verified quotation. The mechanism above is stated in the documented terms the sources above support.

Sources: Vaughan, The Challenger Launch Decision (1996), and “The Dark Side of Organizations,” Annual Review of Sociology 25 (1999); Columbia Accident Investigation Board, Report Vol. I (2003), Ch. 6, Finding F6.1-2, and Ch. 8. Read October 29, 2026.

THE SEEING QUESTION — THE LANGUAGE TRAIL: THE FIELD-TEST MANUAL'S OWN WORDS

The method of this layer: an institution's own words about a practice, quoted, with the documented change shown as before-and-after text. No word is paraphrased into a motive, and no intention behind a wording change is asserted.

  • The 2000 warning — categorical. [Court opinions quoting the manual — State v. Homan, 89 Ohio St.3d 421, ¶11 (2000), citing NHTSA, HS 178 R2/00, Student Manual (2000), at VIII-3; United States v. Horn, 185 F. Supp. 2d 530, at 537 (D. Md. 2002), quoting the same passage at VIII-12 — the two courts' page citations differ and are preserved unsmoothed.] “If any one of the standardized field sobriety test elements is changed, the validity is compromised.”
  • The current warning — conditional. [Primary document — NHTSA/IACP Participant Manual, 2023 curriculum, edited 4/2025, Session 8, Pg. 12; a whole-document search of that manual returns exactly one occurrence of “compromised” — this sentence.] “If any one of the SFST elements is changed, the validity may be compromised.”
  • The documented range — not a year. “is” (2000, court-quoted) → “may” (in place by the 10/2015 edition, primary text) → “may” (2023 curriculum, edited 4/2025, primary text). The exact edition in which “is” became “may” could not be pinned in this research. Claims that the change happened in any specific year rest on defense-advocacy pages only and are not printed here as fact.
  • The institution recharacterizing its own old sentence (2006). [Primary document — NHTSA Instructor Manual, HS 178 R2/06, Session III, pp. III-13–III-14.] Six years after Homan, NHTSA's instructor curriculum recounts the case and the sentence, then re-describes the old wording's scope: “This decision was based upon an older edition of this manual and was a strict interpretation by the court. The phrase in question only applied to the use of SFSTs for training purposes.” The instructor note beside it states: “FOR TRAINING PURPOSES, THE SFSTs ARE NOT AT ALL FLEXIBLE. THEY MUST BE ADMINISTERED EACH TIME, EXACTLY AS OUTLINED IN THE COURSE.” The two texts sit side by side; the distance between them is the exhibit, stated as text only.

Sources: State v. Homan; United States v. Horn; NHTSA/IACP Participant Manual (2023 curriculum, edited 4/2025): SFST Participant Manual (PDF); NHTSA Instructor Manual, HS 178 R2/06. Read October 29, 2026.

THE SEEING QUESTION — THE LANGUAGE TRAIL: TEXAS'S OWN WORDS FOR THE MONEY MACHINERY, BOTH WAYS

One vocabulary — the machinery's administrative words, quoted:

  • “Recoupment” / “reimbursement.” [Court opinion — Weir v. State, 278 S.W.3d 364, 366–367 (Tex. Crim. App. 2009).] Court costs are “‘a nonpunitive recoupment of the costs of judicial resources expended in connection with the trial of the case’”; they are “a form of reimbursement to the State of Texas for the judicial costs incurred in disposing of the case.” A fine is punitive; the cost stack is filed, in the system's own taxonomy, as reimbursement.
  • “Monthly Reimbursement Fee.” [Primary document — statute, Tex. Code Crim. Proc. art. 42A.652.] The charge for being supervised on community supervision is titled a reimbursement fee, “$25 to $60… each month,” waivable on a finding of “significant financial hardship.”
  • “Participant fees.” [Official report — Legislative Budget Board, Criminal and Juvenile Justice Uniform Cost Report.] The defendant-paid half of supervision is booked as “Local Cost (Participant Fees).”
  • Booked as revenue. [Official report — Texas Comptroller, Fees Schedule, Fiscal 2026–27 Estimated Revenues.] Line items printed in the state's estimated-revenue tables: “COBJ 3710 — Court Fines 84,138,000,” “Court Costs 600,000,” “Arrest Fees 1,374,000.”

The other vocabulary — the same system's own record of the effects, quoted at the same size:

  • The Chief Justice's numbers. [Official address — Chief Justice Nathan Hecht, State of the Judiciary, February 1, 2017.] “Altogether, over $1 billion was collected. Some defendants said they couldn't pay at all. Judges believed them in about 100,000 cases, waiving the fines or sentencing them to community service. In 640,000 cases—16%—defendants went to jail for minor offenses.” And: “Jailing criminal defendants who cannot pay their fines and court costs—commonly called debtors' prison—keeps them from jobs, hurts their families, makes them dependent on society, and costs the taxpayers money. Most importantly, it is illegal under the United States Constitution.” On the fee stack itself: “there are extra fees for payment plans, for missed payments, for making payments—yes, there is even a fee for making a payment—pay to pay.”
  • The Legislature's own statement of what it was fixing. [Primary document — SB 1913 Author's/Sponsor's Statement of Intent, Senate Research Center bill analysis (2017).] “Low-income persons throughout Texas become trapped in a cycle of debt when they cannot afford to pay traffic tickets and other low-level, fine-only citations.” And: “While in jail, they lose their jobs and housing and are driven deeper into poverty.”
  • The reform's success, stated in revenue's own units. [Official address — 2019 State of the Judiciary, as printed by the Legislative Reference Library.] After SB 1913: “Judges are issuing fewer warrants, imposing more community service, waiving fines and fees for the indigent, and jailing fewer.” Then: “The Judicial Council was wrong about one thing. It predicted revenue would stay the same. In fact, state and local revenue per case has increased 4%.” Filed as text: the reform's result is reported, by its own authors, partly as a revenue figure.

What this layer documents is language and effect, side by side. It does not state that any official intended the gap between the two vocabularies, or perceived it. Anything about what the speakers saw is CAN'T VERIFY.

Sources: Weir v. State, 278 S.W.3d 364 (Tex. Crim. App. 2009); Tex. Code Crim. Proc. art. 42A.652; Legislative Budget Board, Uniform Cost Report; Texas Comptroller, Fees Schedule, Fiscal 2026–27 Estimated Revenues; Chief Justice Hecht, State of the Judiciary (Feb. 1, 2017): lrl.texas.gov (PDF); SB 1913 bill analysis: capitol.texas.gov — SB 1913 analysis. Read October 29, 2026.

THE SEEING QUESTION — THE LEGAL ANCHOR: CUSTOM IS NOT A DEFENSE

The T.J. Hooper, 60 F.2d 737 (2d Cir. 1932) (Learned Hand, J.) [court opinion]. Two coal barges towed by the tugs Montrose and Hooper were lost in a March 1928 gale; the tugs were held unseaworthy because they carried no working radio receiving sets by which they could have received the weather broadcasts that would have sent them to shelter. The industry had not generally adopted the sets. Hand, at 740:

“Is it then a final answer that the business had not yet generally adopted receiving sets? There are, no doubt, cases where courts seem to make the general practice of the calling the standard of proper diligence; we have indeed given some currency to the notion ourselves… Indeed in most cases reasonable prudence is in fact common prudence; but strictly it is never its measure; a whole calling may have unduly lagged in the adoption of new and available devices. It never may set its own tests, however persuasive be its usages. Courts must in the end say what is required; there are precautions so imperative that even their universal disregard will not excuse their omission.”

Precision notes, printed because this page quotes exactly: (1) the opinion's last word above is “omission” — later paraphrases rendering it “violation” are not the opinion. (2) The often-attributed Hand phrase that custom “never does more than give evidence” was not found in the opinion text and is not attributed to Hand on this page. The rule predates Hand, from the U.S. Supreme Court in a Texas railway case [court opinion — Texas & Pacific Ry. Co. v. Behymer, 189 U.S. 468, 470 (1903) (Holmes, J.)]: “What usually is done may be evidence of what ought to be done, but what ought to be done is fixed by a standard of reasonable prudence, whether it usually is complied with or not.”

Sources: The T.J. Hooper, 60 F.2d 737, 740 (2d Cir. 1932); Texas & Pacific Ry. Co. v. Behymer, 189 U.S. 468, 470 (1903). Read October 29, 2026.

THE SEEING QUESTION — THE LEGAL ANCHOR: THE KNOWLEDGE GAP, AND THE BRADY CONTRAST

Stated from quoted doctrine only — the shape of two elements, not an accusation against any person.

  • Deliberate indifference requires actual knowledge. [Court opinion — Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825, 837–838 (1994).] “a prison official cannot be found liable under the Eighth Amendment for denying an inmate humane conditions of confinement unless the official knows of and disregards an excessive risk to inmate health or safety; the official must both be aware of facts from which the inference could be drawn that a substantial risk of serious harm exists, and he must also draw the inference.” And: “an official's failure to alleviate a significant risk that he should have perceived but did not, while no cause for commendation, cannot under our cases be condemned as the infliction of punishment.” Knowledge “may be inferred” from a risk's obviousness — “but the inference cannot be conclusive, for we know that people are not always conscious of what reasonable people would be conscious of.”
  • Section 242 requires specific intent. [Court opinion — Screws v. United States, 325 U.S. 91 (1945); the Douglas opinion was a plurality, later adopted by binding opinions — labeled per Congressional Research Service.] “Willfully” requires “a specific intent to deprive of a right which has been made specific by the express terms of the Constitution or laws of the United States or by decisions interpreting them” (at 101); the actor must act “in open defiance or in reckless disregard of a constitutional requirement which has been made specific and definite” (at 105); and “it was not sufficient that petitioners had a generally bad purpose” (at 106–107). The Congressional Research Service states the consequence in official-report language [official report — CRS, LSB10486 (2020)]: “the specific intent requirement requires the prosecution to prove that a local official intended to violate a federal right, as opposed to simply intending to, for example, assault a victim.”
  • The gap, stated as doctrine (synthesis of the quoted texts above, labeled as synthesis): both flagship remedies are gated on the official's perception. If the factfinder credits that the official genuinely did not perceive the risk (Farmer) or did not aim at, know of, or recklessly disregard the defined right (Screws), the claim fails on the knowledge/intent element even if the underlying harm is conceded — what remains is at most negligence, which Farmer expressly places outside the Eighth Amendment. The doctrines as built reach the official who sees and disregards, and the official who defies. By their own terms they do not reach the official embedded in a practice the official has never seen as wrong at all — the state the mechanism layer above documents organizations producing.
  • The Brady contrast — the one doctrine that asks nothing about good or bad faith. [Court opinion — Brady v. Maryland, 373 U.S. 83, 87 (1963).] Suppression of favorable material evidence violates due process “irrespective of the good faith or bad faith of the prosecution.” It is the documented exception in this record where knowledge is not an element. (The knowledge-requiring counterpart, Napue v. Illinois, 360 U.S. 264, 269 (1959) — false evidence “known to be such by representatives of the State” — is printed in full in the Knowledge Question above.) One doctrine punishes the knowing act and forgives everything short of knowledge; the other does not ask what the prosecution knew at all. Between them lies the space this layer documents.

Sources: Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825 (1994); Screws v. United States, 325 U.S. 91 (1945); Congressional Research Service, LSB10486 (2020); Brady v. Maryland, 373 U.S. 83, 87 (1963); Napue v. Illinois, 360 U.S. 264 (1959). Read October 29, 2026.

This layer cannot say what any living official sees or does not see. No source in this research documents what any living Texas or federal official personally perceives about any practice documented here. Interior states are CAN'T VERIFY — by design of the research, not by its failure.

The wording change has a documented range, not a year. “Is compromised” (2000, court-quoted) → “may be compromised” (in place by 2015, primary text). No specific year for the change is claimed anywhere in this layer.

Vaughan's concept is a documented mechanism to compare against the paperwork — not proof about Texas courts. Her evidence is NASA's. Applying the mechanism to any other institution is an analogy, presented only as an analogy, with the hindsight critique printed beside it at the same size.

The one-sentence “Vaughan definition” is not printed as a verified quotation, because it was not verified against her 1996 book in this research. The Hand phrase “never does more than give evidence” is not attributed to Hand, because it was not found in the T.J. Hooper opinion.

THE MONEY EXHIBIT — RICH VERSUS POOR: THE DOCUMENTED MECHANISMS

Each item below is a mechanism — a rule or a price that operates differently by wealth — with its evidence. Rhetorical framings (“two-tiered justice”) are attributed where used, not adopted.

1 · Cash bail converts directly into detention — and detention changes the plea. In O'Donnell v. Harris County, 251 F. Supp. 3d 1052 (S.D. Tex. 2017), aff'd as modified (5th Cir. 2018), consent decree approved 2019, the district court found Harris County's secured-money-bail practice for misdemeanors violated due process and equal protection; found that secured money bail “does not meaningfully add” to assuring appearance or absence of new criminal activity vs. unsecured/personal bonds; and credited evidence that detention for inability to pay increases future crime and failure to appear. Expert analysis in the case: over 40% of Harris County misdemeanor arrestees were detained until case disposition because they could not pay. Heaton, Mayson & Stevenson (Stanford Law Review, 2017; Houston/Harris data, cited in O'Donnell): had the lowest-risk misdemeanor defendants (bail ≤ $500) been released on unsecured bonds 2008–2013, an estimated 40,000 more people released, ~6,000 fewer convictions, ~400,000 jail days avoided, and 1,600 fewer felonies + 2,400 fewer misdemeanors committed in the following 18 months — detention, in this study, caused crime and convictions rather than preventing them. The causal evidence, national: Dobbie, Goldin & Yang (American Economic Review, 2018; Philadelphia/Miami, quasi-random judge assignment) — pretrial detention increased guilty pleas from 33% to 44%; release reduced the probability of conviction by 14 percentage points, driven by a 10.8-point drop in guilty pleas; detention had no net effect on future crime and reduced formal employment ~9 points / 20% at 3–4 years. A post-reform evaluation (Texas A&M Public Policy Research Institute, court-monitor team, 2026) tracking 370,000+ Harris County cases over 8 years found overall costs for statistically similar cases fell from $3,633 to $2,442 (−~⅓) after Rule 9 replaced wealth-based with risk-based release, alongside more dismissals and fewer detention-driven pleas. Scope note: O'Donnell was misdemeanor/Harris-County-specific; Texas's 2021 bail legislation (SB 6) moved in a different direction for many offenses — Harris County's decree is not generalized statewide here.

2 · A fixed fine is a different size of punishment by wealth — mechanically, in the statute's own waiver line. The same $6,000 § 709.001 fine is: for a household at or below 125% of the poverty line, waivable in full on a documented indigence finding (§ 709.001(c)–(d)); for a middle-income defendant above that line, months of installment payments under supervision; for a wealthy defendant, a single payment with no supervision-time consequence. The statutory price is identical; the punishment experienced — debt duration, supervision exposure, revocation risk, license risk — is not. That is a description of the rules in the Stack and the Knowledge Trail, not an inference. Day-fine systems exist precisely to equalize this (Purpose Test, above), and the Finnish causal evidence shows fixed fines under-deter the wealthy: the reoffending response to larger fines was concentrated among high-income drivers. Kantorowicz-Reznichenko's point that a fixed schedule lets the wealthy “purchase” the offense at trivial relative cost is an author's characterization in a peer-reviewed law-and-economics article — labeled as argument. Collection reality cuts the other way for the state: unpayable fixed amounts are largely not collected (San Francisco probation fee: 9% collected; Wisconsin: $0.28 on the dollar; Brennan: billions in uncollectible court debt nationally [report finding]).

3 · Fighting vs. pleading is priced. The price of fighting: retained counsel $2,500–$10,000+ for a first misdemeanor DWI [law-firm], plus expert fees if the blood/breath result is contested (one [law-firm] summary: private toxicologist $2,000–$7,000), plus the bond premium, plus months of pretrial conditions — which may themselves include a paid interlock (below). Deferred adjudication is the priced middle path, where it is available: avoiding a “final conviction” avoids the $6,000 state fine and conviction license actions, but the defendant still pays court costs ($270–$370), 12–24 months of supervision fees, IID, classes, and evaluation — the discount for pleading into supervision is itself a multi-thousand-dollar, multi-year payment stream. (At BAC ≥ 0.15, deferred adjudication is statutorily unavailable — Door 6.) One expert in the O'Donnell litigation described the prior Harris County system as “a two-track system 'designed' to elicit guilty pleas from poor people” — [expert opinion in litigation, labeled as such].

4 · The paid alternatives. Each payment below buys something specific — liberty, mobility, or compliance — that not paying does not buy:

Paid alternativeWhat the payment buysDocumented price
Ignition interlockLegal driving during suspension/probation instead of not driving (and instead of the job consequences of not driving); also the condition that makes an occupational license possible in DWI cases$70–$150 install + $60–$150/month + $10 DPS license. The statute provides an ability-to-pay payment plan (up to 2× the period) but not a waiver of the device cost in the sources found
Occupational driver licenseDriving to work, school, and essential duties during suspension$10–$20 DPS + county filing process $200–$500 total [law-firm] + prerequisite SR-22
Community supervision itselfLiberty instead of the jail term — purchased with monthly fees plus program/testing fees for 1–2 years, with revocation exposure for nonpayment subject to Bearden / art. 42A.751$25–$60/month (Stack, above)
Private / program complianceDWI education, intervention, evaluations, VIP, and any recommended treatment — all defendant-paid; non-completion = violation / extended license suspension$55 evaluation (Travis) to $250–$400 intervention; treatment unbounded in the sources
Bondsman / cash bondPretrial liberty — the D1 consequences above attach to not buying it~10% nonrefundable premium, or the full amount in cash (Stack, above)

The interlock's two documented faces, printed together. Interlock is both a pay-to-drive mechanism — legal mobility, for a monthly price, inside this exhibit's paid-alternatives table — and the best-evidenced reducer in the whole exhibit: a median 67% rearrest reduction while installed (CDC systematic review, Purpose Test above). Both faces are documented. Neither cancels the other.

5 · The outcome differences, measured — summary. Dobbie-Goldin-Yang 2018 (judge-leniency design, 420k+ defendants): detention — i.e., inability to pay bail — → more pleas, more convictions, less employment, no net crime benefit. Heaton-Mayson-Stevenson 2017 (Harris County): inability to pay ≤ $500 bail → thousands of avoidable convictions and more subsequent offending. O'Donnell findings 2017–19: secured bail added no measurable appearance/safety benefit; over 40% of misdemeanor arrestees detained for inability to pay. Wisconsin surcharge study: +$200 in fees → more felony recidivism, with harm concentrated in disadvantaged groups [author finding, as summarized by FFJC]. Piquero et al. (Florida juveniles): fees → +3.7 points of recidivism, and the fee amounts assessed were themselves higher for Black and Hispanic youth. Texas OCA data via Slavinski & Pettit: waivers under 1% (county misdemeanors) vs. jail credit at 8.8% on average — the form of “payment” tracks inability to pay in cash: those who cannot pay, pay with jail days at $100–$150/day (the rate conflict in the Time Dimension layer above).

Sources: O'Donnell v. Harris County, 251 F. Supp. 3d 1052 (S.D. Tex. 2017); Heaton, Mayson & Stevenson, 69 Stan. L. Rev. 711 (2017); Dobbie, Goldin & Yang, American Economic Review 108, 201 (2018); Texas A&M PPRI (2026): ppri.tamu.edu — O'Donnell delivers; Transp. Code § 709.001(c)–(d). Interested-source labels: FFJC, Brennan Center, ACLU, and Texas Fair Defense Project / Texas Appleseed are advocacy organizations — their data citations above are traced to OCA/DPS/court records where noted, but their characterizations are advocacy. Law-firm pages are commercial marketing. The AER / Stanford Law Review / RSF / PLOS studies and the O'Donnell findings are peer-reviewed / judicial findings.

THE PREMISE CORRECTION — PRINTED AT FULL SIZE

NO OCCUPANCY GUARANTEES WERE FOUND IN TEXAS STATE CONTRACTS. In the Public Interest's 2013 report Criminal: How Lockup Quotas and “Low-Crime Taxes” Guarantee Profits reviewed many Texas private-prison contracts and found no bed-guarantee clause; TDCJ pays per inmate actually housed, counted at midnight — the “Midnight Strength Report.” Nationally, the same review found 65% of contracts carried 80–100% occupancy guarantees (90% most common) — Texas state contracts were the exception, not the example. Any claim that Texas state prison contracts pay for empty beds is, on this record, UNTRACED and contradicted by the one systematic contract review found.

Occupancy guarantees DO exist — in FEDERAL ICE contracts in Texas. GEO Group's South Texas facility at Pearsall: a 2011 ICE contract under which GEO expected ~$45M annual revenue carried a 75% occupancy guarantee. The Dilley South Texas Family Residential Center (CoreCivic): a no-bid 4-year, ~$1B contract reported as paying as if at 100% capacity regardless of occupancy, at $285/day per resident at full capacity — 2014-era figures, labeled with their era; other contemporaneous reporting put Dilley near $300/day and GEO's Karnes facility at $132–160/day depending on year — printed as dated, differing-by-year figures, not one rate.

UNTRACED, printed as untraced: a widely repeated claim that “GEO made $254M profit in 2025” was seen only in a secondary explainer and could not be traced to a filing in this research. It is not counted as fact anywhere on this page.

Source: In the Public Interest (2013): inthepublicinterest.org — Criminal: Lockup Quotas report (PDF). Read October 29, 2026.

THE JAIL MONEY — HOW OFTEN THEY ARE PAID, AND THE STRUCTURE ITSELF

How often: billing is per-diem invoicing against the count — monthly cycles are the documented norm in the contracts and reporting reviewed; no Texas contract text stating a different cycle was verified. For TDCJ contracts, the count that is paid on is the actual midnight count (above).

The operators and facilities (documented): GEO Group (South Texas ICE Processing Center, Pearsall; Karnes; Montgomery/Conroe; Central Texas Detention, San Antonio), CoreCivic (Houston Processing Center — ICE extension to 2029, reported ~$39.5M, labeled as reporting; T. Don Hutto, Taylor — direct ICE contract to 2030; Eden), MTC, and LaSalle Corrections. TDCJ's FY2026–27 request documents 2,040 private prison beds, 4,080 privately operated state-jail beds, 500 DWI beds, and a 500-bed work facility.

The structure is shrinking on the state side: on September 1, 2025, TDCJ assumed operations of seven formerly MTC-run, state-owned facilities — Billy Moore, Bradshaw State Jail, Bridgeport, Coleman, Kyle, Lindsey State Jail, and Willacy State Jail — funded by the 89th Legislature.

Source: TDCJ, private facilities transition: tdcj.texas.gov — facilities transition. Read October 29, 2026.

THE JAIL MONEY — TARRANT COUNTY: THE GAP, STATED PLAINLY

No current federal per-diem rate for Tarrant County Jail was found in this research — stated plainly as a gap, rather than estimated. Tarrant's documented jail-economics episode is the food contract:

In 2003–04, Aramark bid $0.88 per meal against the incumbent's $0.96 and took over Tarrant County Jail food service in December 2003 on a $3.3M contract. After documented complaints (soured beans, food-safety temperatures), the county issued a 30-day ultimatum in February 2004, and Aramark resigned effective March 22, 2004. The successor, Mid-State, resumed on a $3.79M contract serving 3,500 prisoners/day (≈ $2.97/day at three meals — arithmetic, labeled).

Source: Prison Legal News summary of local reporting. Read October 29, 2026.
