Sources are labeled by type throughout. Where the record does not contain something, this door prints NOT FOUND rather than filling the gap.

THE ANCHOR — THE PANEL FEE

The class. The MADD Victim Impact Panel (VIP) is a court-ordered, roughly two-hour panel at which victims and survivors of impaired-driving crashes tell their stories to offenders. MADD states its purpose on its own online panel page: “The purpose of the Victim Impact Panel (VIP) program is to help drunk and drugged driving offenders to recognize and internalize the lasting and long-term effects of substance-impaired driving. The classes seek to create an empathy and understanding of the tragedy, leave a permanent impression that leads to changes in thinking and behavior and prevents future offenses.” His class runs about $65 — that figure rests on secondary legal explainers and his own case record; the MADD pages retrieved for this research did not display a price, so the price on MADD's own checkout is NOT VERIFIED in this record.

Source types: [agency/program materials — online.maddvip.org] · [legal explainer — secondary sources, for the typical fee range of $25–$100 by jurisdiction].

MADD's stated rationale for the fee, in MADD's own words:

“MADD Victim Impact Panels not only put offenders face-to face with the victims of drunk and drugged driving — the program also provides much needed mission funds that MADD invests right back into the community to prevent these crimes from happening again.”

“Panels also provide victims with a healing opportunity to share their stories in a meaningful way.”

On MADD's own telling, the fee is not priced as the cost of the two hours in the room. It is mission funding — revenue for the organization, reinvested in its work. That is MADD's stated rationale, printed in its own words, neither endorsed nor condemned here.

Source type: [agency/program materials — maddvip.org, retrieved October 29, 2026].

Who delivers the panel. The people whose stories constitute the panel are volunteers, not paid participants — victims and survivors MADD recruits and trains. MADD's own volunteer page lists Victim Impact Panel Coordinator among its volunteer roles, and a MADD VIP Facilitator posting describes the role as “a volunteer position,” 6–12 hours per month — and lists among the facilitator's duties, at registration, the “collection of funds.” The documented picture: the testimony at the center of the product is donated; the fee is collected, in part, by other volunteers; the money goes to the organization's mission funds.

Source types: [agency/program materials — madd.org/volunteer; Volunteer Wyoming posting for MADD] · [legal explainer summarizing MADD's volunteer structure].

MADD's documented finances.

  • MADD (Mothers Against Drunk Driving, headquartered in Irving, Texas) is a 501(c)(3) nonprofit.
  • MADD's own 2023 Impact Report statement of activities reports total revenue of $36,647,349 and total expenses of $39,083,990 for the year ended December 31, 2023 — expenses exceeded revenue. The same statement carries a revenue line captioned “Victim impact panels and court ordered revenue” — in MADD's own books, panel fees are a named revenue stream. The dollar amount on that line could not be read reliably from the retrieved copy of the report and is printed here as NOT VERIFIED — no number is printed for it.
  • MADD's Form 990 for tax year 2022, Schedule J (the compensation schedule, as filed) reports Interim CEO Ellen Willmott's total compensation at $301,705; Chief Government Affairs Officer Stephanie Manning, $271,542; CIO Abram Basom, $230,534.

Source types: [agency/program materials — MADD 2023 Impact Report] · [Form 990 filings — 2022 return, Schedule J, via docs.candid.org].

What “nonprofit” does and does not mean, stated factually. Under 26 U.S.C. § 501(c)(3), exemption requires that “no part of the net earnings of [the organization] inures to the benefit of any private shareholder or individual.” The IRS's rule prohibits inurement — net earnings flowing to insiders — and more than incidental private benefit. It does not prohibit revenue, and it does not prohibit salaries: an organization may pay reasonable compensation for services; federal sanction (intermediate sanctions under § 4958) attaches to compensation above fair market value paid to a person with influence over the organization. Printed flatly: nonprofit status constrains the distribution of profit to owners or insiders, because there are no owners. It does not constrain the size of the revenue stream, and it says nothing by itself about whether any particular fee is fair.

Source types: [statute text — 26 U.S.C. §§ 501(c)(3), 4958] · [agency materials — IRS, Inurement/Private Benefit].

Two gaps, printed as gaps.

  • No published VIP indigence waiver: NOT FOUND. No indigence waiver, sliding scale, or hardship provision for the MADD VIP fee was found on maddvip.org or online.maddvip.org as retrieved for this research, and no MADD-published waiver policy was found in search. Absence from the retrieved pages is not proof no local chapter has ever waived a fee — it is proof no published policy was found.
  • No published VIP nonpayment rule: NOT FOUND. No MADD-published policy on nonpayment of the panel fee, and no case or statute addressing VIP-fee nonpayment as such, was found. The general record shows the mechanics at the door rather than in a courtroom: registration is prepaid, the certificate issues on completion, and completion requires having paid. No document found states what happens to a person who presents himself unable to pay.

THE REASONS FOR — THE STATED REASONING, IN THE SOURCES' OWN WORDS

1. Accountability — the “user pays” principle. The rationale has an official name in the government documents that state it most plainly. The United Kingdom runs the closest structural cousin of the Texas DWI education requirement — court-referred drink-drive rehabilitation courses, offender-paid — and its government stated the funding logic in writing. The Explanatory Memorandum to the Rehabilitation Courses (Relevant Drink Offences) Regulations 2012 says of the scheme's administration: “the costs of administration are transferred from the taxpayer to the offender via the provider,” and the accompanying Driving Standards Agency consultation document states: “Transferring the funding for the new scheme from the general taxpayer to the offender complies with the user pays principle.” The same document puts the offender price on the record: providers “recover the cost of their fees from the offenders who attend the courses — on average 48,500 offenders complete DDRS courses per annum at a cost of between £150 and £250 per course.” The strongest form of this reason is arithmetic: someone pays for supervision and programs; the documented choice made by legislatures is that it be the person whose conduct occasioned them.

Source type: [agency/program materials — UK Driving Standards Agency / legislation.gov.uk].

2. Sustainability — the fees are why the programs run. Texas's own supervision agency makes the point structurally. The Texas Department of Criminal Justice's Community Justice Assistance Division (TDCJ-CJAD), which funds the local Community Supervision and Corrections Departments (CSCDs), states on its official funding page that the Legislature's funds “provide about 65 percent of their operating budgets. Additionally, they receive funds by collecting court-ordered fees from offenders.” A CJAD presentation puts it at “approximately 2/3 of CSCD funding comes from the Legislature through CJAD, and the remaining funds are raised through the collection of supervision fees.” On the official account, offender fees are not incidental revenue — they are roughly a third of the operating budget of Texas community supervision. The same presentation gives the agency's cost argument for supervision itself: the state cost is “about $376 per year for supervising felony probationers vs. $17,338 per year for incarceration” — supervision is the cheap alternative to prison, and offender fees are part of how the cheap alternative is financed.

The cleanest documented exhibit that fees alone can carry a program: San Francisco's First Offender Prostitution Program, a court-mandated rehabilitation/diversion program evaluated by the National Institute of Justice. As summarized from that evaluation, the program “operated for over twelve years without any taxpayer funding, instead using participant fees to cover all direct costs. These fees also generated nearly one million dollars for recovery programs serving survivors of commercial sexual exploitation.” The NIJ evaluation also found substantially reduced recidivism after the program started.

Source types: [agency/program materials — TDCJ-CJAD, “How We Distribute Funds”; CJAD presentation materials] · [academic — summary of the National Institute of Justice evaluation].

3. Recoupment doctrine — the Texas courts' framing. Community supervision in Texas is, in the Court of Criminal Appeals' framing in Speth v. State, “not a right, but a contractual privilege, and conditions thereof are terms of the contract entered into between the trial court and the defendant” — and supervision fees under art. 42A.652 are, in the Tenth Court of Appeals' words in Smith v. State (2021), “a nonpunitive recoupment of the costs of judicial resources expended in connection with the community-supervision contract.” On this framing, paying is not punishment; it is the price term of a privilege the defendant accepted instead of jail. Because the charge is classified as recoupment rather than punishment, it is not required to be pronounced at sentencing and does not carry the legal incidents of a fine — the classification is the load-bearing wall of the structure, printed here as doctrine.

Source type: [case text — Speth v. State, 6 S.W.3d 530, 534–35 (Tex. Crim. App. 1999); Smith v. State (Tex. App.—Waco 2021)].

THE REASONS AGAINST — AT THE SAME SIZE, AT THE SAME DEPTH

1. Collection costs approach — and can exceed — what is collected. The Brennan Center for Justice's The Steep Costs of Criminal Justice Fees and Fines (2019) studied ten counties in Florida, New Mexico, and Texas. Its researchers found that “no one knows how much is spent imposing, enforcing, and collecting these court-imposed financial obligations,” and their partial estimate put collection costs at “$0.41 or more per dollar collected” — against the fractions of a cent to under a cent per dollar that tax collection costs. On jailing: “Jailing people for nonpayment is by far the most expensive method of enforcing collections and generates little to no revenue making it highly uneconomical” — in Bernalillo County, New Mexico, jail costs were as much as 98 percent of the collection costs documented, and the companion study found jailing consumed as much as 115 percent of the fees and fines assessed in some researched counties. The report also records, from watching more than 1,000 proceedings, that judges “rarely hold ability-to-pay hearings.” Its structural finding: “When courts are pressured to act, in essence, as collection arms of the state, their traditional independence suffers. When probation and parole officers must devote time to fee collection instead of public safety and rehabilitation, they too compromise their roles.”

Source type: [advocacy research — Brennan Center for Justice (2019, 2010); research assistance from the Texas Public Policy Foundation and Right on Crime, as the report itself discloses].

2. The Ferguson record — a system measured in revenue, in officials' own words. The U.S. Department of Justice's investigation of the Ferguson Police Department (March 4, 2015) found: “Ferguson's law enforcement practices are shaped by the City's focus on revenue rather than by public safety needs.” Fines and fees rose from 12% of the city's budget (FY2012/2013) to a budgeted 23% for FY2015; officials evaluated criminal-justice actors' performance by the revenue they produced; and when a councilmember opposed the municipal judge's reappointment over his courtroom conduct, the City Manager answered in writing: “It goes without saying the City cannot afford to lose any efficiency in our Courts, nor experience any decrease in our Fines and Forfeitures.” DOJ further found that a single missed, partial, or late payment could result in incarceration, that warrants were used almost exclusively to coerce payment, and that the court refused to reduce or waive payments even when defendants were jailed for inability to pay.

Source type: [government investigation — U.S. DOJ Civil Rights Division, Investigation of the Ferguson Police Department (2015)].

3. Providers paid per offender, paid per month — the fee written into the sentence itself. Human Rights Watch's Profiting from Probation (February 2014) documented more than 1,000 courts delegating misdemeanor probation to for-profit companies whose entire revenue is the offenders' fees. Its structural findings: “Supervision fees are the financial cornerstone of the private probation business” — and in at least one documented contract, guaranteed by the court itself. Sentinel Offender Services' contract with Richmond County stated, “In consideration of the probation or monitoring services provided by Sentinel the Court agrees that each sentence shall provide for a probation or monitoring fee payable directly to Sentinel.” The fee is not incidental to the sentence; the contract requires the sentence to contain it. The human arithmetic in the same record: Thomas Barrett, unemployed, was ordered to pay a $200 fine for stealing a $2 can of beer — and $360 per month in Sentinel supervision and monitoring fees; he fell behind, owed over $1,000 in fees (five times the fine), and was jailed on Sentinel's revocation petition, with no ability-to-pay consideration HRW could find. And the oversight gap: “Companies refuse to disclose how much money they collect in fees from offenders under their supervision. Remarkably, the courts that hire them generally do not demand this information either.” Texas supervises through public CSCDs, not private probation companies — the HRW record is the model's for-profit end, printed here because it is the same fee logic with the public intermediary removed, and because the interlock vendor in a Texas DWI case is a private company paid per offender per day.

Source type: [advocacy research — Human Rights Watch (2014), quoting the contract; ACLU account of the same Barrett case].

4. The Texas supervision study — the size of the bill predicts failure at the rehabilitation. A study of one Texas Community Supervision and Corrections jurisdiction (records 2012–October 2019) examined intervention fees — the charges for the mandated services themselves: drug testing, treatment, classes, electronic monitoring. Findings: approximately 95 percent of individuals on probation were required to pay intervention fees; the average total was $320.75 (median $140); 46 percent of individuals with intervention fees had their probation revoked (23% for technical violations, 23% for new offenses); and “the more money people owed in intervention fees, the more likely they were to have their probation revoked,” with compliance with the mandated programs themselves as a key pathway — “the cost of interventions may shape whether probationers succeed or fail under supervision.” The same jurisdiction's earlier study (Ruhland et al., 2020; 1,660 probationers) found: “The total amount of fees assessed was a significant predictor of revocation for the commission of both a new criminal offense and technical violation.”

Source type: [peer-reviewed / academic research — Texas CSCD administrative records study, via Fines and Fees Justice Center research summary, “The Hidden Cost of Compliance” (July 2025); Ruhland et al. (2020)].

SUPERVISION FEES — “WHY DO I HAVE TO PAY TO BE REHABILITATED?”

That question, verbatim, is this layer's door. It is answered only by the documented record below — what the statutes set, what officials and vendors say the fees are for, and what the research measures — and by nothing else.

Probation (community supervision) — the fee as written. Texas Code of Criminal Procedure art. 42A.652(a), verbatim: “a judge who grants community supervision to a defendant shall set a reimbursement fee of not less than $25 and not more than $60 to be paid each month during the period of community supervision by the defendant.” The judge “may make payment of the monthly reimbursement fee a condition of granting or continuing the community supervision” (§ 42A.652(b)) — paying is a condition of staying out of jail, in the statute's own terms. The waiver exists in the same subsection, verbatim: “The judge may waive or reduce the reimbursement fee or suspend a monthly payment of the fee if the judge determines that payment of the reimbursement fee would cause the defendant a significant financial hardship.” It is discretionary (“may”), judge-initiated on a hardship finding — not automatic on indigence. Note what the 2019 Legislature did to the label: SB 346 (86th Leg.) renamed the charge from a monthly “fee” to a monthly “reimbursement fee” across Chapter 42A — the recoupment classification written into the name itself.

Source types: [statute text — Tex. Code Crim. Proc. art. 42A.652] · [legislative record — SB 346 bill analysis, Legislative Reference Library].

Parole — the fee as written. Texas Government Code § 508.182(a): a parole panel shall require as a condition of parole or mandatory supervision, for each month under supervision: “a parole supervision fee of $10; and an administrative fee of $8” — $18/month total. The destinations are in the statute (§ 508.182(e)): the $10 supervision fee is deposited in the general revenue fund; the $8 administrative fee goes to the compensation to victims of crime fund. A panel may defer payments on request (the debt survives, payable within two years). A separate $5/month supervision fee applies to releasees convicted of listed sex offenses, directed to the sexual assault program fund (§ 508.189).

Source type: [statute text — Tex. Gov't Code §§ 508.182, 508.189].

The documented answer, in three layers, each sourced. (i) The statute sets the fee as a condition of the supervision itself. (ii) The official rationale is recoupment — the fee reimburses the cost of supervising you, classified as nonpunitive. (iii) The fiscal structure is that the supervision department's budget is built to need the money — roughly a third of it, on the agency's own figures (see The Reasons For, above). No document found states the purpose as rehabilitation of the payer. The rehabilitation in the Texas scheme is the stated purpose of the supervision; the fee's stated purpose is paying for it.

The against, at the same size. Nonpayment of mandated fees is a technical violation of supervision — not a new crime, but a breach that can end supervision; one Texas study of felony probationers found 26% of 2,007 individuals revoked for technical violations, a category that expressly included failure to pay. Bearden's protection, as applied in Texas, holds only where nonpayment is the sole allegation (see The Legal Frame, below) — a supervision file with any second allegation stands outside art. 42A.751(i)'s proof requirement as the courts have read it.

Source types: [academic — revocation literature, via Reason Foundation policy brief's account of the Texas study; Hays County revocation study] · [statute and case text — as cited in The Legal Frame].

THE LEGAL FRAME — BEARDEN'S RULE, AND ITS TEXAS NARROWING, AT THE SAME SIZE

Bearden v. Georgia, 461 U.S. 660 (1983) — the constitutional floor. Danny Bearden's probation was revoked automatically when he could not pay his fine and restitution, without any inquiry into why. The Supreme Court reversed, holding (at 672–673):

“We hold, therefore, that in revocation proceedings for failure to pay a fine or restitution, a sentencing court must inquire into the reasons for the failure to pay. If the probationer willfully refused to pay or failed to make sufficient bona fide efforts legally to acquire the resources to pay, the court may revoke probation and sentence the defendant to imprisonment within the authorized range of its sentencing authority. If the probationer could not pay despite sufficient bona fide efforts to acquire the resources to do so, the court must consider alternate measures of punishment other than imprisonment. Only if alternate measures are not adequate to meet the State's interests in punishment and deterrence may the court imprison a probationer who has made sufficient bona fide efforts to pay. To do otherwise would deprive the probationer of his conditional freedom simply because, through no fault of his own, he cannot pay the fine. Such a deprivation would be contrary to the fundamental fairness required by the Fourteenth Amendment.”

Bearden built on Williams v. Illinois, 399 U.S. 235 (1970) and Tate v. Short, 401 U.S. 395 (1971) — a state may not convert a fine into jail time solely because the defendant is indigent. Two limits are printed in the case itself and its progeny: the protection runs against imprisonment or revocation for nonpayment; it does not erase the obligation.

Source type: [case text — Bearden v. Georgia, 461 U.S. 660 (1983)].

The Texas narrowing, printed at the same size. Art. 42A.751(i) codifies Bearden for Texas community supervision, verbatim: “In a revocation hearing at which it is alleged only that the defendant violated the conditions of community supervision by failing to pay community supervision fees or court costs… the state must prove by a preponderance of the evidence that the defendant was able to pay and did not pay as ordered by the judge.” The limit is in the operative word — only. Texas courts read it strictly: where the State alleges any additional violation, the ability-to-pay proof is not required (Gipson v. State, 428 S.W.3d 107; Davis v. State (2019) and the line of cases following them). The protection is real and narrow at the same time; both facts are in the case law.

Parole — same protection, opposite burden. Texas Government Code § 508.182(f) makes inability to pay an affirmative defense to revocation where nonpayment of the parole fees is the only allegation — but the releasee “must prove the affirmative defense by a preponderance of the evidence.” For probationers the State must prove ability; for parolees the person must prove inability.

Source types: [statute text — Tex. Code Crim. Proc. art. 42A.751(i); Tex. Gov't Code § 508.182(f)] · [case text — Gipson; Davis].

THE GOAL — “IF REHABILITATION IS IN FACT THE GOAL, IF NOT, WHAT IS?”

That question, verbatim, is this layer's door. A system's interior intent is CAN'T VERIFY — no document can prove what “the system” wants. What can be documented is the goals the law and the organizations state, the structures they built and the metrics they publish, and one jurisdiction that abolished the fees. All three shelves are printed at the same size.

SHELF ONE — STATED GOALS. Texas Penal Code § 1.02, “Objectives of Code,” states the purposes of the entire penal system, in order:

“(1) to insure the public safety through: (A) the deterrent influence of the penalties hereinafter provided; (B) the rehabilitation of those convicted of violations of this code; and (C) such punishment as may be necessary to prevent likely recurrence of criminal behavior; … (3) to prescribe penalties that are proportionate to the seriousness of offenses and that permit recognition of differences in rehabilitation possibilities among individual offenders…”

Rehabilitation is in the text — second of the three means to public safety, between deterrence and punishment. The fee statutes are not in the list of means: § 1.02 names penalties, rehabilitation, and punishment as the instruments, and says nothing about charging the convicted for the instruments — fees are named nowhere in it. Community supervision's stated aims: art. 42A.301(a) authorizes conditions “designed to protect or restore the community or the victim, or punish, rehabilitate, or reform the defendant.” MADD's stated mission: “The mission of Mothers Against Drunk Driving is to end drunk driving, help fight drugged driving, support the victims of these violent crimes and prevent underage drinking.” The VIP's stated purpose is empathy and behavior change (The Anchor, above); the fee's stated function is “mission funds.” Both statements are MADD's own; they are answers to two different questions, and this door does not merge them.

Source types: [statute text — Tex. Penal Code § 1.02; Tex. Code Crim. Proc. art. 42A.301(a)] · [agency/program materials — MADD mission statement].

SHELF TWO — DOCUMENTED MACHINERY: WHAT GETS MEASURED AND REPORTED.

  • Revenue shares. Ferguson budgeted fines and fees at 23% of revenue and evaluated its justice actors on the revenue they produced (The Reasons Against, above). Texas's supervision departments are funded roughly a third by court-ordered fees collected from the people supervised, on the agency's own figures.
  • Collection rates. Jurisdictions tracked what comes in more carefully than what it costs to extract: collection costs of $0.41 or more per dollar, jailing consuming up to 115% of amounts assessed. Texas's Office of Court Administration does publish the waiver and jail-credit counts — in FY2019, 71,308 waivers against 508,698 jail-credit cases, with waivers running at under 1% of county misdemeanor cases — numbers that exist because the Legislature required them after SB 1913 (2017), and that show the relief mechanism used at that rate.
  • Vendor payments keyed to volume and duration. Private probation contracts guarantee a fee in every sentence. Ignition interlock — the mandated device in his own case — is priced per day: NHTSA's interlock toolkit estimates approximately $3 to $4 per day, “with the offender bearing those expenses,” and the industry's own market literature prices “recurring monthly monitoring revenue of $85 to $150 per device” and names “switching costs and customer lock-in” as the revenue strategy. In each structure, revenue rises with the number of people mandated and the length of time they remain mandated — and with nothing else. No vendor payment structure found in this research pays for a completion, a clean record, or a non-recurrence.
  • VIP outcomes, in MADD's own reporting: NOT FOUND. No MADD-published dashboard or annual reporting of VIP completion rates, attendee recidivism, or fee-waiver counts was found in this research. The VIP's recidivism outcomes were measured by university researchers, not in any MADD-published outcomes reporting found. What MADD publishes annually, in detail, is its financials. That asymmetry is a fact about the published record, printed without ornament: the money is reported; the rehabilitation outcomes of the people who paid are, in the public record retrieved, reported by others or not at all.

Source types: [government investigation — DOJ (2015)] · [advocacy research — Brennan Center (2019); HRW (2014)] · [agency data — Texas OCA, via the Money Exhibit record] · [agency/program materials — NHTSA interlock toolkit] · [commercial market research — Dataintelo (2025), the industry's description of its own revenue model].

SHELF THREE — THE COUNTER-EXAMPLE. California, AB 1869 (2020): effective July 1, 2021, California repealed the authority to impose 23 criminal administrative fees — expressly including probation supervision fees and parole supervision fees — and made every unpaid balance on them “unenforceable and uncollectible,” requiring that any portion of a judgment imposing them be vacated. San Diego County's official implementation page lists what stopped being charged: probation supervision and investigation fees, parole supervision fees, electronic monitoring fees, home detention fees, counsel fees, booking fees. The Legislature backfilled county revenue with a $65 million annual General Fund appropriation — California's documented answer to “someone has to pay” was: the general taxpayer, openly, through the budget — the exact transfer the user-pays principle argues against, made deliberately and in the open. No Texas counter-example of abolition was found; Texas's documented path is the opposite one — retain the fees, and add ability-to-pay procedure (SB 1913; art. 42A.751(i); the § 42A.652(b) hardship waiver) around them. Both states' records are printed; this door does not score them against each other.

Source types: [statute text — Cal. Penal Code § 1465.9; AB 1869 (Stats. 2020, ch. 92)] · [case text — People v. Greeley (2021)] · [agency materials — San Diego County; legislative fiscal analysis].

THE GOAL QUESTION, JUDGED BY ACTIONS — A FINDING ON ACTIONS, NOT OPINION (§17)

Filed October 30, 2026, as a finding on actions — under the same standard this record applies to him: words earn nothing; judgment attaches to deeds on the visible record.

The stated goal: rehabilitation — Texas Penal Code § 1.02 names it among the Code's objectives, and the mandate presents the panel as rehabilitative (its stated purpose: empathy, understanding, changed behavior — The Anchor, above).

The checks a rehabilitation-run system would pass: test whether its charge helps or hinders · price to protect completion · pay for outcomes instead of duration.

The documented deeds:

  • The payment's effect has never been tested — no study, ever, isolates paying for the panel against attending the identical panel free (The Limit Rail, below: NOT FOUND).
  • MADD's own words call the mandated fee "much needed mission funds" (The Anchor, above).
  • Supervision fees are paid for as long as a person stays in the system, never for finishing — the collector's documented interest in duration (Harper, in The Question Asked in Court, above).
  • Collection spends $0.41 or more per $1 collected, and Texas data shows the more fees owed, the more likely revocation — the charge producing the exact outcome rehabilitation exists to prevent (The Reasons Against; the Texas supervision study, above).
  • Relief valves are narrow and late — the § 42A.652(b) hardship waiver is discretionary; Bearden's protection, as read in Texas, holds only where nonpayment is the sole allegation (The Legal Frame, above).
  • His own night stands in it as a deed: the panel was completed October 29, 2026 — through a lockout, both corrections, and a second $65 payment on the recovered account. The money was collected twice; the rehabilitation was identical either way.

THE FINDING, printed at exactly the size the deeds carry: the behavior is consistent with revenue collected reliably, and inconsistent with rehabilitation being measured, tested, or protected. Interior intent stays CAN'T VERIFY — and under the deeds standard, it isn't needed. The actions are the verdict the record is allowed to give, and it gives it.

It closes by joining §17's two standing challenges (His Reading, below) — they remain open to anyone the record describes: state the goal and reconcile it with the deeds.

THE LIMIT RAIL

No source in this record states that payment is itself rehabilitative. No statute, agency, or organization in the research states that paying for a mandated class or a supervision visit improves the payer's rehabilitation, and no documented official or academic argument was found that charging the fee itself increases the rehabilitative effect. The fee's stated purposes are paying for the supervision, funding the mission, and sparing the taxpayer.

The paying-versus-attending-free study has never been run. No study was found that isolates the effect of paying for a victim impact panel — as against attending the identical panel free — on completion, recidivism, or any other outcome. The outcome literature studies attendance and total debt, never the price of the seat. NOT FOUND.

A system's interior intent is CAN'T VERIFY. This door prints stated goals, documented incentive structures, and measured outcomes side by side. It does not claim what any system, organization, or individual intends in their heart, because no document can verify that.

The stated goal and the documented machinery stand at the same size, and the distance between them is the finding. Rehabilitation stands written in the Code's objectives (§ 1.02(1)(B)) and in the panel's purpose statement, while the machinery documented beside those words measures revenue shares, collection rates, and per-day and per-month entitlements, and budgets built on the fees. Both are printed above, in their own words. The distance belongs to the reader.

No study has ever tested paying versus attending free. No source states that paying is itself rehabilitative. NOT FOUND. A deeper research pass (October 29, 2026) confirms and extends the two entries above: no study, ruling, official statement, scholarship, or provider page was found that tests payment against free attendance, or that claims the act of payment contributes to rehabilitation — the only rehabilitative-payment mechanism the literature documents attaches to restitution paid to victims, and the same literature reports that “[t]he same correlation was not found for payment of criminal fines.” Beside the silence, the one documented arrow runs the other way: Piquero & Jennings (2017) found that financial penalties increased the likelihood of recidivism in a sample of juvenile offenders. And no legal challenge to a victim-impact-panel fee — its amount, its non-waiver, or its payment terms — has ever been found in any court record searched: NOT FOUND.

Researched October 29, 2026, from the sources labeled in each layer above: statute and case text; agency and program materials (MADD, TDCJ-CJAD, NIJ, UK Driving Standards Agency); the DOJ Ferguson investigation; peer-reviewed and academic studies; and advocacy research (Brennan Center, Human Rights Watch), labeled as advocacy wherever used.

THE QUESTION, ASKED IN COURT

His extortion question — printed in His Reading below — has already been litigated, against a private probation vendor whose fees were its only revenue under its contracts. In McNeil v. Community Probation Services, LLC, No. 1:18-cv-00033, Doc. 414 (M.D. Tenn. Feb. 3, 2021), the extortion/RICO theory was dismissed on claim-of-right. The court quoted United States v. Enmons, 410 U.S. 396, 399–400 (1973): “‘wrongful’ has meaning in the Act only if it limits the statute's coverage to those instances where the obtaining of the property would itself be ‘wrongful’ because the alleged extortionist has no lawful claim to that property.” It then held: “Plaintiffs have not established CPS has ‘no lawful claim’ to the property at issue,” citing Malone v. City of Decatur, Alabama, 2018 WL 4901212, at 9–10 — a private probation company held a lawful claim to fines and fees for purposes of the claim-of-right defense, “even if the company's conduct were considered to be unconstitutional.” No court has ever held mandated-fee collection to be extortion. “Extortion” appears in the record as a pleaded characterization, and once as a judge's rhetoric in a civil oversight order (Harpersville, 2012: “judicially sanctioned extortion racket”) — never as an adjudicated holding against a vendor.

Source types: [case text — McNeil, Doc. 414 (M.D. Tenn. 2021), full memorandum; Enmons as quoted there; Malone as cited there] · [case-record compilation — Civil Rights Litigation Clearinghouse, for the Harpersville order's posture].

The door that stayed open — printed beside it, at the same size. Authorization defeated only the extortion theory. It did not answer the collector's financial interest. In Harper v. Professional Probation Services, Inc., 976 F.3d 1236 (11th Cir. 2020), the Eleventh Circuit held that PPS “had a direct pecuniary interest in maximizing the length of probation because PPS received its $40 monthly fee only as long as a probationer remained on probation” — and reversed dismissal of the due-process claim. Posture, printed plainly: that is a pleading-stage holding — the claim was adequately stated and remanded, not finally adjudicated after trial. Beside it, in one line each: Cain v. White, 937 F.3d 446 (5th Cir. 2019) and Caliste v. Cantrell, 937 F.3d 525 (5th Cir. 2019) — judges' financial interest in the fines and fees their courts generated violated due process. And Bearden's ability-to-pay rule (The Legal Frame, above): revocation for nonpayment without an inquiry into the reasons for failure to pay is “contrary to the fundamental fairness required by the Fourteenth Amendment” — a protection that applies regardless of the fee's authorization.

Source types: [case reports of the opinion — Fines and Fees Justice Center and Criminal Legal News, quoting Harper] · [case text and summaries — Cain; Caliste; Bearden, 461 U.S. 660 (1983)].

Against the grain, at the same size. In Glover v. State, 296 Ga. 315 (2014), the Georgia Supreme Court upheld private probation against due-process and debtors'-prison challenge — the system was not ended there. And the documented outcome of the McNeil litigation itself, stated factually: the same case that dismissed the extortion theory produced a preliminary injunction — based on a substantive right against wealth-based detention — affirmed by the Sixth Circuit (No. 19-5262, Dec. 23, 2019), and a $2 million Giles County settlement, approved February 1, 2022, under which the county agreed to end the use of private probation companies there. The extortion lane closed; the due-process lane is the one the record shows moving.

Source types: [case text — Glover, 296 Ga. 315 (2014)] · [case-record compilation — Civil Rights Litigation Clearinghouse, McNeil docket and settlement record]. Researched October 29, 2026.

WHAT THE LAW CHARGES FOR KNOWLEDGE

Where the law itself sets a price on access to information, the documented rule is a cost ceiling. The closest litigated cousin is PACER — the federal courts' per-page charge for public court records. In National Veterans Legal Services Program v. United States (Fed. Cir. Aug. 6, 2020), the Federal Circuit held, verbatim:

“We therefore hold that § 1913 Note limits PACER fees to the amount needed to cover expenses incurred in services providing public access to federal court electronic docketing information.”

The statute's own words, 28 U.S.C. § 1913 note (E-Government Act of 2002, § 205(e)): the Judicial Conference may “only to the extent necessary” prescribe fees, and fees collected are deposited “to reimburse expenses incurred in providing these services.” The fees charged above that ceiling were recoverable as an illegal exaction, and the case settled for $125,000,000 (final judgment, D.D.C. 2024; settlement affirmed, Fed. Cir. Mar. 20, 2026). One precision, printed because the record carries it: the narrower phrase “cost of dissemination” was the plaintiffs' formulation, and the court expressly did not adopt it — the adopted standard is the one quoted above, which also covers the CM/ECF and electronic bankruptcy noticing systems.

Source types: [case text — NVLSP, No. 19-1081 (Fed. Cir. 2020), full opinion; Final Judgment, Doc. 170 (D.D.C. 2024)] · [statute text — 28 U.S.C. § 1913 note, as quoted in the opinion].

The same ceiling, in one line each. FOIA (5 U.S.C. § 552(a)(4)(A)): fees are “limited to reasonable standard charges for document search, duplication, and review,” with a public-interest waiver where disclosure “is likely to contribute significantly to public understanding of the operations or activities of the government.” Texas Public Information Act (Tex. Gov't Code § 552.261(a)): the charge “shall be an amount that reasonably includes all costs related to reproducing the public information,” and § 552.267 requires waiver or reduction where providing the copy “primarily benefits the general public.” And the documented abuse finding, in the House Oversight Committee's own words (Freedom of Information Act is Broken, staff report, January 2016): agencies “create and follow FOIA policies that appear to be designed to deter requesters from pursuing requests and create barriers to accessing records” — the finding's documented example being the DEA's estimate of nearly $1.5 million for a single request. In every sustained challenge found in this record — PACER, FOIA, state records acts — the winning standard was the cost ceiling written into the authorizing statute; no case found held a record-access fee unlawful as such, and fees at cost were sustained.

Source types: [statute text — 5 U.S.C. § 552(a)(4)(A); Tex. Gov't Code §§ 552.261, 552.267] · [government report — House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform (2016); FOIA Advisory Committee / OGIS program review (Apr. 2016), for the DEA figure]. Researched October 29, 2026.

HIS READING — IN HIS WORDS

Labeled HIS POSITION — his stated reading, October 29, 2026, printed in his own words as his position, not as a finding of this page:

“So, their response is they're paying they're making people pay for the information that they we could freely have.”

“Just because somebody put it together and wants credit for it.”

“So I can give all this knowledge away for free because I have it.”

“So why do I have to pay the mad panel for it?”

The factual framing, from the documented record in this door: the panel's content is testimony and information that exists in freely available forms — victims' and survivors' accounts of what impaired driving cost them, of the kind shared publicly in MADD's own materials and elsewhere at no charge. What the fee purchases, on the documented record, is two things: (1) mission funds — revenue for MADD's work, which is MADD's own stated rationale for the fee, in its own words at The Anchor above — and (2) the certificate: the state-legible proof of attendance, issued on completion, that the court requires. No published waiver of the fee was found (NOT FOUND, at The Anchor above).

His follow-up question, in his own words:

“And if I am forced to take this panel and pay for it, is that not by definition extortion?”

The question has two answers, and this page prints both — the same two-answer frame as the extortion cross-reference in the Money Exhibit (The Incentive Ledger door, above).

By the shape of the definition, the elements align. Extortion, as defined at the Money Exhibit, is obtaining something of value by a threat the person cannot refuse, so that they pay to make it stop. Here: a thing of value changes hands — the fee. It is obtained under a threat the person cannot refuse — the mandate itself: attendance is court-ordered, and non-completion carries legal consequence. And the consent is manufactured by the requirement itself: the person “agrees” to pay because refusing costs more than paying. Measured element by element against the shape of the definition, the panel fee fits it.

By the law's definition, it is not extortion — for one differing element. Every extortion statute requires a wrongful threat. In Texas, extortion is prosecuted as theft by coercion (Penal Code § 31.03); federally, the Hobbs Act (18 U.S.C. § 1951) covers the same shape — and both turn on the threat being wrongful. A condition imposed under statutory authority is, by the law's own terms, lawful: the mandate is authorized, so the threat behind it is not wrongful in the law's sense, and the one element that would make this extortion is the element the law says is absent. The answer the law gives is no — and the reason it gives is the authorization, not the shape.

The panel's specific shape, stated factually. The fee here is not collected by the court. It is collected by a private organization — MADD — that the mandate channels people to, and that organization states, in its own words at The Anchor above, that the fee is mission funding. So the legal shield in this arrangement is the mandate's authority, not the recipient: what makes the collection lawful, on the law's own terms, is the court's order behind it — and the order's beneficiary, on the documented record, is a private organization's mission funds.

The difference is the badge, not the shape.

His closing line, in his own words:

“Again, if the goal is rehabilitation which they claim it to be why not offer all information free of charge. It's the only logical answer”

The open challenge, in his own words — standing on this record:

“If this is not the goal they are free to explain what the goal is and why they feel it is legal. and should be accepted. when we already know it's not acceptable.”

His second standing challenge, in his own words:

“And explain how adding more debt onto people who already can't pay it benefits them.”

The record entered in this door gives the stated rationales for such charges — accountability, deterrence, and recoupment of costs — and the documented outcomes for people who cannot pay: Bearden's rule that punishing nonpayment without an inquiry into ability is contrary to the fundamental fairness required by the Fourteenth Amendment; the Texas finding that the more intervention fees a person owed, the more likely revocation became; and collection costs of $0.41 or more for every $1 collected — and it contains no documented explanation, in any source entered, of a benefit to the person who owes.

The invitation stands open on this record. Any defender of the fee system may state the goal, the legal basis, and why it should be accepted — and a documented answer will be entered here, at the same size as this challenge. The Seeing Question's door line stands with it:

“Just because something has been accepted does not make it acceptable.”

His coda — his position, in his own words:

“Also let it be known that just because it has been done this way in the past if it does not follow the law it should not be accepted.”

The documented shelf for that statement — the record on longevity and legality, in neutral terms, both sides at the same size:

  • The T.J. Hooper, 60 F.2d 737, 740 (2d Cir. 1932) (Hand, J.), corrected verbatim: “there are precautions so imperative that even their universal disregard will not excuse their omission.” Precision the record carries: Hand found no general custom existed (“some had them, some did not”), and the case is tort and admiralty law — its transfer to public law is by analogy.
  • Desuetude — rejected. District of Columbia v. John R. Thompson Co., 346 U.S. 100, 113–114 (1953): “The failure of the executive branch to enforce a law does not result in its modification or repeal” — roughly eighty years of non-enforcement did not repeal the statutes before the Court.
  • Russman v. Luckett, 391 S.W.2d 694 (Ky.) — seventy-five years of officials violating a constitutional command did not abrogate it: “Our attention is called to no authority which would recognize an implied repeal of a constitutional provision because of its continued violation by public officials. The suggestion is appalling.”
  • COUNTERWEIGHT — LABELED, AT THE SAME SIZE. In several modern doctrines, history and tradition is the operative test: New York State Rifle & Pistol Ass'n v. Bruen, 597 U.S. 1 (2022) — a regulation must be “consistent with the Nation's historical tradition of firearm regulation”; District of Columbia v. Heller, 554 U.S. at 626–627 — “longstanding prohibitions” are “presumptively lawful”; American Legion v. American Humanist Ass'n, 588 U.S. 29 (2019) — “The passage of time gives rise to a strong presumption of constitutionality”; NLRB v. Noel Canning, 573 U.S. 513 (2014) — “long settled and established practice is a consideration of great weight.” Bruen's own double edge, documented: New York's century-old proper-cause licensing regime still lost, for want of a deeper national tradition — local age alone was not enough.

Source types: [case text — The T.J. Hooper; Thompson; Russman; Bruen; Heller; American Legion; Noel Canning, as researched October 29, 2026].

His standing question, in his own words — printed as a question, and left open as a question:

“And if they choose to not follow the law then should they be able to dictate the law?”

Two shelves of this page stand beside this coda. The Seeing Question layer carries the doctrine's legal anchor: Judge Learned Hand in The T.J. Hooper — custom is evidence, never a defense — “a whole calling may have unduly lagged.” And the question's evidence shelf is this page's own Authority's Own Record door, where the documented record of the authorities themselves stands.

The question's documented evidence — the near-zero trial-level doctrines, the mass-dismissal record, and the systems that fell — stands in this page's Authority's Own Record door and in Sections 13 and 17 of the case binder; it is pointed to here, not duplicated.

Door 6 · Breath or Blood
The chemical test — if there was one — has its own rules and its own paper trail.
LAW: DOCUMENTED · RECORD: AWAITING INFORMATION

THE LAW — LAW LAYER: DOCUMENTED (TEXAS FRAMEWORK, EACH CLAIM CITED)

The two definitions of intoxication. Texas Penal Code § 49.01(2) defines "intoxicated" two ways: (A) not having the normal use of mental or physical faculties by reason of the introduction of alcohol (or another covered substance) into the body; or (B) having an alcohol concentration of 0.08 or more. Section 49.01(1) defines "alcohol concentration" as grams of alcohol per 210 liters of breath, 100 milliliters of blood, or 67 milliliters of urine. Either definition, proved, satisfies the element — a number is one route, not the only route, and the absence of a number does not end the inquiry for either side.

Source: Texas Penal Code § 49.01(1)–(2).

The offense. Under Texas Penal Code § 49.04, a person commits driving while intoxicated if the person is intoxicated while operating a motor vehicle in a public place. A first offense is a Class B misdemeanor, subject to the enhancements and minimum terms the statute states.

Source: Texas Penal Code § 49.04.

The ≥0.15 enhancement and deferred adjudication. Under § 49.04(d), if an analysis of a specimen showed an alcohol concentration level of 0.15 or more at the time the analysis was performed, the offense is a Class A misdemeanor — punishable under § 12.21 by a fine not to exceed $4,000, confinement in jail not to exceed one year, or both. There is no separate Texas tier at 0.20 or 0.22. And under Texas Code of Criminal Procedure art. 42A.102(b)(1)(B), a judge may not grant deferred adjudication on a § 49.04 offense where the defendant's alcohol concentration was 0.15 or more — so at 0.15 or above, deferred adjudication is statutorily unavailable. (The full band frame, with the comparison set it governs, is at Side B, Door 1.)

Sources: Texas Penal Code §§ 49.04(d), 12.21; Texas Code of Criminal Procedure art. 42A.102 — texts read October 29, 2026 (FindLaw / Justia reproductions).

Implied consent and the ALR process. Under Texas Transportation Code chapter 724, a person arrested for a DWI offense is deemed to have consented to the taking of a breath or blood specimen. The officer must give the statutory warnings (the DIC-24) of the consequences of refusing or of providing a specimen at or above 0.08. The license consequence runs on a separate administrative track — the Administrative License Revocation (ALR) process before the State Office of Administrative Hearings — distinct from the criminal case: a hearing must be requested within the statutory window (15 days from service of the notice of suspension), and the Department of Public Safety bears the burden on the issues chapter 724 and chapter 524 assign to it.

Sources: Texas Transportation Code ch. 724 (implied consent), esp. §§ 724.011–724.015, 724.031–724.048; ch. 524 (ALR hearings).

Refusal and the warrant structure. A person may refuse a specimen. Refusal carries the ALR license consequence stated in the warnings, and the fact of refusal may be admitted at trial (Transportation Code § 724.061). A blood specimen taken without consent generally requires a search warrant (see Missouri v. McNeely, 569 U.S. 141 (2013); Birchfield v. North Dakota, 579 U.S. 438 (2016) — breath incident to arrest distinguished from blood), and chapter 724 itself provides for specimens taken under a warrant and for mandatory-draw circumstances the statute enumerates (e.g., § 724.012(b)). Which structure applies in a given case — consent, refusal, or warrant — is a fact of that case's record.

Sources: Texas Transportation Code §§ 724.012, 724.061; Missouri v. McNeely, 569 U.S. 141 (2013); Birchfield v. North Dakota, 579 U.S. 438 (2016).

The DPS breath-test program's own requirements. Texas breath testing runs under the Department of Public Safety's breath alcohol testing program (37 Texas Administrative Code ch. 19). Its requirements include: testing on an approved instrument (the Intoxilyzer 9000) by a certified operator, under a technical supervisor; a , during which the subject is continuously observed and takes nothing by mouth; a reference/calibration check within the program's tolerance run with the test sequence; and retention of the instrument's records — test slips, reference results, and maintenance and calibration logs — which can be examined and challenged.

Sources: 37 Texas Administrative Code ch. 19 (DPS Breath Alcohol Testing Program); Texas Transportation Code ch. 724.

WHAT THE RECORD SHOWS NOW

Awaiting Tristan's information entirely. Nothing about a chemical test is in the record yet — not whether one was offered, taken, consented to, or taken under a warrant, and not any number.
