Searched for, and not found or not verified to source. Printed as not found. Nothing in this list may be promoted into the layers above without a new verification pass.

  • A founding adherent statement for living constitutionalism (e.g., Justice Brennan's 1985 Georgetown address, often quoted for the approach) — searched for; the speech's text was not verified to a primary source in this research. NOT FILED as a quote. The Schools layers therefore present two schools in their adherents' words and flag the third's absence rather than filling it from secondary say-so.
  • A purposivist statement from Justice Breyer's Active Liberty (2005) verified to the book's text — not retrieved. Hart & Sacks (1958) carries the school's founding statement instead.
  • Jerome Frank's exact “worked out backward” formulation — not verified to the text of Law and the Modern Mind (1930) in this research. Only his documented endorsement of Hutcheson (via “Blinking on the Bench,” citing Frank at 111–12) is filed, in the Old Line layer.
  • The GPO hearing-volume PDF itself — not opened. The Roberts statement is verified through uscourts.gov's reproduction, which expressly sources its excerpt to that volume (pp. 55–56), plus independent scholarly pinpoint corroboration. Verification is strong; the method is stated because this door's rule is: show the door you actually walked through.
  • Scalia's A Matter of Interpretation in the original — under copyright, not read in full text. The p. 22 sentence is pinpoint-corroborated by multiple independent law reviews. The original-meaning sentence's wording is corroborated; its pinpoint page was not pinned.
  • Hart & Sacks's 1958 tentative edition, read directly — not read; every statement attributed to it is corroborated by multiple independent scholarly quotations.
  • Hutcheson's p. 285 sentence, read on the article's own page — verified through citing scholarship; the article PDF was located but the p. 285 passage itself was taken from the scholarly quotations of it. Pp. 278–279 were read in the article's text.
  • Scholarship naming “the law compels this result” as a responsibility-denial script — not separately retrieved. The underlying decide-then-justify sequence is documented (the Justification Sequence layer); the specific label is not.
  • The interior state, motive, or actual reasoning process of any living judge or official — CAN'T VERIFY. Not searched for as a fact, because it is not obtainable as one; nothing in this door attempts it, and any version of this door that implies it has left the record.
  • Whether any particular interpretation in any particular case was actually produced by the machinery in the Human Machinery layers — CAN'T VERIFY from this material. The studies measure groups given test problems. They cannot reach inside a single real decision, and this door does not use them to.
THE BENDABLE RULES — AND WHY
The documented exceptions that allow officers to do what would otherwise be unlawful — each entry labeled by its kind, each permission printed with its documented reason and its documented limit at the same size. Researched October 29, 2026, from the texts themselves.
LAW: DOCUMENTED FROM THE TEXTS · LIMITS PRINTED AT THE SAME SIZE

HOW THIS DOOR IS LABELED

Every entry is labeled by its kind: STATUTE / COURT OPINION / OFFICIAL GUIDELINE / AGENCY POLICY / REPORTED PRACTICE. A practice is never presented as law; a guideline is never presented as a statute; a plurality is never presented as a majority. Where a source states a reason, that reason is printed in the source's own words. Where no source was found, that is printed as not found.

1 · DECEPTION — MISPLACED TRUST

[COURT OPINION] Hoffa v. United States, 385 U.S. 293 (1966); Lewis v. United States, 385 U.S. 206 (1966).

“Neither this Court nor any member of it has ever expressed the view that the Fourth Amendment protects a wrongdoer's misplaced belief that a person to whom he voluntarily confides his wrongdoing will not reveal it.” — Hoffa, 385 U.S. at 302.

“[W]hen, as here, the home is converted into a commercial center to which outsiders are invited for purposes of transacting unlawful business, that business is entitled to no greater sanctity than if it were carried on in a store, a garage, a car, or on the street. A government agent, in the same manner as a private person, may accept an invitation to do business and may enter upon the premises for the very purposes contemplated by the occupant.” — Lewis, 385 U.S. at 211.

What it permits: an officer or informant may conceal that he is an officer, accept an invitation, and testify to — or record, under the statute at Layer 2 — what was voluntarily shown or said to him.

The limit in the same opinion: “During neither of his visits to petitioner's home did the agent see, hear, or take anything that was not contemplated, and in fact intended, by petitioner as a necessary part of his illegal business.” — Lewis, 385 U.S. at 210. An invitation obtained by ruse is an invitation for the purposes contemplated by the occupant; it is not a general search authorization.

Source: Hoffa v. United States, 385 U.S. 293 (1966); Lewis v. United States, 385 U.S. 206 (1966).

1 · DECEPTION — IMPERSONATION: A GATE, NOT A BAN

[OFFICIAL GUIDELINE] Attorney General's Guidelines on FBI Undercover Operations (rev. Nov. 13, 1992), §IV.C(2), published by the U.S. Department of Justice.

Printed as found: in the governing texts found, posing as an attorney, physician, clergyman, or member of the news media is not categorically barred. It is classified as a “sensitive circumstance” that removes field-level approval and requires FBI Headquarters review by the Criminal Undercover Operations Review Committee and approval by the Director or a designated Assistant Director. The gated categories include any operation where there is a reasonable expectation of:

“(i) Attendance at a meeting or participation in communications between any individual and his or her lawyer; (j) A significant risk that a third party will enter into a professional or confidential relationship with a person participating in an undercover operation who is acting as an attorney, physician, clergyman, or member of the news media; (k) A request to an attorney, physician, member of the clergy, or other person for information that would ordinarily be privileged or to a member of the news media concerning an individual with whom the news person is known to have a professional or confidential relationship…” — AG Guidelines, §IV.C(2)(i)–(k).

The same list gates investigations of public officials, religious and political organizations, and the news media themselves (§IV.C(2)(a)–(c)). False representations to conceal identity may be approved at field level, but “(this exemption does not include any statement under oath or the penalties of perjury)” — §IV.C(2)(g).

Negative, printed: no federal statute was found in this research that categorically bars officers from posing as clergy, press, or counsel. The documented federal control is the guideline gate above — an internal approval rule, not a statute, and it binds the FBI, not state officers.

Source: Attorney General's Guidelines on FBI Undercover Operations (rev. 11/13/92), U.S. Department of Justice: justice.gov — AG Guidelines on FBI Undercover Operations.

1 · DECEPTION — THE FALSE-AUTHORITY LIMIT ON CONSENT

[COURT OPINION] Bumper v. North Carolina, 391 U.S. 543 (1968).

Deception about identity is permitted above; deception about legal authority destroys the consent it produces. Officers told a homeowner they had a search warrant; she let them in. The Court held there was no consent:

“When a law enforcement officer claims authority to search a home under a warrant, he announces in effect that the occupant has no right to resist the search. The situation is instinct with coercion — albeit colorably lawful coercion. Where there is coercion there cannot be consent.” — Bumper, 391 U.S. at 550.

The government's burden of proving voluntary consent “cannot be discharged by showing no more than acquiescence to a claim of lawful authority” — id. at 548–49.

2 · THE CARVE-OUTS — ONE-PARTY-CONSENT RECORDING

[STATUTE] 18 U.S.C. § 2511(2)(c).

“It shall not be unlawful under this chapter for a person acting under color of law to intercept a wire, oral, or electronic communication, where such person is a party to the communication or one of the parties to the communication has given prior consent to such interception.” — 18 U.S.C. § 2511(2)(c).

What it permits: an officer (or an informant working with officers) who is a party to a conversation, or who has one party's prior consent, may record it with no prior judicial approval — the U.S. Attorneys' Manual states: “Under this provision, no prior judicial approval of the intercept is required” (DOJ, Criminal Resource Manual §1054).

The stated why, from the opinions: “Neither the Constitution nor any Act of Congress requires that official approval be secured before conversations are overheard or recorded by Government agents with the consent of one of the conversants.” — United States v. Caceres, 440 U.S. 741, 744 (1979). “We think the risk that petitioner took in offering a bribe to [the agent] fairly included the risk that the offer would be accurately reproduced in court, whether by faultless memory or mechanical recording.” — Lopez v. United States, 373 U.S. 427, 439 (1963). The statutory text itself states no purpose clause.

Boundary: the consent must come from a party to the communication; interception of a conversation no consenting party is part of is the wiretap regime of §2518, with a judge's order required.

Source: 18 U.S.C. § 2511(2)(c); DOJ Criminal Resource Manual §1054: justice.gov — Criminal Resource Manual §1054.

2 · THE CARVE-OUTS — TRASH PULLS, WITH THE DISSENT AT THE SAME SIZE

[COURT OPINION] California v. Greenwood, 486 U.S. 35 (1988) (6–2).

“It is common knowledge that plastic garbage bags left on or at the side of a public street are readily accessible to animals, children, scavengers, snoops, and other members of the public.” — Greenwood, 486 U.S. at 40.

“[H]aving deposited their garbage ‘in an area particularly suited for public inspection and, in a manner of speaking, public consumption, for the express purpose of having strangers take it’… respondents could have had no reasonable expectation of privacy in the inculpatory items that they discarded.” — id. at 41.

“[T]he police cannot reasonably be expected to avert their eyes from evidence of criminal activity that could have been observed by any member of the public.” — id. at 41.

The other side, at the same size: Justices Brennan and Marshall dissented — they would have held that the expectation of privacy in sealed trash is one society should recognize as reasonable. The majority rule governs federally; Greenwood's own text limits it to garbage “left for collection outside the curtilage of a home” — the opinion does not authorize entering the curtilage to seize trash kept there.

2 · THE CARVE-OUTS — PRETEXT STOPS, WITH THE PROBABLE-CAUSE FLOOR

[COURT OPINION] Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806 (1996) (unanimous).

“Subjective intentions play no role in ordinary, probable-cause Fourth Amendment analysis.” — Whren, 517 U.S. at 813.

“As a general matter, the decision to stop an automobile is reasonable where the police have probable cause to believe that a traffic violation has occurred.” — id. at 810.

The boundary inside the holding: the stop still requires objective probable cause of an actual violation — Whren is not permission to stop without one (the Court contrasted Delaware v. Prouse, where a stop on a whim failed). Intentionally discriminatory enforcement is challenged under the Equal Protection Clause, not the Fourth Amendment, on the Court's own routing.

2 · THE CARVE-OUTS — HOT PURSUIT AND EXIGENT CIRCUMSTANCES

[COURT OPINION] Warden v. Hayden, 387 U.S. 294 (1967); United States v. Santana, 427 U.S. 38 (1976).

“The Fourth Amendment does not require police officers to delay in the course of an investigation if to do so would gravely endanger their lives or the lives of others.” — Hayden, 387 U.S. at 298–99.

In Santana, a suspect may not defeat an arrest set in motion in a public place “by the expedient of escaping to a private place” — 427 U.S. at 42–43 — and there was a realistic expectation that “any delay would result in the destruction of evidence” — id. at 43.

Boundary: the doctrine is case-specific — gravity of the offense and genuine immediacy matter (e.g., Welsh v. Wisconsin, 466 U.S. 740, refusing home entry for a minor offense). It is an exigency doctrine, not a standing entry permit.

2 · THE CARVE-OUTS — THE VEHICLE EXCEPTION, WITH ITS OWN WARRANT LIMIT

[COURT OPINION] Carroll v. United States, 267 U.S. 132 (1925).

“[I]f the search and seizure without a warrant are made upon probable cause, that is, upon a belief, reasonably arising out of circumstances known to the seizing officer, that an automobile or other vehicle contains that which by law is subject to seizure and destruction, the search and seizure are valid.” — Carroll, 267 U.S. at 149.

“[The Fourth Amendment] has been construed, practically since the beginning of the Government, as recognizing a necessary difference between a search of a store, dwelling house or other structure in respect of which a proper official warrant readily may be obtained, and a search of a ship, motor boat, wagon or automobile, for contraband goods, where it is not practicable to secure a warrant because the vehicle can be quickly moved out of the locality or jurisdiction in which the warrant must be sought.” — id. at 153.

The limit in the same opinion: “where the securing of a warrant is reasonably practicable, it must be used” — id. at 156. Later cases added a second rationale (reduced expectation of privacy in vehicles, California v. Carney, 471 U.S. 386 (1985)) and kept probable cause as the floor.

2 · THE CARVE-OUTS — CONSENT WITHOUT A WARNING

[COURT OPINION] Schneckloth v. Bustamonte, 412 U.S. 218 (1973).

“[W]hether a consent to a search was in fact ‘voluntary’ or was the product of duress or coercion, express or implied, is a question of fact to be determined from the totality of all the circumstances. While knowledge of the right to refuse consent is one factor to be taken into account, the government need not establish such knowledge as the sine qua non of an effective consent.” — Schneckloth, 412 U.S. at 227; restated at 248–49.

What it permits: police may ask for consent without first advising of the right to refuse, and consent given without that knowledge can still be valid. Boundary: consent must in fact be voluntary — not “acquiescence to a claim of lawful authority” (the Bumper limit above) — and the prosecution bears the burden of proving voluntariness.

Source: Schneckloth v. Bustamonte, 412 U.S. 218: law.cornell.edu — Schneckloth opinion.

3 · DRIVING, TEXAS — THE STATUTORY PRIVILEGE AND ITS CONDITIONS

[STATUTE] Tex. Transp. Code §§ 546.001–546.005.

§ 546.001. Permissible conduct. In operating an authorized emergency vehicle the operator may: (1) park or stand, irrespective of another provision of this subtitle; (2) proceed past a red or stop signal or stop sign, after slowing as necessary for safe operation; (3) exceed a maximum speed limit, except as provided by an ordinance adopted under Section 545.365, as long as the operator does not endanger life or property; and (4) disregard a regulation governing the direction of movement or turning in specified directions.

§ 546.002. When conduct permissible. Section 546.001 applies only when the operator is: (1) responding to an emergency call; (2) pursuing an actual or suspected violator of the law; (3) responding to but not returning from a fire alarm; (4) directing or diverting traffic for public safety purposes; or (5) conducting a police escort.

§ 546.003. Signals. Except as provided by Section 546.004, the operator shall use, at the discretion of the operator in accordance with policies of the department or the local government that employs the operator, audible or visual signals that meet the pertinent requirements of Sections 547.305 and 547.702.

Source: Tex. Transp. Code ch. 546 (§§546.001–546.005): law.justia.com — Texas Transportation Code ch. 546.

3 · DRIVING, TEXAS — SILENT RUNNING, §546.004(c)

[STATUTE] Tex. Transp. Code § 546.004(c).

“A police officer may operate an authorized emergency vehicle for a law enforcement purpose without using the audible or visual signals required by Section 546.003 if the officer is: (1) responding to an emergency call or pursuing a suspected violator of the law with probable cause to believe that: (A) knowledge of the presence of the officer will cause the suspect to: (i) destroy or lose evidence of a suspected felony; (ii) end a suspected continuing felony before the officer has obtained sufficient evidence to establish grounds for arrest; or (iii) evade apprehension or identification of the suspect or the suspect's vehicle; or (B) because of traffic conditions on a multilaned roadway, vehicles moving in response to the audible or visual signals may: (i) increase the potential for a collision; or (ii) unreasonably extend the duration of the pursuit; or (2) complying with a written regulation relating to the use of audible or visible signals adopted by the local government that employs the officer or by the department.”

Inside three fences: a §546.002 purpose, a §546.004(c) predicate (probable cause of evidence-destruction / flight / traffic danger, or a written departmental regulation standing in for the predicate), and the §546.005 duty of care that never lifts.

Source: Tex. Transp. Code § 546.004: law.justia.com — § 546.004.

3 · DRIVING, TEXAS — THE DUTY OF CARE THAT NEVER LIFTS, §546.005

[STATUTE] Tex. Transp. Code § 546.005.

“This chapter does not relieve the operator of an authorized emergency vehicle from: (1) the duty to operate the vehicle with appropriate regard for the safety of all persons; or (2) the consequences of reckless disregard for the safety of others.”

Texas courts read §546.005 to impose “a duty to drive with due regard for others by avoiding negligent behavior, but it only imposes liability for reckless conduct” — City of Amarillo v. Martin, 971 S.W.2d 426, 431 (Tex. 1998). Civil immunity under the Texas Tort Claims Act is separately waived only where the emergency action either (a) was “not in compliance with the laws and ordinances applicable to emergency action,” or (b) absent such a law, was taken “with conscious indifference or reckless disregard for the safety of others” — Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 101.055(2).

3 · DRIVING, TEXAS — THE FEDERAL CONSTITUTIONAL FLOOR FOR PURSUITS

[COURT OPINION] County of Sacramento v. Lewis, 523 U.S. 833 (1998).

“[I]n such circumstances only a purpose to cause harm unrelated to the legitimate object of arrest will satisfy the element of arbitrary conduct shocking to the conscience, necessary for a due process violation.” — Lewis, 523 U.S. at 836.

“[H]igh-speed chases with no intent to harm suspects physically or to worsen their legal plight do not give rise to liability under the Fourteenth Amendment.” — id. at 854.

The Court rejected the lower “deliberate indifference” standard for chases because such a standard is workable “only when actual deliberation is practical” (id. at 851), and officers in a chase act “in haste, under pressure, and frequently without the luxury of a second chance” (id. at 853).

Boundary: Lewis sets the federal constitutional floor only. It does not repeal Texas's statutory duty of care (§546.005), departmental pursuit policies, or state tort liability under §101.055(2).

Source: County of Sacramento v. Lewis, 523 U.S. 833: law.cornell.edu — Lewis opinion.

3 · DRIVING, TEXAS — STATUTE / POLICY / PRACTICE, IN SEPARATE LANES

[STATUTE — privilege:] §§546.001–546.005 above. Outside a §546.002 purpose, the chapter grants nothing: an officer driving on routine patrol is under the same speed and signal laws as everyone else.

[AGENCY POLICY — discipline layer:] department general orders govern what the statute leaves to discretion. Documented example: Tyler Police Department General Order 07.200 (Emergency Response / Pursuit) restates §546.004 and then adds a requirement the statute does not contain — an officer running silent under a §546.004 exception “shall verbally inform dispatch via radio” (“Responding ‘Code 3’ without lights / without siren / without lights and siren”). Policy here is stricter than the statute, and violating it is a discipline matter, not itself a traffic offense.

Source: Tyler Police Department General Order 07.200: tylerpolice.com — General Order 07.200 (PDF).

[REPORTED PRACTICE — not law:] the Texas appellate record in cases like Green and Pineda documents officers in fact responding with lights but intermittent or no siren, and deciding signal use call-by-call. Courts tested that conduct against the statute and the department's policy — never against a freestanding “practice” right. Where conduct fits neither §546.004(c) nor a written regulation, the record found no source calling it permitted.

Honest negatives, printed at full size: no Texas statute was found authorizing non-emergency, non-pursuit speeding. No statutory pursuit speed cap exists — speed in pursuits is governed by the §546.001(3) condition (“does not endanger life or property”), §546.005's duty of care, and departmental policy, not by a number in the code.

4 · UNDERCOVER PARTICIPATION IN CRIME — “OTHERWISE ILLEGAL ACTIVITY”

[OFFICIAL GUIDELINE] Attorney General's Guidelines on FBI Undercover Operations (rev. Nov. 13, 1992), §IV.H, published by the U.S. Department of Justice. Flagged as what it is: these are internal executive guidelines for the FBI only. They are not a statute, they create no court-enforceable right, and they do not bind Texas or local officers. The 1992 text was verified verbatim from DOJ's own publication in this research; later revisions exist and were not re-verified line-by-line — see the negatives layer.

“Except when authorized pursuant to these Guidelines, no undercover employee shall engage in any activity that would constitute a violation of Federal, state, or local law if engaged in by a private person acting without authorization. For purposes of these Guidelines, such activity is referred to as otherwise illegal activity.” — §IV.H.

The stated justification test — approval requires that participation be justified:

“(a) to obtain information or evidence necessary for the success of the investigation and not reasonably available without participation in the otherwise illegal activity; (b) to establish or maintain credibility of a cover identity; or (c) to prevent death or serious bodily injury.” — §IV.H(1).

And: “The FBI shall take reasonable steps to minimize the participation of an undercover employee in any otherwise illegal activity.” — §IV.H(2).

The prohibitions — what remains barred even with approval:

“An undercover employee shall not (a) participate in any act of violence except in self-defense; (b) initiate or instigate any plan to commit criminal acts except in accordance with Part V (Avoidance of Entrapment) below; or (c) participate in conduct which would constitute unlawful investigative techniques (e.g., illegal wiretapping, illegal mail openings, breaking and entering, or trespass amounting to an illegal search).” — §IV.H(3).

The three approval tiers:

  • Special Agent in Charge (field level) may authorize: otherwise illegal activity that “is a misdemeanor or similar minor crime”; consensual monitoring “even if a crime under local law”; “the purchase of stolen or contraband goods”; “the delivery or sale of stolen property which cannot be traced to the rightful owner”; “the controlled delivery of drugs which will not enter commerce”; “the payment of bribes” not within the sensitive circumstances; and “the making of false representations to third parties in concealment of personal identity or the true ownership of a proprietary (but not any statement under oath or the penalties of perjury…).” — §IV.H(5)(a).
  • Assistant Director, after review by the Undercover Review Committee — required for participation in otherwise illegal activity “which is a felony or its equivalent under Federal, state, or local law.” — §IV.H(5)(b).
  • Director or Deputy Director, after review by the Undercover Review Committee — required for activity “which involves a significant risk of violence or physical injury.” — §IV.H(5)(c).

Emergency lane: if consultation is impossible and there is “an immediate and grave threat to life, physical safety, or property,” the employee may participate without prior approval but “does not take part in and makes every effort to prevent any act of violence,” with prompt reporting and possible retroactive authorization. — §IV.H(5)(d).

5 · STING ARCHITECTURE

[COURT OPINION] The constitutional frame is the Sorrells–Sherman–Jacobson line (quoted in Layer 6): the government may “merely afford opportunities or facilities for the commission of the offense” to a person already disposed; it may not originate the criminal design in an innocent mind.

[COURT OPINION] United States v. Russell, 411 U.S. 423 (1973): a government agent supplied an essential, hard-to-obtain precursor chemical to a defendant already running a drug lab — conviction upheld, because the defendant was predisposed; the government's supplying an ingredient did not itself defeat the prosecution.

[COURT OPINION] Controlled delivery — Illinois v. Andreas, 463 U.S. 765 (1983). Customs lawfully opened a container, found marijuana, resealed it, and agents posing as deliverymen delivered it; after arrest, the container was reopened without a warrant. Upheld:

“No protected privacy interest remains in contraband in a container once government officers lawfully (as here) have opened that container and identified its contents as illegal. The simple act of resealing the container to enable the police to make a controlled delivery does not operate to revive or restore the lawfully invaded privacy rights…” — Andreas, 463 U.S. at 771. The standard: “absent a substantial likelihood that the contents have been changed, there is no legitimate expectation of privacy in the contents of a container previously opened under lawful authority” — id. at 773.

[OFFICIAL GUIDELINE] The AG Guidelines place controlled deliveries of drugs “which will not enter commerce,” purchases of contraband, delivery/sale of untraceable stolen property, and bribe payments inside the SAC-authorizable items (§IV.H(5)(a)); but “providing goods or services which are essential to the commission of a crime, which goods and services are reasonably unavailable to a subject of the investigation except from the Government” is a sensitive circumstance requiring headquarters review (§IV.C(2)(f)).

Reverse stings (government as seller): NOT FOUND as a separate doctrine. No separate Supreme Court doctrine was found; they stand or fall under the same Sorrells/Jacobson frame (opportunity vs. implantation) plus the guideline tiers. No distinct “reverse sting” permission text is claimed.

Source: Illinois v. Andreas, 463 U.S. 765: law.cornell.edu — Andreas opinion.

6 · THE LIMITS — ENTRAPMENT, WHERE PERMISSION ENDS

[COURT OPINION] Sorrells v. United States, 287 U.S. 435 (1932); Sherman v. United States, 356 U.S. 369 (1958); Jacobson v. United States, 503 U.S. 540 (1992).

“It is well settled that the fact that officers or employees of the Government merely afford opportunities or facilities for the commission of the offense does not defeat the prosecution. Artifice and stratagem may be employed to catch those engaged in criminal enterprises.” — Sorrells, 287 U.S. at 441, quoted and reaffirmed in Jacobson, 503 U.S. at 548.

“In their zeal to enforce the law, however, Government agents may not originate a criminal design, implant in an innocent person's mind the disposition to commit a criminal act, and then induce commission of the crime so that the Government may prosecute.” — Jacobson, 503 U.S. at 548–49.

“Law enforcement officials go too far when they ‘implant in the mind of an innocent person the disposition to commit the alleged offense and induce its commission in order that they may prosecute.’” — Jacobson, 503 U.S. at 553, quoting Sorrells, 287 U.S. at 442.

“To determine whether entrapment has been established, a line must be drawn between the trap for the unwary innocent and the trap for the unwary criminal.” — Sherman, 356 U.S. at 372.

“[T]he Government [may not] pla[y] on the weaknesses of an innocent party and beguil[e] him into committing crimes which he otherwise would not have attempted.” — Sherman, 356 U.S. at 376.

Jacobson applied the limit: after 2½ years of government mailings, the Court held “rational jurors could not say beyond a reasonable doubt that petitioner possessed the requisite predisposition prior to the Government's investigation” — 503 U.S. at 553. Predisposition must exist before and independent of the government's persuasion.

[OFFICIAL GUIDELINE] The FBI's own guidelines internalize the same limit: “Entrapment must be scrupulously avoided” (AG Guidelines, Part V.A), with inducement standards the guidelines concede are “not required by law” but are “imposed to ensure that the Government does not offer inducements to engage in crime to persons who are not predisposed to do so” (Part V.C).

Source: Jacobson v. United States, 503 U.S. 540: law.cornell.edu — Jacobson opinion.

6 · THE LIMITS — OUTRAGEOUS GOVERNMENT CONDUCT, AS IT ACTUALLY STANDS

[COURT OPINION] United States v. Russell, 411 U.S. 423 (1973); Hampton v. United States, 425 U.S. 484 (1976) (plurality).

“[W]e may some day be presented with a situation in which the conduct of law enforcement agents is so outrageous that due process principles would absolutely bar the government from invoking judicial processes to obtain a conviction.” — Russell, 411 U.S. at 431–32.

Three years later, in Hampton, a three-justice plurality would have closed that door for any predisposed defendant:

“If the police engage in illegal activity in concert with a defendant beyond the scope of their duties the remedy lies, not in freeing the equally culpable defendant, but in prosecuting the police under the applicable provisions of state or federal law.” — Hampton, 425 U.S. at 490 (plurality opinion).

Both sides at the same size, because the case produced no majority rule: Justice Powell, joined by Justice Blackmun, concurred only in the result and refused to foreclose the defense, writing that he was “unwilling to join the plurality in concluding that, no matter what the circumstances, neither due process principles nor our supervisory power could support a bar to conviction in any case where the Government is able to prove predisposition” (425 U.S. at 495). Three justices dissented and would have barred the conviction. The documented state of the doctrine is: five justices in Hampton left the outrageous-conduct door theoretically open; the plurality would have shut it for the predisposed; and no Supreme Court decision found in this research has ever actually dismissed a prosecution on that ground.

6 · THE LIMITS — FABRICATED DOCUMENTS: A SPLIT, PRINTED UNRESOLVED

[COURT OPINION — state] State v. Cayward, 552 So. 2d 971 (Fla. Dist. Ct. App. 1989).

Police fabricated two scientific reports on official-looking stationery falsely showing the suspect's semen on a victim's clothing, showed them to him in interrogation, and obtained a confession. The Florida court suppressed it:

“[W]e think the manufacturing of false documents by police officials offends our traditional notions of due process of law under both the federal and state constitutions.” — Cayward, 552 So. 2d at 974.

The manufacture and use of false documents by police to induce a confession “has no place in our criminal justice system.” — id. at 974.

The court's stated reasoning: unlike oral misrepresentations, manufactured documents carry “the facial appearance of authenticity,” and tolerating them would erode public confidence in genuinely official documents.

The other side, at the same size: the Nevada Supreme Court considered the same practice (a falsified lab report in Sheriff v. Bessey, 914 P.2d 618 (Nev. 1996)) and refused the bright line: “We disagree with the Florida court's standard and rationale,” holding instead that a fabricated document is one factor in the totality-of-the-circumstances voluntariness test — and on its facts, the statements were voluntary. Other courts (e.g., South Carolina in State v. Von Dohlen) have likewise declined Cayward's per-se rule. The honest statement of the law: spoken deception in interrogation is broadly permitted (see the page's existing deception material); fabricated documents are barred per se in Florida's rule, treated as a voluntariness factor elsewhere; no U.S. Supreme Court decision found in this research resolves the split. Florida and Nevada openly disagree, and this door prints the disagreement unresolved.

Source: State v. Cayward, 552 So. 2d 971, and Sheriff v. Bessey, 914 P.2d 618 (Nev.): law.justia.com — Bessey.

6 · THE LIMITS — TEXAS'S OWN LIMIT: CUSTODIAL STATEMENTS MUST BE RECORDED

[STATUTE] Tex. Code Crim. P. art. 38.22, § 3.

“(a) No oral or sign language statement of an accused made as a result of custodial interrogation shall be admissible against the accused in a criminal proceeding unless: (1) an electronic recording, which may include motion picture, videotape, or other visual recording, is made of the statement; (2) prior to the statement but during the recording the accused is given the warning in Subsection (a) of Section 2 above and the accused knowingly, intelligently, and voluntarily waives any rights set out in the warning; (3) the recording device was capable of making an accurate recording, the operator was competent, and the recording is accurate and has not been altered; (4) all voices on the recording are identified; and (5) not later than the 20th day before the date of the proceeding, the attorney representing the defendant is provided with a true, complete, and accurate copy of all recordings of the defendant made under this article.”

Subsection (b) requires every such recording to be preserved until the conviction is final and appeals are exhausted. Subsection (e) commands that “[t]he courts of this state shall strictly construe Subsection (a).” Subsection (c) prints the statute's own exception (statements containing facts found true that conduce to establish guilt, such as locating secreted property), and § 5 preserves statements that do not stem from custodial interrogation at all. Texas appellate courts describe this provision as state-law protection greater than the federal constitutional floor. Whatever deception federal doctrine tolerates in the interrogation room, an unrecorded custodial oral statement is unusable in a Texas courtroom.

Source: Tex. Code Crim. P. art. 38.22 §3, text as quoted in State v. Simmons (Tex. App.—Beaumont 2020): law.justia.com — State v. Simmons.

6 · THE LIMITS — CROSS-REFERENCES, NOT DUPLICATED

Lying under oath and to the court — no permission exists. The page's existing record elsewhere holds this material and it is not duplicated here: perjury is a crime for officers as for anyone; Franks v. Delaware governs false statements in warrant affidavits; Napue v. Illinois bars knowing use of false testimony. One corroborating line from this research, [OFFICIAL GUIDELINE]: the AG Guidelines' list of field-authorizable deceptions expressly excludes “any statement under oath or the penalties of perjury” (§IV.C(2)(g)) — even inside the system that authorizes undercover law-breaking, sworn lying is carved out of the lowest approval tier.

Juvenile-deception reforms: cross-referenced to the page's existing deception material — more than ten states now restrict deceptive interrogation of juveniles; Texas is not among them. Not re-researched or duplicated here.

Interrogation deception generally: Frazier v. Cupp, 394 U.S. 731, 739 (1969), is held in the page's existing record; its reasoning is quoted once in Layer 7 because this door was asked for the why in the sources' own words.

7 · THE WHY, IN THEIR OWN WORDS

Each rationale below is stated by the source itself. None is imputed.

  • Why deception at all — because the crimes are committed in secret, with victims who cannot or do not protest [COURT OPINION]: “Were we to hold the deceptions of the agent in this case constitutionally prohibited, we would come near to a rule that the use of undercover agents in any manner is virtually unconstitutional per se. Such a rule would, for example, severely hamper the Government in ferreting out those organized criminal activities that are characterized by covert dealings with victims who either cannot or do not protest. A prime example is provided by the narcotics traffic.” — Lewis, 385 U.S. at 210.
  • Why the suspect bears the risk of the betrayal [COURT OPINION]: “Neither this Court nor any member of it has ever expressed the view that the Fourth Amendment protects a wrongdoer's misplaced belief that a person to whom he voluntarily confides his wrongdoing will not reveal it.” — Hoffa, 385 U.S. at 302.
  • Why the risk includes accurate reproduction [COURT OPINION]: “We think the risk that petitioner took in offering a bribe to [the agent] fairly included the risk that the offer would be accurately reproduced in court, whether by faultless memory or mechanical recording.” — Lopez, 373 U.S. at 439.
  • Why stratagem is a legitimate tool against people already engaged in crime [COURT OPINION]: “Artifice and stratagem may be employed to catch those engaged in criminal enterprises.” — Sorrells, 287 U.S. at 441.
  • Why undercover enforcement is stated as settled [COURT OPINION]: “There can be no dispute that the Government may use undercover agents to enforce the law.” — Jacobson, 503 U.S. at 548.
  • Why the undercover technique specifically — the government's own guideline [OFFICIAL GUIDELINE]: “The use of the undercover technique, including proprietary business entities, is essential to the detection, prevention, and prosecution of white collar crimes, public corruption, terrorism, organized crime, offenses involving controlled substances, and other priority areas of investigation. However, these techniques inherently involve an element of deception and may require cooperation with persons whose motivation and conduct are open to question, and so should be carefully considered and monitored.” — AG Guidelines, §I.
  • Why an agent may join in crime — only three reasons are accepted [OFFICIAL GUIDELINE]: participation must be justified “(a) to obtain information or evidence necessary for the success of the investigation and not reasonably available without participation in the otherwise illegal activity; (b) to establish or maintain credibility of a cover identity; or (c) to prevent death or serious bodily injury.” — AG Guidelines, §IV.H(1).
  • Why a deceptive interrogation can still yield a “voluntary” confession — the Court's reasoning in Frazier v. Cupp, labeled as the Court's [COURT OPINION]: “The fact that the police misrepresented the statements that Rawls had made is, while relevant, insufficient in our view to make this otherwise voluntary confession inadmissible. These cases must be decided by viewing the ‘totality of the circumstances’…” — Frazier v. Cupp, 394 U.S. 731, 739 (1969). In the Court's framework, deception is one circumstance weighed among others; it does not, by itself, overpower the will.
  • Why warrantless entries in emergencies [COURT OPINION]: “The Fourth Amendment does not require police officers to delay in the course of an investigation if to do so would gravely endanger their lives or the lives of others.” — Hayden, 387 U.S. at 298–99.
  • Why cars are treated differently from houses [COURT OPINION]: “[I]t is not practicable to secure a warrant because the vehicle can be quickly moved out of the locality or jurisdiction in which the warrant must be sought.” — Carroll, 267 U.S. at 153.
  • Why Texas holds emergency drivers to recklessness, not negligence — the Texas Supreme Court's stated policy reason [COURT OPINION]: “[H]aving liability predicated on reckless conduct better serves the public interest in minimizing emergency response delays. The possibility of incurring civil liability for what amounts to a mere failure of judgment could deter emergency personnel from acting decisively and taking calculated risks in order to save life or property or to apprehend miscreants. The ‘reckless disregard’ test… is better suited to the legislative goal of encouraging emergency personnel to act swiftly and resolutely while at the same time protecting the public's safety to the extent practicable.” — City of Amarillo v. Martin, 971 S.W.2d 426, 430 (Tex. 1998), quoting Saarinen v. Kerr.
  • Why the federal pursuit standard is intent-to-harm [COURT OPINION]: “deliberate indifference” is workable “only when actual deliberation is practical” (Lewis, 523 U.S. at 851); officers in a chase decide “in haste, under pressure, and frequently without the luxury of a second chance” (id. at 853).

THE HEADLINE NEGATIVE, AT FULL SIZE: the claim that enforcement would be IMPOSSIBLE without these powers appears in NO source found in this research — in those words or any equivalent holding. The sources say “severely hamper” (Lewis) and “essential” (AG Guidelines §I). This door prints their words, not the stronger claim.

WHAT COULD NOT BE VERIFIED — THE NEGATIVES, PRINTED

Searched for, and not found. Printed as not found. Nothing in this list may be promoted into the layers above without a new verification pass.

  • A verbatim “officers need not announce themselves or enforcement is impossible” holding — not found in any opinion, statute, or guideline searched.
  • A federal statute categorically barring officers from posing as clergy, press, or counsel — not found. The documented federal control is the AG Guidelines' “sensitive circumstances” gate (Layer 1), which escalates approval rather than banning the impersonation.
  • Texas-level impersonation gates or undercover-conduct statutes for state/local officers comparable to the FBI guidelines — not found in this research. The FBI guidelines bind the FBI; what written rules bind a Texas officer's undercover deception, beyond the case law in Layers 1–2 and art. 38.22, remains an open research item, not a claim.
  • Current (post-1992) AG Undercover Guidelines, full text — the 1992 revision was verified verbatim from DOJ's own publication; later revisions (2002 and after) are known to exist and the DOJ OIG's 2005 report describes the same committee/Group I structure in operation, but the current text was not re-verified line-by-line in this research. Nothing in Layer 4 should be quoted as “the current guideline” without that check. Secondary source: DOJ Office of the Inspector General, The FBI's Compliance with the Attorney General's Investigative Guidelines (2005), ch. 7: oig.justice.gov — OIG report, ch. 7.
  • A Texas statute authorizing emergency-style driving outside the §546.002 purposes (e.g., routine-patrol or “catch-up” speeding with no emergency call and no pursuit of a suspected violator) — not found; the chapter's text grants the privileges only inside those purposes.
  • A statutory speed cap for Texas pursuits — not found. Speed limits in pursuits are governed by the §546.001(3) condition, §546.005's duty of care, and departmental policy — not by a number in the code.
  • A distinct Supreme Court doctrine for “reverse stings” — not found; the practice is analyzed under the general Sorrells/Jacobson opportunity-vs.-implantation frame (Layer 5).
  • A U.S. Supreme Court resolution of the fabricated-documents split (Cayward vs. Bessey) — not found; the split among state courts stands as documented in Layer 6.
  • A Supreme Court decision ever sustaining an outrageous-government-conduct dismissal — not found (Layer 6).

8 · THE LIMIT RAIL — WHAT THIS DOOR CAN AND CANNOT HONESTLY CLAIM

This door can honestly claim that American law, in texts anyone can read, gives officers a documented set of exceptions to rules that bind everyone else: they may lie about who they are (Hoffa, Lewis), record with one party's consent and no judge (§2511(2)(c)), take curbside trash (Greenwood), stop for a traffic violation in order to look for something else (Whren), enter a home without a warrant when delay would endanger life or lose evidence (Hayden, Santana), search a car on probable cause alone (Carroll), ask consent without stating that refusal is permitted (Schneckloth), and — under written Texas law — speed, run signals, and run silent without lights or siren inside defined conditions (Tex. Transp. Code §§546.001–546.004), while federal undercover guidelines let agents participate in real crimes up through felonies with ascending written approvals (AG Guidelines §IV.H). It can also honestly claim that every one of those permissions arrives with its limit printed in the same documents — probable cause floors, purpose limits, a duty of care that never lifts, an entrapment line at implanting crime in an innocent mind, a Texas statute that makes unrecorded custodial statements unusable, and approval gates around the most sensitive deceptions — and that the whys on record are the sources' own: crime is covert, its victims often cannot protest, cars move, delay endangers, and a suspect assumes the risk of the confidant he chose.

What this door cannot honestly claim is just as important: it cannot call departmental policy or street practice “the law”; it cannot widen any permission one inch past its text (there is no statutory blessing for non-emergency speeding, no categorical federal ban on posing as clergy or press — only an approval gate — and no Supreme Court case ever dismissing a prosecution for outrageous conduct); it cannot present the fabricated-documents question as settled when Florida and Nevada openly split; and it cannot supply the one rationale people most often assert — that enforcement would be impossible without these powers — because no source found says it: the sources say enforcement would be hampered, and that undercover work is essential to detecting crimes that hide. This door documents the bendable rules and their stated reasons at full size, the limits at the same size, and the gaps as gaps.

Door 7 · The Evidence Clock
The video that proves Doors 3, 5, and 8 is on a deletion schedule.
OPEN DOOR

THE LAW

Dashcam, bodycam, and jail/intake video exist or existed. Video is kept on retention schedules, not forever — jail video is commonly kept only 30 to 90 days — and then it is deleted in the ordinary course. A through counsel is the step that stops the clock.

THE RECORDINGS RULE — EVERY RECORDING ON THIS PAGE

This rule governs every recording named on this page — dash camera, body camera, jail and intake video, and any other recording. Any camera footage or recording submitted in a case can be public record. Each recording carries one of three states, printed at full size and never blurred together:

  • PRODUCED — in the record, checked and quoted.
  • EXISTS BUT NOT PRODUCED — the gap printed as a gap, with the standing question beside it: "What is the reason?" A reason actually stated is recorded as stated, with its source. Where no reason is stated, the entry reads REASON NOT STATED — never guessed behind.
  • NEVER EXISTED — stated as its own fact where the record proves it: no camera fitted, no system in the room.

The retention clock above stays. A recording that lapses unrequested is a gap somebody chose — and the record says which kind of gap it is, by the state printed beside it.

Framing note — his guardrail over all of today's statements, stated by him: nothing in these statements admits, hints at, concedes, or denies anything about the night of the stop — not the stop as alleged, and not any blood figure; his figure is stated nowhere in this record, and he is not denying anything either. A practical rider from this record's own evidence work: recordings like that live on a clock (Door 7). His opinion on the clock the recordings run on — HIS OPINION:

"There's a reason in my opinion that their videos run on a clock and they use it in their favor all the time."

The thin paper cuts both ways — his argument: if the morning's paper proof cannot prove somebody wasn't there, it cannot prove somebody was there either. In his words:

"If they can't prove that somebody wasn't there, then they can't prove that somebody was there. And a piece of paper means nothing — even a signature can be forged. So make it make sense."

On keeping the video — his point, with the honest limits printed beside it at the same size:

"Because we all know they could keep all this video and have access to it anytime they wanted. Nothing gets completely erased, does it?"

The capability he points at is real: nothing in the technology forces video to die on a short schedule — storage is cheap, and a retention period is a policy choice, written by the institution itself. The honest limit, printed beside it: once a scheduled overwrite actually runs on a loop-recording system, that footage is genuinely gone; and on modern evidence platforms, exports and deletions are themselves logged — so even a deletion leaves a record of itself. Erased and unaccounted-for are not the same thing. What stands either way: the deadline is set by the same institution the deadline protects, and it is set short — which is why the counter-move is a preservation request through counsel before the clock runs (Door 7).

After the erasure — his question, and the record's answer:

On the coercion step: the law itself refuses to treat pressured agreement as free agreement — voluntariness is its own doctrine (§18) — and an acknowledgment signed standing in a hallway, under a warrant's shadow, before the evidence was looked at, is exactly the kind of agreement that doctrine exists for.

On changing his mind — his statement:

He is — and this record models it: he corrected himself twice on October 30, 2026 (the attorney's name; the reach of "had not been looked at"), and both corrections made the record stronger, not weaker. Beside it, only what is documented: the machine has forms for the answers given under pressure, in hallways, on the spot. It has no form for the answer a man gives after he has had time to think.

Section 18 · Coercion, Extortion, and the Blank Price
The two words as the law uses them — and the price no statute names.
DOCUMENTED · HIS OPINION LABELED

THE TWO WORDS, AS THE LAW USES THEM

Coercion is the pressure itself. Extortion is coercion + a taking + no claim of right. The difference is the badge, not the shape — the element-by-element cross-reference stands in the Incentive Ledger door (The Money Exhibit), and §17's Question Asked in Court carries the litigated version: authorization defeated the extortion theory in McNeil; no court has ever held mandated-fee collection to be extortion.

HOW THE SYSTEM USES COERCION AGAINST PEOPLE — DOCUMENTED

  • Plea pressure — Bordenkircher v. Hayes, 434 U.S. 357 (1978): a prosecutor may present a defendant with the choice of pleading to a lesser charge or facing a more serious one the evidence supports — the pressure is documented, and lawful, in the Supreme Court's own holding.
  • The fee stack: the documented stack stands in the Incentive Ledger door — with Harper's documented collector incentive beside it: the private probation company "had a direct pecuniary interest in maximizing the length of probation because [it] received its $40 monthly fee only as long as a probationer remained on probation" (11th Cir. 2020, pleading-stage holding).
  • The repealed Driver Responsibility Program: the Legislature's own documented cycle — annual surcharges, suspension for nonpayment, new charges — repealed in 2019 and restructured into new fines, not abolished (Knowledge Trail, Incentive Ledger door).
  • The code's asymmetry: pressure downward is administration; pressure upward is a crime. A private person who takes value by threat commits theft by coercion (Penal Code § 31.03); the identical shape, authorized, is process.
  • Evans — the limit inside the asymmetry: an official taking beyond the authorization commits extortion, badge or not. The authorization is the whole shield, and it covers only what it authorizes.

WHO DICTATES WHAT THE LAW MAY TAKE — AND THE BLANK AT THE CENTER

Authorizations are dictated by the Legislature and the courts, and they change day to day — the Knowledge Trail (Incentive Ledger door) is the dated record of what was known, when, and what changed. At the center of this door stands the blank:

The Texas interlock statutes name no amount and no cap. The price a person is ordered to pay for the mandated device is set by a private company's price list. Tennessee caps theirs by rule. Texas's one printed relief is a narrow, discretionary, deferred-track-only discount. The documented market structure stands in §17 (Shelf Two): priced per day, per month, per device — revenue rising with the number of people mandated and the length of time they remain mandated, and with nothing else.

HIS STATEMENT — LABELED HIS OPINION, BESIDE THE FACTS BOTH WAYS AT THE SAME SIZE

His statement on this section stands in the binder (§18), labeled HIS OPINION — his reading, printed as his position, not as a finding of this page. His words on the blank price, given October 30, 2026, printed in full:

"No price set — they could literally charge anything, and as a person, as an American citizen, I am just forced to pay it or face jail time because I didn't pay these people money. Which is unlawful and illegal in my opinion."

The last sentence is printed as he labeled it himself: his opinion. The documented blank it points at stands in the layer above — the Texas interlock statutes name no amount and no cap — and the facts both ways stand beside it, at the same size:

  • Bearing his way: the revocation data and the fee-collection record — the more intervention fees a person owed, the more likely revocation became (Texas supervision study, §17); collection costs of $0.41 or more per $1 collected (Brennan Center, §17); a collector paid only while a person stays on probation (Harper).
  • The law's stated limits, at the same size: Bearden's rule — revocation for nonpayment without an inquiry into the reasons is "contrary to the fundamental fairness required by the Fourteenth Amendment" — and the hardship provisions as written (§ 42A.652(b); art. 42A.751(i), with its Texas narrowing printed in §17).

Whether those limits are honored at the window is a factual question — which is the fight, and it is written down here where counsel and anyone else can read it.

Researched and filed October 29–30, 2026 (binder §18). Sources as cited in the layers and in §§17–19 of this page.

Section 19 · Why a Known-Broken System Is Allowed to Run
The law's test is who authorized it — never whether it helps.
DOCUMENTED

THE LEGAL ANSWER, IN ONE LINE

The law's test is who authorized it and whose rights can be proven violated — never whether it helps. A system can be useless and lawful at once. The full doctrine stands in the Incentive Ledger door (The Knowledge Question): no statute makes "knowingly operating a flawed system" an offense by itself; the specific named pieces of it are offenses, each with its own elements.

THE GATES THAT PRODUCE THAT ANSWER

  • Intent requirements that demand proof of what a specific official knew — Farmer v. Brennan's subjective deliberate-indifference test; 18 U.S.C. § 242's willfulness (Screws); Texas Penal Code § 39.03's "knows is unlawful." (Knowledge Question layers, Incentive Ledger door.)
  • Qualified immunity's clearly-established gate — Harlow / Anderson: even proven knowledge fails a damages suit if no prior decision particularized the right on materially similar facts. Its printed limits stand beside it there: it does not apply to criminal cases, to municipalities, or to injunctions.
  • Monell's policy gate — the county is liable only where an official policy or custom is the "moving force" behind a constitutional violation; a failure to train counts only where it amounts to deliberate indifference (City of Canton).
  • Finality — where "too long, too costly" is not just an excuse but reasoning printed in the holdings themselves (Teague; McCleskey's floodgates). The age of a practice is not its vindication — the Seeing Question layer and The T.J. Hooper stand in the Incentive Ledger and §17 doors: custom is evidence, never a defense.

HIS OPINION — FILED IN HIS WORDS, WITH HIS OWN STANDARD ATTACHED

His opinion on this section stands in the binder (§19), filed in his words, with his own standard attached — "facts will show one way or the other." The documented flows stand at the same size: the probation fee that lasts only while a person stays on probation (Harper); the panel's mission funds (MADD's own words, §17); Ferguson's fines and fees rising from 12% to a budgeted 23% of the city's revenue (DOJ, 2015). Motive is printed as NOT DOCUMENTED — because it isn't, and this record doesn't invent it.

Entered as he put it: a question, addressed to whoever reads this record. One part of it is labeled precisely: that the person was set up to fail on purpose is HIS OPINION — intent is NOT DOCUMENTED, and this record does not print it as fact. The rest of the question needs no label: one side's choices are charged, priced, and signed for on paper, in this record and in the court's; the other side's choices — the blank price (§18), the setting that exchanged nothing (Door 9), the known flaws left running (the gates above) — had no ledger at all until this one. Now both sides have one.

THE JURISDICTION QUESTION — HIS QUESTION, AND THE LAW'S ANSWER AT THE SAME SIZE

"If a jurisdiction is knowingly breaking the law, can they even practice the law? Legally?"

"No matter how big or small that law is."

"No cut corners. Do it the right way."

The law's answer, printed plainly: legally, yes. A jurisdiction's courts keep their power even when the jurisdiction's own side breaks the law. Jurisdiction comes from constitutions and statutes, not from the officeholder's conduct, and no doctrine in the books — for a big law or a small one — dissolves a court's power because its own side broke the rules. That remedy does not exist.

What exists instead is narrower, and act-by-act: the law undoes the tainted act (evidence suppressed, convictions reversed); it can kill the specific case where the government's own conduct poisoned it (entrapment — the State may not originate the crime it prosecutes); it punishes the specific official, through the high intent gates in the layers above; and it enjoins the specific practice. Never the institution.

His principle has been spoken inside the law itself — once, famously, in dissent. Justice Brandeis, Olmstead v. United States, 277 U.S. 438 (1928), dissenting: "If the Government becomes a lawbreaker, it breeds contempt for law; it invites every man to become a law unto himself; it invites anarchy." A dissent is where his rule lives in their books: honored, quoted, and never adopted. His standard — no cut corners, do it the right way, no matter how big or small the law — is stricter than the law's rule for itself. That gap is documented here, at full size, with his question and the law's answer printed beside each other, neither one shrunk.

BEND, BREAK, OR IGNORE — HIS FRAME FOR THIS WHOLE RECORD

His frame, in his words: this all goes back into "what they are allowed — what rules they are allowed to bend and not break versus the people — or what rules they can ignore completely, and their justifications for it."

The record is built to that frame. The documented permissions — the rules officers and officials may bend, each with its stated reason and its limit printed at the same size — stand in the Bendable Rules door. The gates that let a known-broken practice keep running stand in the layers above. And the justifications are printed beside each permission and each gate, so neither the permission nor its justification ever stands alone for a reader to mistake for the whole picture.

THE STRONG-ARM QUESTION — PUT AS A QUESTION, WITH THE LAW'S PARTIAL ANSWERS BESIDE IT

"Or will they just knowingly strong arm people because they know they can whether it's legal or not. Just to get them to shut up."

Entered as a question, as he put it. The silencing kind is named in the law — retaliation for speaking is unconstitutional — but the gates hung on it are printed at the same size: a retaliatory-prosecution claim requires proof of retaliatory motive and that there was no probable cause at all (Hartman v. Moore, 547 U.S. 250 (2006)); and since Nieves v. Bartlett, 587 U.S. 391 (2019), the existence of probable cause generally defeats a retaliatory-arrest claim at the door. Meanwhile the pressure that travels inside a lawful charge — sign this, take this, or face the worse number — is not a loophole in the doctrine; it is the doctrine (Bordenkircher, at §18). So the honest map, printed whole: pressure to make a person plead is legal by design; pressure to make a person shut up is illegal on paper and gated nearly shut in practice. The distance between those two sentences is the same distance this whole section measures.

THE SYMMETRY PRINCIPLE — IN HIS WORDS, RENDERED CLEAN

"If they want to use loopholes to snag people, people can use loopholes to get out."

Rendered clean under his legal-lane rule (vulgarity omitted, substance preserved — the rule is printed in the position box at the top of this page).

The record's gloss, beside it: the doors used here aren't hidden defects — they're the system's own published text. The suppression standards, Brady, Bearden, the hardship waivers, the DA's Deferred Prosecution Program (§21), deferred adjudication. The State reads that text to charge and collect; a defendant reading the same text and walking through the doors it leaves open is using the law, not evading it. Same words, same doors, other direction.

Researched and filed October 29–30, 2026 (binder §19). Doctrine in full at the Incentive Ledger door.

The consequence for getting presence wrong runs one way; the proof of presence runs on a highlighter. That contrast is documented. What anyone did with the highlighter is not claimed.

Section 20 · Abuse of Power, Favors, and the Money in Politics
The offenses the code itself names — and the line the courts drew around corruption.
DOCUMENTED · INFLUENCE: DOCUMENTED OR NOT DOCUMENTED

THE OFFENSES THE CODE ITSELF NAMES

  • Tex. Penal Code § 39.02 — abuse of official capacity: a public servant commits an offense if, with intent to obtain a benefit or harm another, he intentionally or knowingly violates a law relating to his office, or misuses government property, services, personnel, or anything of value belonging to the government that has come into his custody or possession by virtue of his office.
  • Tex. Penal Code § 39.03 — official oppression: intentionally subjecting another to mistreatment, arrest, detention, search, seizure, dispossession, assessment, or lien that he knows is unlawful; or intentionally denying or impeding another's exercise of a right, knowing his conduct is unlawful. (Element analysis at the Incentive Ledger door.)
  • Tex. Penal Code § 36.02 — bribery: the provable exchange — a benefit offered, promised, or given as consideration for an official's decision, opinion, recommendation, vote, or other exercise of discretion. This is also the transaction printed in §22's correction: a payment that buys an outcome from a court is this crime, and it appears nowhere in the documented record there.
  • Honest-services fraud, after Skilling: the federal honest-services doctrine (18 U.S.C. §§ 1341, 1343, 1346) survives Skilling v. United States, 561 U.S. 358 (2010), only as confined to bribery and kickback schemes — the Supreme Court limited it to the core it could sustain.

THE FAVOR QUESTION — ANSWERED BY THE TIES-CHECK DISCIPLINE

The documented count is printed at Door 1 of Side B (The Ties Check): across its core set of identifiable entries, exactly 1 documented family tie and exactly 1 documented connection (Ethan Couch — the wealth-based treatment argument actually made in court, with the judge's on-record statement that it did not influence her printed at the same size). The widened set adds exactly one more documented connection: the Hunter Biden pardon, whose documented why is the President's own statement. Purchasable doors are documented as structure — the treatment placement that made probation available to Couch, while the same judge found no program that would take a poor 14-year-old and imposed 10 years (the Structural Finding, Door 1). Influence is printed as documented or NOT DOCUMENTED — for everyone, either direction. A tie is a fact, never causation; causation prints only where a source documents it.

THE CAMPAIGN-MONEY LINE THE COURTS DREW THEMSELVES

Corruption in law means the provable exchange alone. Citizens United v. FEC, 558 U.S. 310 (2010), in its own words: "ingratiation and access are not corruption." A donor-friendly voting record, perfectly documented on both sides, is politics in law — which is exactly why this record's job is documenting the flows at full size: the flows are facts whether or not the law names them corruption, and the record prints them as facts, no further. The causation rail at Side B, Door 4 stands with this section: a donation is a fact; a donation beside a lighter outcome is a pattern; a trade is claimed only where a court has found one.

Researched and filed October 30, 2026 (binder §20). Statute text and case text as cited; the ties count from Door 1 of Side B, researched October 29, 2026.

Section 22 · The Comparison at the Top — The President's Cases
Weight, not value — the heaviest ledger in the system, beside the lightest, measured side by side.
DOCUMENTED · NO CONCLUSION ABOUT HIS CASE

THE LEDGER, PRECISELY

The current President of the United States: 4 cases, 91 counts.

  • One conviction — 34 counts (New York, falsifying business records): convicted on all 34 counts, and sentenced to an unconditional discharge — no jail, no probation, no fine. The convictions stand; the sentence imposed no punishment.
  • Three cases dismissed — the two federal cases (the election-interference case and the classified-documents case) and the Georgia case: dismissed or ended without trial, on the documented mechanisms in the next layer.
  • The gravity, printed precisely: it sits in the charges — fraud against the United States, obstruction, the January 6 case — not in any personal violent-crime conviction, because there is none on file in this ledger. Printed plainly, because precision is what keeps the comparison standing.

THE WHY, PRINTED FROM THE DOCUMENTS

  • The immunity ruling — Trump v. United States (2024): presumptive immunity for official acts, absolute immunity for core constitutional powers — the ruling that reshaped the federal election case's scope.
  • The sitting-president policy — the Justice Department's longstanding Office of Legal Counsel position that a sitting president may not be indicted or criminally prosecuted while in office.
  • The prosecutors' own motions after the election — the Special Counsel moved to dismiss the federal cases following the 2024 election, on that policy; the documents name the office and the policy as the grounds.
  • The sentence the New York court shaped around the office — unconditional discharge, imposed with the presidency-elect in view, so that no condition would burden the office.

Those mechanisms belong to the office. They are not precedent his court can apply to him, and this section does not present them as such.

BESIDE IT — HIS PROFILE, AND HIS ARGUMENT IN HIS WORDS

His profile: a non-violent misdemeanor, first offense, no harm — proceeding on schedule (Door 1; §00A). A free man is not the comparison's puzzle; the comparison is one legal order, heaviest cases and lightest, measured side by side.

His argument, filed in his words (parent conversation, October 30, 2026): the precedent has been set that the President is allowed to do it, therefore so is he — unless money is a deciding factor. And his principle closes the section:

"No case big or small, is above the law."

Whether money decided anything: CAN'T VERIFY. The documents name offices and policies, not payments — printed at the same size as the argument it qualifies. This section draws no conclusion about his case; the two columns stand as documented.

THE QUESTION, PUT AND CORRECTED — AT HIS REQUEST

Put by Tristan: "Therefore according to this correct me if I'm wrong if enough money is paid the crime is irrelevant correct?" — refined by him in the same exchange: "Case by case basis, of course."

The correction, as filed: as stated, no — and the record is stronger for saying so. Nowhere in the documented record does a payment buy an outcome from a court; that transaction is itself a crime (§36.02). The 34 convictions above stand — what the office shaped was the punishment (none), not the verdict on the past. Money does not erase the crime anywhere in the ledger.

What money is documented to buy is the doors, not the verdict. The treatment placement that made probation available to Couch, while the same judge found no program that would take a poor kid and imposed 20 years. Counsel, experts, bond freedom while the case pends. And running the other way: the fees-to-revocation pipeline (§§17, 23) can only trap someone who can't pay — a person who pays every charge on time never meets it.

The statement the record supports, in his refined frame: the crime is identical on paper either way; the consequences are not — money decides which doors are open, case by case, and the outcomes behind those doors range from probation to decades.

Researched and filed October 30, 2026 (binder §22). The Couch/Miller structural record stands at Door 1 of Side B (The Structural Finding).

Section 23 · Community Supervision (Probation) — Rehabilitation vs. the Fee Structure, in the Law's Own Terms
Not extortion in law, for one differing element — authorization. What the documents support instead is printed in its place.
DOCUMENTED IN LEGAL TERMS

WHAT PROBATION IS, LEGALLY

A contractual privilege, not a right — Speth v. State, 6 S.W.3d 530 (Tex. Crim. App. 1999): community supervision is "not a right, but a contractual privilege, and conditions thereof are terms of the contract entered into between the trial court and the defendant" — the suspended sentence in exchange for conditions the defendant "elects." That election framing is the foundation everything in this section stands on. The fee statute itself is at §17: art. 42A.652 sets the monthly reimbursement fee at $25–$60, payable as a condition, waivable on a finding of significant financial hardship.

THE STRUCTURE — FEES ENFORCED BY REVOCATION

  • Fee conditions in statute (art. 42A.652), enforced by revocation — judge-decided, on a preponderance standard, no jury, and counsel not automatic (Morrissey v. Brewer, 408 U.S. 471 (1972); Gagnon v. Scarpelli, 411 U.S. 778 (1973) — the documented due-process floor for revocation: a hearing, notice, and counsel case-by-case).
  • Bearden's protection — real, and narrowed in Texas practice. Art. 42A.751(i) requires the State to prove ability to pay only where nonpayment is the only allegation; where any additional violation is alleged, that proof is not required (Gipson v. State, 428 S.W.3d 107; Davis (2019)) — the full frame stands at §17 (The Legal Frame).
  • Duration, not completion, is what the structure pays for — supervision fees run for as long as a person stays in the system; no documented actor in the structure is paid when someone finishes (§17, Shelf Two; Harper).

THE EXTORTION QUESTION — ELEMENT BY ELEMENT, IN CORRECT LEGAL TERMS

ElementStatus in the probation structure
ThreatPresent — revocation and the suspended sentence behind it.
Thing of valuePresent — fees, labor, waivers.
ConsentManufactured between a cell and a fee schedule — and in law, consent: the "election" of the Speth contract.
WrongfulnessAbsent where authorized.

So, in correct legal terms: not extortion in law — for the one differing element, authorization. What the documents support instead is printed in its place: a coercive, fee-bearing mandate whose incentives pay for duration, never for completion, while stating rehabilitation as its purpose. The stated purposes stand at §17 (Shelf One): § 1.02(1)(B) names rehabilitation; art. 42A.301(a) authorizes conditions "designed to protect or restore the community or the victim, or punish, rehabilitate, or reform the defendant."

THE COUNTERWEIGHT — AT THE SAME SIZE

Supervision is also the door by which people serve no custody at all. In the county's own January 2026 numbers (§21): of 385 first-offense DWI dispositions in Tarrant County, 91 took deferred placement (23.6%) — the common non-conviction door — and community supervision is the mechanism by which a sentence is suspended rather than served. Both columns stand in this one section: the door out of custody, and the fee structure hung on it. The stated purpose and the documented structure are both in the record now. They are not the same document — and this section says exactly that.

Researched and filed October 30, 2026 (binder §23). Case and statute text as cited; §17 and §21 of this page carry the underlying record.
