Side B — And if not: how everyone else was treated

If the honest record says dismissal is not what the law gives here, this side turns to comparison — documented outcomes for people in similar, worse, and better situations, with the documented reason each one differed, or the gap printed as a gap when no reason is documented. Nothing on this side is invented: every cell below starts as an honest empty state and fills only from public records.

Door 1 · Similar
Same charge, same county, same blood-alcohol band — Tarrant County cases at BAC 0.20 and above, including above 0.24, each with its documented offender status, and every exit stage from intake to trial.
DOCUMENTED AS FAR AS PUBLIC RECORD ALLOWS

STATUS

DOCUMENTED — researched October 29, 2026, in two passes; January 2026 county data added October 30, 2026 (binder §21). The county's own published numbers for January 2026 — DWI—First Offense: 385 dispositions, 13 dismissed (3.4%), 238 convictions (61.8%), 91 deferred placements (23.6%); his court's court group: 812 misdemeanor dismissals of 2,135 dispositions (38.0%) — stand in full at Side A, Door 21, and the superseded "no published Tarrant DWI dismissal rate" rail in Door 5 has been replaced by them. The honest gap stands at the same size: no individually documented dismissal of an ordinary Tarrant DWI case was found in any public source. Both bands are now in this door: the 0.20–0.24 set and the above-0.24 supplement. A further layer, researched the same day, sits under this door's rule below: “Heavier than DWI, let off completely” — Tarrant County cases where charges heavier than a misdemeanor DWI ended with no conviction at all, each with its stated reason or REASON NOT STATED. This door is as complete as the public record allows. That is a statement about the public record, not about what happened in Tarrant County: the limits printed at this door — no BAC dataset, no way to search cases by BAC, and a findable set skewed toward crashes and contested cases — remain in force and are printed below at full size.

THE HONEST FRAME — PRINTED AT THE DOOR, AT FULL SIZE

There is no public dataset of Tarrant County DWI outcomes by BAC, and there is no way for the public to search cases by BAC. No portal has a BAC field. The county's own monthly court report — the Tarrant County Clerk / Texas Office of Court Administration Combined Monthly Report — reports misdemeanor DWI only as “DWI 1st” and “DWI 2nd,” with no BAC column, category, or field anywhere in the report.

So no “average outcome” at this BAC level exists to quote, and none is quoted here. Any statement of the form “defendants at ~0.22 in Tarrant County usually get X” cannot be supported from public sources.

This set is what is publicly findable, and it is skewed. Appellate opinions exist only where a convicted defendant appealed — contested trials, repeat offenders, and deaths. News coverage exists almost only where someone died. The most common real-world path for a high-BAC misdemeanor — a negotiated plea — usually generates neither an opinion nor a news story and is not searchable by BAC. These tables therefore describe what is publicly documented, not what typically happens.

The scale, printed at the same size: the Tarrant County Criminal District Attorney reports 4,956 DWI cases filed in Tarrant County in 2024 alone — with no BAC breakdown. Against that volume, the small findable set on this door is a sliver. Most high-BAC cases are publicly invisible.

The control fact, printed at the same size: a Tarrant County defendant, DeAujalae Evans, pleaded guilty to intoxication manslaughter at a reported BAC of 0.12 — below every band on this door — and a Tarrant County jury sentenced her to LIFE in prison (DA release, March 30, 2026). Sentence severity does not require a high BAC. Outcomes do not track the number alone — priors, a death, flight, and the litigation path move them, as the tables below show case by case.

Source: Tarrant County Clerk OCA Combined Monthly Report, April 2026 (read in full October 29, 2026): tarrantcountytx.gov — CriminalSummariesApril2026.pdf. Filing count source: Tarrant County Criminal District Attorney holiday release (2025): tarrantcountytx.gov — DA holiday release (4,956 DWI filings in 2024). Control-fact source: Tarrant County Criminal District Attorney release, March 30, 2026: tarrantcountytx.gov — Evans life sentence.

THE FIRST-OFFENDER FINDING — PRINTED AT THE DOOR, AT FULL SIZE

Tristan is a first offender — confirmed by him, October 29, 2026. The comparison that matters most for him is therefore the first-offender one. This door applied that lens to every row: each case now carries its offender status exactly as documented — FIRST OFFENDER where a source states it, REPEAT/PRIOR where priors are documented, and NOT STATED where no source says. Offender status is never inferred.

The honest finding: the publicly findable set contains almost no documented first-offender simple-DWI cases at these levels — and above 0.24, none at all. Every verified case above 0.24 involves priors, a crash, or both. That is because first-offender cases at these levels rarely generate an appellate opinion or a news story: they are the cases most likely to end in a negotiated plea that leaves no public trace carrying a BAC.

This absence is printed as a finding, not a gap to be filled by guessing. No first-offender outcome at these levels is quoted, estimated, or constructed here.

THE DOOR'S RULE — MAKE IT MAKE SENSE

His line, in his words, stated as this door's rule: "the crime itself is irrelevant if it's a slap on the wrist" — cases are weighed by what was actually done about them, not by what the charge was called.

So an outcome never stands alone on this door. Every case here that ended in someone being let off — dismissed at any stage, no-billed, declined, pleaded down, sentence suspended, acquitted, or relieved on appeal — carries, beside the outcome and at the same size: (1) the reason stated for that outcome, with its source; (2) the documented facts behind that reason, where a source gives them; and (3) where no reason was ever publicly stated, the printed label REASON NOT STATED — the absence is itself a fact on the record, never filled in, never guessed behind.

This page does not declare that an outcome makes sense or that it doesn't. It prints outcome and reason side by side at the same size, so the sense — or its absence — shows itself.

HIS PRINCIPLES — STATED AT THIS DOOR, IN HIS WORDS

Stated by Tristan, October 29, 2026. These are his principles, printed as he stated them. They govern how this door reads the comparisons; they are not findings about any case.

(i) Sentencing should be considered based off crime type. Outcomes across case types should form a ladder that tracks the crime's seriousness — the more serious the crime, the more serious the outcome. Where the documented outcomes on this door do not form that ladder — where a lesser crime drew more than a greater one — that disorder in the ladder is printed as a finding, not smoothed over.

(ii) The ability to pay money should have nothing to do with conviction or punishment. Whether a person can pay should not move whether they are convicted, or what their punishment is. Where the record on this page shows money touching an outcome, it is documented as fact under the rails already printed on this page — nothing more, nothing less.

(iii) The trace rule, with its hedge, in his words: anytime it happens they will do everything they can to keep it off the books, but it's written somewhere — if it has happened at all. The hedge governs absolutely: where a lawful record of a thing is found, this page prints it with its source; where nothing is found, this page prints the absence — NOT PUBLISHED, REASON NOT STATED, DOOR CLOSED — and never invents the room behind the door.

HEAVIER THAN DWI, LET OFF COMPLETELY — THE HONEST NEGATIVE AND THE LIMITS, PRINTED AT THE DOOR, AT FULL SIZE

Researched October 29, 2026. Under this door's rule — the crime itself is irrelevant if it's a slap on the wrist — this layer weighs cases by what was actually done about them: Tarrant County cases where a charge heavier than a standard misdemeanor DWI (Tex. Penal Code §49.04) ended with no conviction at all. 12 named, publicly documented people were found: 8 charged cases that were no-billed, dismissed after filing, or dismissed after a conviction was vacated, and 4 fatal-shooting cases a grand jury declined to take up where no formal charge had been filed. Every let-off below carries its stated reason at the same size, or REASON NOT STATED.

THE HONEST NEGATIVE: no named, publicly documented complete dismissal of an intoxication manslaughter, intoxication assault, or felony DWI case in Tarrant County was found. Publicly, complete dismissals in those categories exist only as unnamed defense-firm claims — no names, no case numbers, nothing that can be checked. They are not rows in any table below.

The aggregate rail — the only charge-specific figure that exists: for capital murder only, the Texas Defender Service examined 431 capital-murder cases charged and completed in Tarrant County from January 1, 2003 to December 31, 2022, and reported “Acquittal, No-Billed Indictment, or Dismissal” — combined — 7%. That figure combines acquittals, no-bills, and dismissals; the dismissal component is not broken out, and no dismissal count is back-calculated from it here. The report's text separately notes nine people were not indicted and seven were acquitted. Source: Texas Defender Service, The Use and Ultimate Outcomes of Capital Murder Charges in Tarrant County (an advocacy organization, not an official statistical body; built from Public Information Act requests and the clerk's portal, whose data the report itself calls incomplete): Texas Defender Service report (PDF) (link unverified).

The limits, printed at the same size: there is no public Tarrant County search by charge-and-outcome — public routes are defendant name or case number, and remote felony-record access is a paid subscription ($50 processing fee plus $35/month, per the District Clerk). Pre-filing exits leave no court file: a DA declination or a pre-indictment no-bill creates nothing to search, and grand-jury proceedings are secret, so no reason is ever officially given for one. Dismissed, no-billed, and acquitted cases are also routinely expunged, erasing them from public view over time. The county's total number of heavy-charge dismissals is NOT publicly knowable. The 12 found are a small, skewed sample, selected by newsworthiness and by who chose to publicize them — high-profile defendants, fatal shootings news cameras cover, and wrongful-conviction cases with a press release. Ordinary felony dismissals generate none of that and stay invisible.

The stated reasons, counted only across the 12 found — never a county rate: actual innocence established, 3 (Nolley, Hawkins, Wiggins); insufficient evidence stated, 2 (Gasser; Goines's felony); self-defense found on the DA's own evidence review, 1 (West); constitutional violation leading to vacatur, then dismissal, 1 (Toney — categories overlap: Hawkins also involved withheld evidence, and is counted under actual innocence); prosecutorial discretion with no substantive reason stated, 1 companion count (Goines's misdemeanor); REASON NOT STATED, 5 (Perkins, Clark, Miller, Roper, Wilson — all grand-jury actions). No dismissal stated to be for witness failure or speedy-trial violation was found in any named heavy-charge case.

ACQUITTED — NOT A DISMISSAL

ACQUITTED — NOT A DISMISSAL. An acquittal is a verdict after a charge was filed and fought; it is not the system letting a case go, and these five sit in their own table, never blended into the dismissal counts above. A not-guilty-by-reason-of-insanity verdict is not a plain acquittal either — it leads to commitment proceedings, not release — so the one NGRI case found is not in this table.

Cases ending in a plea deal, a refiled charge, or a standing conviction were excluded from every table in this layer — a felony dropped as part of a plea to something else is a conviction, not a let-off, and a charge no-billed and then refiled never completely exited.

THE LAW FOR THIS BAND — LAW LAYER: DOCUMENTED

The offense. Tex. Penal Code §49.04(a): a person commits an offense if intoxicated while operating a motor vehicle in a public place. “Intoxicated” includes an alcohol concentration of 0.08 or more (§49.01(2)(B)). The default first offense is a Class B misdemeanor (§49.04(b), minimum 72 hours' confinement).

At 0.15 or above, a first offense is a Class A misdemeanor. §49.04(d), verbatim: “If it is shown on the trial of an offense under this section that an analysis of a specimen of the person's blood, breath, or urine showed an alcohol concentration level of 0.15 or more at the time the analysis was performed, the offense is a Class A misdemeanor.” There is no separate Texas tier at 0.20 or 0.22 — everything from 0.15 upward is the same Class A. High BAC alone does not make DWI a felony; felony DWI requires priors (§49.09), a child passenger (§49.045), injury (§49.07), or death (§49.08).

The Class A range. §12.21: fine not to exceed $4,000, confinement in jail not to exceed one year, or both. For comparison, the Class B range (§12.22) — relevant because plea-downs to Class B occur (Davis, below) — is fine ≤ $2,000, jail ≤ 180 days, or both.

Deferred adjudication is statutorily unavailable at 0.15 or above. Tex. Code Crim. Proc. art. 42A.102(b)(1)(B) provides the judge may not grant deferred adjudication on a §49.04 offense if “the defendant's alcohol concentration … was 0.15 or more” at the time of the offense (art. 42A.102(b)(1)(C) also bars it where punishment may be increased under §49.09). At BAC 0.22, deferred adjudication is legally unavailable.

Sources: Tex. Penal Code §49.04 (FindLaw reproduction, current as of Jan. 1, 2026): codes.findlaw.com — §49.04; Tex. Penal Code §§12.21–12.22 (Justia): law.justia.com — Penal Code Ch. 12; Tex. Code Crim. Proc. art. 42A.102 (Justia, 2025 statutes, read in full October 29, 2026): law.justia.com — art. 42A.102. Statute texts read October 29, 2026.

App.—Amarillo June 30, 2023, mem. op., do not publish; transferred from the Second Court; County Criminal Court No. 2, Tarrant, Trial No. 1711862) .22 DWI with BAC ≥ .15, Class A under §49.04(d) Plea bargain: pleaded guilty to Class B DWI; $1,000 fine + 90 days' incarceration, both suspended for 18 months. One law-firm anecdote (a client called “Stacy,” DWI-with-child at .22, reduced to a misdemeanor) does not state the county and is UNVERIFIED as Tarrant County; it is excluded. Near-misses were also deliberately excluded to prevent false comparisons — including cases with only an extrapolated BAC range, cases below or above the band, and in-band cases from other counties.

FELONY REPEAT-OFFENDER DWI (§§49.04, 49.09) — ABOVE 0.24 · APPELLATE OPINIONS

The rail for this sub-table, at the same size as everything else on this door: these sentences were driven by prior convictions, not by BAC alone. Every verified case in this set is a repeat-offender felony. They are in this door by BAC and county, and they are not comparators for a first-offender misdemeanor case.

CaseBAC as statedChargeOutcomeOffender statusSource
Harold Michael Moore v. State, 508 S.W.3d 645 (Tex. App.—Fort Worth 2016), reviewed as Moore v. Texas, No. PD-1056-16 (Tex. Crim. App. June 7, 2017). Moore rear-ended a car stopped at a red light on the Highway 114 service road at Dove Road; the other driver and her 14-year-old daughter suffered bruises, scratches, and soreness (not serious bodily injury); their BMW was a total loss. .27 — "shortly after his arrest," per the Court of Criminal Appeals opinion Felony DWI, charged as a felony because of prior DWI convictions; Moore was on probation for a 2006 felony DWI Open plea of guilty; pleaded not true to the deadly-weapon allegation. Concurrent sentences of 18 years (new offense) and 10 years (revocation of community supervision for the 2006 conviction), as summarized by the Second Court. Appeal: the Second Court deleted the deadly-weapon finding and otherwise affirmed as modified; the Court of Criminal Appeals reversed that deletion and reinstated the original judgment, including the deadly-weapon finding. REPEAT/PRIOR — prior DWI convictions documented: a 2006 felony DWI (on probation for it) and misdemeanor DWI convictions from the 1990s. Justia — Moore, Court of Criminal Appeals opinionAppellate opinion, read October 29, 2026. The 18-year term is as summarized by the Second Court; the CCA opinion reinstates the original judgment without restating the term in the text reviewed.
Robert Othella James Lewis v. State, No. 02-13-00416-CR (Tex. App.—Fort Worth Mar. 12, 2015, mem. op.; Criminal District Court No. 1, Tarrant, Trial No. 1299359D). Late-night stop July 17, 2012 for speeding and failing to stop at a stop sign; field-sobriety tests refused; warrantless mandatory blood draw at a hospital under Tex. Transp. Code §724.012(b)(3). .27 DWI with a felony-repetition paragraph alleging two prior DWI convictions Jury found Lewis guilty; the jury assessed 5 years' confinement. Appeal: the Second Court held the trial court erred in denying the motion to suppress the warrantless blood draw, held the error was not harmless, and REVERSED and REMANDED. Let off — its reason, at the same size: the documented reason is the suppression error itself — the blood was drawn without a warrant, the trial court erred in denying the motion to suppress it, and the error was not harmless beyond a reasonable doubt. Those are the documented facts behind the reason, as the opinion states them. Follow-up: the State filed a petition for discretionary review (No. PD-0410-15); its final disposition is UNVERIFIED and is printed as unverified — it is not stated here that the conviction was reinstated, affirmed, or finally reversed. REPEAT/PRIOR — two prior DWI convictions alleged in the felony-repetition paragraph. Justia — Lewis opinionAppellate opinion, read October 29, 2026.
Gustavo Rodriguez v. State, No. 08-11-00345-CR (Tex. App.—El Paso 2013; appeal from Criminal District Court No. 2, Tarrant County, Trial No. 1254226R — a Tarrant County trial case decided by the Eighth Court on transfer; the trial court, not the appellate court, controls the county). Arlington convenience-store encounter, June 12, 2010; hospital blood test. .26 Felony DWI — the indictment alleged four prior DWI convictions; the State and Rodriguez stipulated to the first two (2002 and 2004) and the State abandoned the third and fourth Jury guilty; 8 years' imprisonment. Appeal: affirmed. REPEAT/PRIOR — prior DWI convictions stipulated. Justia — Rodriguez opinionAppellate opinion, read October 29, 2026.

INTOXICATION MANSLAUGHTER (§49.08) — ABOVE 0.24 · SEPARATE SUB-TABLE · A DIFFERENT OFFENSE

The rail for this sub-table, at the same size as everything else on this door: these sentences were driven by deaths, not by BAC alone. Intoxication manslaughter is a different offense from DWI. These cases are included only because the BAC is above 0.24 and the cases are Tarrant County, and they are not comparators for a misdemeanor case.

CaseBAC as statedChargeOutcomeOffender statusSource
Benjamon Ray Stewart v. State, Nos. 02-15-00164-CR through 02-15-00167-CR (Tex. App.—Fort Worth June 6, 2016, mem. op.; 372nd District Court, Tarrant, Trial Nos. 1336013D–1336016D). Early morning of July 24, 2013, on Interstate 30 in Arlington, Stewart's truck struck the family's Camry at 73–78 mph; Stewart fled and hid in brush. Killed: Najib Intidam, his wife Hanane Bakchine, and their 11-month-old daughter Nour Elhouda Intidam. TWO figures, both printed, neither resolved: the opinion's background section states a search-warrant blood sample showed an alcohol concentration of 0.289 four hours after the crash; its sufficiency section states Stewart stipulated his alcohol concentration was 0.268 four hours after the accident. The discrepancy is inside the opinion itself; neither figure is preferred here. Three counts of intoxication manslaughter with a vehicle, plus failure to stop and render aid Jury guilty on each offense, deadly-weapon findings true on the manslaughter counts. Sentences: 12 years (Najib) and 12 years (Hanane), concurrent, plus 10 years (Nour) consecutive, and 5 years (failure to stop and render aid) concurrent — an effective 22 years. Appeal: affirmed. A secondary injury-law blog rounds the BAC to ".28%"; that rounding does not replace the opinion's two figures.
State v. Erica Marie Hernandez (Tarrant County Criminal District Court No. 3). About 2:30 a.m. on August 6, 2014, Hernandez drove east on I-20 in Arlington, clipped a gasoline tanker truck, lost control, and crashed into the vehicle of Elaine Plummer, 57, of Arlington, who died shortly afterward. 0.28 — per the Tarrant County Criminal District Attorney's own release (blood drawn at the hospital approximately 30 minutes after the crash). Her later appellate opinion states no BAC, so the 0.28 rests on the DA release alone. Intoxication manslaughter, with a deadly-weapon allegation (the vehicle) Jury found her guilty; the jury recommended an 8-year prison sentence. Appeal (No. 02-15-00360-CR, Tex. App.—Fort Worth, May 18, 2017): affirmed — that opinion does not state any BAC. NOT STATED — no source cited here states Hernandez's offender status; not inferred. THE FULL PIPELINE — EVERY EXIT STAGE, INCLUDING EXITS BEFORE COURT

The comparison on this door covers the full pipeline of exits, not only cases that reached a verdict — declined at intake, no-billed, dismissed before court, dismissed at any stage, reduced, diversion and program outcomes, pleas, and trials. Every stage of the ladder is present below. A stage is populated only where a public source documents a case in these BAC bands at it. A stage where no publicly documented case in these bands could be found prints NOT PUBLISHED — NO PUBLIC RECORD FOUND. An empty stage is never printed as zero cases, and never filled by guessing.

StageWhat the public record holds in these bands (Tarrant County, BAC 0.20 and above)
Declined at intake — arrest made, no charge filed NOT PUBLISHED — NO PUBLIC RECORD FOUND. No publicly documented Tarrant County case in these bands was found at this stage.
Grand-jury no-bill NOT PUBLISHED — NO PUBLIC RECORD FOUND. No publicly documented Tarrant County case in these bands was found at this stage.
Dismissed before trial — charge filed, then dismissed before court NOT PUBLISHED — NO PUBLIC RECORD FOUND. No publicly documented Tarrant County case in these bands was found at this stage.
Dismissed at a later stage NOT PUBLISHED — NO PUBLIC RECORD FOUND. (Lewis, above, was reversed and remanded on appeal — a different event from a dismissal, and it is not counted as one.)
Charge reduced DOCUMENTED — Davis: a Class A charge under §49.04(d) pleaded down to a Class B DWI (table above). The reason for the reduction: REASON NOT STATED in the opinion — printed beside the outcome in the Davis row, under this door's rule.
Diversion / deferred / program outcome NOT PUBLISHED — NO PUBLIC RECORD FOUND. Separately documented as law, not as a case count: deferred adjudication is statutorily unavailable at 0.15 or above (art. 42A.102, in this door's law layer), so that particular exit does not exist in these bands at all.
Plea DOCUMENTED — Davis (plea bargain to Class B); Moore (open plea of guilty); Cook (pleaded guilty); Couch (pleaded guilty/admitted, juvenile). The let-off elements among these pleas — Davis's reduction and suspension, Couch's probation — carry their reasons, or REASON NOT STATED, in their own rows above, at the same size.
Trial — conviction DOCUMENTED — Shomefun (jury); Spence (bench trial); Voltmann, Gasca, Smith, Blanchard, Rodriguez, Stewart, Hernandez (juries).
Trial — acquittal DOCUMENTED — the unnamed defendant at 0.21 found NOT GUILTY by a Tarrant County jury (news table above; source is the defense firm's own release, labeled as such). The reason: REASON NOT STATED — a jury verdict states no reason.
Appeal — reversed DOCUMENTED — Lewis: REVERSED and remanded on the warrantless blood draw (the State's later petition is UNVERIFIED, as printed in his row).

Why the early stages are empty, printed at full size: pre-court outcomes are the least publicly documented of all. A case declined at intake or dismissed early rarely leaves a public trace carrying a BAC, and dockets cannot be searched by BAC — so a case that exited early is, to public BAC-based research, effectively invisible. The empty stages above record that invisibility. They do not record that no such cases happened; against 4,956 DWI filings in Tarrant County in 2024 alone, early exits certainly happened. They are simply not publicly findable by BAC, and this door prints that rather than a zero.

WHERE THE DATA COMES FROM

Public records only: the appellate opinions above (all read in full on Justia, October 29, 2026), the Tarrant County Criminal District Attorney releases cited above (Hernandez's BAC and disposition; the 2024 filing count; the Evans control fact), the news and firm reports above with their types labeled, and the county's official monthly report. Sealed records are not used. A charge label may itself reveal the enhancement tier — e.g., a charge styled “DWI BAC >= 0.15” — without giving the exact value; an exact BAC appears, if at all, only inside case-file documents, not in portal summaries.

THE LIMIT, PRINTED AT FULL SIZE

Sealed and cases vanish from public data. Whatever set this door builds is the survivors — the cases that stayed visible — and it will be read with that limit in mind.

Juvenile cases (e.g., Couch) are confidential in the court-record sense; what is known about them comes from news coverage, not portals. An ordinary high-BAC DWI arrest that produced no crash coverage and no appeal is effectively invisible to public BAC-based research.

COMPLETENESS — WHAT THIS DOOR NOW IS

Both research passes are complete — the 0.20–0.24 band and the above-0.24 supplement, researched October 29, 2026 — and this door is as complete as the public record allows. The honest limits printed at this door remain true and remain printed: there is no BAC dataset, cases cannot be searched by BAC, and the findable set is skewed toward crashes and contested cases. Wrong-county cases surfaced by the research were excluded and do not appear here. If a new public record surfaces — an opinion, a DA release, a reported disposition — it enters this door by the same rule as every row above: sourced, labeled, and never inferred.

THE TIES CHECK — THE COUNT, PRINTED FIRST

Researched October 29, 2026, across all Tarrant County criminal cases in this record — the charge type is irrelevant under this door's rule; only the outcome is weighed. The question put to every name was threefold: is a family tie, history, or connection documented; is the tie itself documented as connected to the outcome; and what why does the record state. The discipline of this layer is the discipline of the whole page: a tie is a fact, never causation; causation is printed only where a source documents it; stated reasons print at the same size; gaps and negatives print at full size; categories are never blended.

CategoryCountWho / what it means
Named entries in the core set21Every named person in this record's comparison sets — the DWI comparators, the heavier-charges-ended-without-conviction set, and the violent-crime dismissal set.
Not uniquely identifiable from the record5Davis, Spence, Lewis, Rodriguez, Hernandez — surnames alone, with no single matching defendant identifiable. Counted NOT IDENTIFIABLE, never as negatives, and no person is guessed at.
Identifiable entries16Couch, Shomefun, Moore, Stewart, Evans, Perkins, Gasser, Goines, West, Toney, Nolley, Hawkins, Wiggins, Brown, Flowers, Lerma.
Documented FAMILY tie — of the 16 identifiableExactly 1Ethan Couch — documented family wealth. No other identifiable entry carries a documented family tie, family wealth, or family prominence.
Documented CONNECTION between a tie and an outcome — of the 16Exactly 1Couch — the wealth-based “affluenza” defense was actually argued in court. The judge's on-record statement that it did not influence her is printed at the same size, in the layer below.
Documented “history” that is the person's OWN criminal record — not a family tie4Shomefun, Moore, Stewart, Evans — their priors print as the documented mechanism of their outcomes, in the layer below. A prior record is a lawful sentencing input, not a connection.
Neither tie nor connection11Perkins, Gasser, Goines, West, Toney, Nolley, Hawkins, Wiggins, Brown, Flowers, Lerma — subject to the skew printed at full size at the end of this layer: “no documented tie” means none in the accessible public record, not proof none existed.

The dominant documented drivers of unusually light or unusually harsh outcomes in this record are ordinary, and they are printed in the cases themselves: prior convictions and statutory enhancements, deaths and victim status, evidentiary collapse, withheld exculpatory evidence, and self-defense — not family connections.

THE TIES CHECK — THE ONE DOCUMENTED FAMILY TIE, AND THE ONE DOCUMENTED CONNECTION

Ethan Couch (Tarrant County, 2013). Age 16, BAC .24 (with Valium and marijuana), driving 68–70 mph in a 40-mph zone in his father's company truck; four people killed. He pleaded guilty to four counts of intoxication manslaughter and two of intoxication assault — ordinarily a second-degree felony, 2–20 years (Tex. Penal Code §§49.08, 12.33); prosecutors sought 20 years. Judge Jean Hudson Boyd (323rd District Court) sentenced him on December 10, 2013 to 10 years' probation with court-ordered rehabilitation — no incarceration at sentencing.

The documented tie: a wealthy family. His father, Fred Couch, founded Cleburne Metal Works. Treatment was ultimately at the North Texas State Hospital (Vernon) at $715/day, with the parents ordered to pay $1,170/month on a sliding scale; a roughly $450,000/year California facility was initially reported as proposed.

The documented connection — the only one in the core set: the connection is a defense actually argued in court. Defense psychologist Dr. G. Dick Miller, hired by the defense, testified that Couch was a product of “affluenza” — that his parents taught him wealth buys privilege and that he had learned no link between behavior and consequences. The defense argued rehabilitation over prison.

The counterweight, printed at the same size: Judge Boyd later stated specifically that the “affluenza” argument did not influence her; reporting of her statements says she believed Couch needed treatment and, given his parents' resources, could obtain better treatment in rehabilitation than in youth detention. Victim Eric Boyles's stated belief that money kept Couch out of jail is a victim-family belief, not a court finding, and is labeled as such.

Later record: Couch fled to Puerto Vallarta, Mexico, with his mother in December 2015 after a video surfaced appearing to show him at a beer-pong party. In adult court in April 2016, Judge Wayne Salvant imposed four consecutive 180-day terms (about 2 years). He was released April 2, 2018.

Sources: en.wikipedia.org — Ethan Couch; dallas.culturemap.com — Couch probation / “affluenza”; en.wikipedia.org — Jean Hudson Boyd. Read October 29, 2026.

THE TIES CHECK — FOUR WHOSE DOCUMENTED “HISTORY” IS THEIR OWN CRIMINAL RECORD, NOT A FAMILY TIE

For these four, the record documents a history — and the history is the defendant's own record. It prints here as what it is: the documented mechanism of the outcome, through the lawful enhancement channels printed at the end of this layer. None of the four carries a documented family tie, and none carries a documented connection.

PersonOutcomeDocumented history — the mechanismTie / connection
DeAujalae Evans (Tarrant) Pleaded guilty to intoxication manslaughter of a peace officer — Fort Worth Sgt. Billy Randolph, killed August 12, 2024 on I-35W. Because the victim was a peace officer, §49.09(b) elevated the offense to a first-degree felony, 5–99 years or life. A Tarrant jury sentenced her to life on March 30, 2026. Roughly 10 shots of tequila, BAC .12 measured hours later, driving the wrong way up an exit ramp, continuing about a quarter-mile and fleeing on foot — and she was on probation for an earlier aggravated assault in which she shot her girlfriend, with a no-alcohol condition. The life sentence sits inside a statutory range created by the victim's status; the documented aggravators above are the ordinary explanation, printed at full size. No family tie documented. NO DOCUMENTED CONNECTION.
Shomefun Oluwaseun (Tarrant) DWI conviction in County Criminal Court No. 10; 60 days in jail (Class B misdemeanor DWI range: up to 180 days). Affirmed on appeal in 2024 (Shomefun v. State, No. 02-23-00231-CR). A Nigerian national who entered on an F-1 student visa in 2011, with multiple law-enforcement encounters including DWI and disorderly-conduct arrests; a federal immigration judge denied bond as a danger to the community and ordered removal. Why the 60-day figure: REASON NOT STATED beyond the conviction record. No family tie documented. NO DOCUMENTED CONNECTION.
Stewart — two distinct Texas men; which one the set intended is NOT VERIFIED, and both print rather than a choice being made Benjamon Ray Stewart (Tarrant, 372nd): 2013 I-30 Arlington crash killed three people, including an 11-month-old; BAC .289; fled and hid. Convicted on three intoxication-manslaughter counts plus failure to stop and render aid — effectively 22 years (one secondary source says 27 total; the appellate opinion is authoritative). Stewart Le Richardson (Tarrant, 396th): 2009 Arlington crash, BAC about .25–.28; the child victim died in 2015. Pleaded guilty; 50 years for aggravated assault plus 20 years felony DWI, concurrent. Richardson's documented history is the sentencing factor in his record: nine prior intoxicated-driving convictions, 22 offenses in five states, probation nine times. For Benjamon Stewart: why = jury verdict on the documented facts. No family tie documented for either. NO DOCUMENTED CONNECTION.
Moore — probable identification: Antonio Moore (Bowie County, not Tarrant) Convicted of felony murder and intoxication assault from a May 24, 2014 crash (BAC .196; his passenger killed, another driver injured). Sentenced as a habitual offender to 99 years on each count, concurrent. His documented prior record is the stated enhancement mechanism — habitual-offender status under §12.42(d) (see the lawful channels, below). A different “Matthew Moore” of Dallas — 15 years on three intoxication-manslaughter counts by plea — also appears in the records and is not conflated here. No family tie documented. NO DOCUMENTED CONNECTION.

THE TIES CHECK — THE NO-CONVICTION SET: THE DOCUMENTED WHYS ARE ALL EVIDENTIARY

Across the heavier charges that ended without conviction, every documented “why” is an evidentiary or legal defect — withheld evidence, excluded physical evidence, discredited informants, self-defense, or insufficient evidence. No family tie is documented for any of them.

PersonWhat happenedDocumented whyTie
John Nolley (Tarrant) Convicted in 1998 of the 1996 murder of Sharon McLane (Bedford); life sentence; served 19 years. Conviction vacated May 2018; DA Sharen Wilson dismissed the charges October 3, 2018, on actual innocence. A bloody palm print at the scene excluded him — a grand-jury prosecutor's own note asked, “How can we indict Nolley if bloody palm is not his?” — and jailhouse-informant testimony was discredited amid withheld witness information. None documented. NO DOCUMENTED CONNECTION.
Michael Roy Toney (Tarrant) Death sentence for the 1985 Lake Worth bombing that killed three. Conviction overturned December 17, 2008 (Ex parte Toney); a specially appointed prosecutor dismissed the charges; released September 2, 2009. The prosecution withheld/suppressed 14 pieces of evidence impeaching its only two witnesses. The Tarrant County DA's Office recused itself in January 2009 — for the withheld evidence, not for any family relationship. The dismissal motion's stated reason was insufficient time to complete an independent investigation before a deadline; the state reserved the right to refile. None documented.
Roger Earl Hawkins Jr. (Tarrant) 1993 aggravated-sexual-assault-of-a-child conviction; served 10 years. Held actually innocent August 24, 2022; charges dismissed September 27, 2022. Per the Tarrant DA's own press release: the Conviction Integrity Unit's reinvestigation found innocence; 1993 prosecutors withheld favorable material evidence including alibi information; ineffective assistance of counsel contributed. None documented.
David Lee Wiggins (Tarrant) 1989 life sentence for a 1988 sexual assault of a 14-year-old; served 24 years. Vacated and freed August 24, 2012; exonerated October 12, 2012. DNA on the victim's clothing excluded him; the conviction had rested largely on the victim's identification, and neither fingerprint at the scene matched him. The Tarrant DA consented to the DNA testing. None documented.
Alfred Dewayne Brown (Harris County) Sentenced to death in 2005 for the 2003 capital murders of Houston Officer Charles Clark and Alfredia Jones. Conviction overturned in 2014; DA Devon Anderson dismissed the case June 8, 2015; declared actually innocent May 3, 2019. The Harris County DA had withheld phone records supporting his alibi. The dismissal stated insufficient evidence to corroborate the co-defendant's testimony; a later special prosecutor concluded Brown “could not physically have been at the crime scene.” The documented “connection” in his case runs the other way — prosecutorial suppression (see Brady, in the Knowledge Question layer of the Incentive Ledger door). None.
Curtis Flowers (Mississippi — not Texas) Capital murder of four people in 1996. Tried six times; four death sentences, all reversed; two hung juries. The U.S. Supreme Court reversed 7–2 in Flowers v. Mississippi (2019). The Mississippi Attorney General dismissed with prejudice on September 4, 2020. Prosecutor Doug Evans had struck 41 of 42 Black prospective jurors across the trials, violating Batson; the AG dismissed because conviction was nearly impossible and no new living witnesses remained. The documented driver of the harshness was the prosecutor's racial jury selection plus recanted informant/route witnesses. None documented.
Tristan (Avery) Lerma (Bexar County) Age 18; charged in 2023 with the murder of Michael Molina, 61, plus aggravated assault with a deadly weapon, deadly conduct, and a separate aggravated assault — all dismissed (reported October 2026). He still faced unrelated evading-arrest charges. Stated reason (Bexar DA, via KSAT/secondary reporting): “due to the unavailability of several key witnesses, which necessitated further investigation.” The case was returned to police and may be refiled; dismissal is not a finding of innocence. None documented.
Jessica Gasser (Tarrant and Rusk counties) Arrested July 2023 on injury-to-a-child / “medical child abuse” allegations. Rusk County charges dismissed at the DA's request in February 2024; the last Tarrant felony allegations were no-billed by a Tarrant grand jury on June 3, 2025, and dismissed by the DA for insufficient evidence (per her attorney's statement as summarized in secondary coverage). The child had been independently and repeatedly diagnosed with gastroparesis and hypoglycemia; the parallel CPS case was also dismissed. Insufficient evidence, as stated. Her attorney's claims about officials seeking fame are advocacy claims, labeled as such, not findings. Best sources are secondary. None documented.
Janie Perkins (probable identification; Tarrant) Age 63, of Azle; arrested November 6, 2025, on capital murder in the 1991 Arlington cold-case killing of Cynthia Gonzalez. A Tarrant grand jury returned a no-bill on March 20, 2026. Prosecutor Kim D'Avignon (to the Star-Telegram): “This is the difficult reality of many cold cases” — witnesses have died and no modern technological evidence exists. Her attorney's statement that “the Perkins family is grateful” is the only family mention in the record — it is not a tie. Police said she failed two polygraphs (inadmissible) and had shared a love interest who left her for Gonzalez — a personal-history/motive allegation by police, not a tie to any official. (Reginald Perkins, executed in 2009 for a different Tarrant County capital murder, is a different person and is not conflated.) None documented.
West (first name NOT VERIFIED; Fort Worth/Tarrant) Indicted for murder after a minor traffic accident and disagreement on/near University Drive; the charges were dismissed. Documented self-defense: DA Phil Sorrells's statement said a review of the evidence showed the other man, Shin, retrieved a handgun and pointed it at those present, and West fired in defense of himself and others. Sole source located is a forum copy of a WFAA report — lower confidence, labeled. None documented.

Sources: innocenceproject.org — Nolley dismissal; deathpenaltyinfo.org — Toney freed; Tarrant DA press release (Hawkins, September 27, 2022); innocenceproject.org — Wiggins; eji.org — Brown; exonerationregistry.org — Flowers; eltejanorgv.com — Lerma; fox4news.com — Perkins no-bill. Read October 29, 2026.

THE TIES CHECK — THE INVERSION, AND THE COUCH PARENTS

Gerald Goines (Harris County — printed as the inversion). The former Houston narcotics officer fabricated a confidential informant and a heroin buy to obtain the no-knock warrant for the January 28, 2019 Harding Street raid that killed Dennis Tuttle and Rhogena Nicholas. An earlier set of state felony-murder charges was quashed in April 2024 because the indictment did not adequately specify the underlying felony; he was then convicted of felony murder and sentenced in October 2024 to 60 years on each of two murders, concurrent, parole-eligible after 30 years, plus $20,000 in fines. His documented connection runs the opposite direction from this layer's question: as the investigating officer, his lies caused the dismissal or reversal of at least 164 cases he had initiated. No family tie benefiting him is documented.

Fred Couch (Tarrant). Ethan's father. Convicted in December 2016 of falsely identifying himself as a peace officer (Class B misdemeanor) after displaying a “search and rescue” badge to North Richland Hills officers in 2014; sentenced to 1 year of probation, 120 days in jail only if he violates, and a $260 fine. His relationship to Ethan is a fact; no source states it affected his sentence, and no documented effect is printed here because none is documented. WHY: REASON NOT STATED beyond the plea/verdict record located.

Tonya Couch (Tarrant). Ethan's mother; charged with hindering apprehension and money laundering for the December 2015 Mexico flight. Final disposition: NOT VERIFIED — bond history and failed drug tests are documented, but no verified final outcome was located in this research (a low-quality blog claim of “180 days” was excluded as unverified). Nothing is stated for her, because nothing verified was found.

Sources: reason.com — Goines case dismissals; police1.com — Fred Couch convicted. Read October 29, 2026.

THE TIES CHECK — NO TIE, AND THE OUTCOME STANDS ON ITS OWN RECORD

Crystal Mason (Tarrant). Cast a provisional ballot in 2016 that was never counted while on federal supervised release for tax fraud; convicted of illegal voting in 2018 and sentenced to 5 years (illegal voting is a second-degree felony in Texas, 2–20 years). The Second Court of Appeals acquitted her on March 28, 2024 — no evidence showed she knew she was ineligible; the Court of Criminal Appeals dismissed the State's petition (reported September 24, 2026, 5–4), leaving the acquittal intact. DA Phil Sorrells's stated position: she was on felony supervision, and “anyone who attacks [elections] with an illegal vote will be prosecuted.” Tie: none documented. The harshness explanation in the record is the prosecution's election-integrity stance plus her supervised-release status. Her acquittal turns on knowledge — the State could not prove she knew — and it is held here for the mirror that closes the Knowledge Question layer in the Incentive Ledger door, where the same element is measured against officials.

Aaron Dean (Tarrant). Former Fort Worth officer; indicted for the murder of Atatiana Jefferson (October 12, 2019). The jury rejected murder and convicted him of manslaughter on December 15, 2022; sentence 11 years, 10 months, 12 days (manslaughter range 2–20 years). His own documented history was in the record (a 2004 Arlington Class C assault-by-contact citation discussed in his hiring interview; supervisor notes on “tunnel vision”), but no source connects any tie to the outcome, and his police employment produced no documented lighter outcome — a jury convicted him on the lesser-included offense. Officer status is a fact; a benefit from it is not documented.

Sources: prisonlegalnews.org — Mason conviction tossed; en.wikipedia.org — Killing of Atatiana Jefferson. Read October 29, 2026.

THE TIES CHECK — THE WIDENED SET: ONE MORE DOCUMENTED CONNECTION, TWO TIES WITHOUT ONE

Hunter Biden — the second documented connection, and the only other one found. Son of President Joe Biden. Convicted June 2024 in Delaware on three felony gun counts (exposure up to 25 years) and pleaded guilty in September 2024 in California to nine tax counts (exposure up to 17 years). Before sentencing, on December 1, 2024, his father issued a “full and unconditional pardon” covering January 1, 2014–December 1, 2024. The father's official statement is the documented connection and the documented why: Hunter was “selectively, and unfairly, prosecuted,” “singled out only because he is my son,” “raw politics has infected this process,” and “I hope Americans will understand why a father and a President would come to this decision.” Printed at the same size: two federal judges had rejected Hunter Biden's selective-prosecution theory (one called it “nonsensical under the facts here”).

Robert H. Richards IV (Delaware) — tie documented, connection not documented. A du Pont family heir — documented family wealth/prominence. He pleaded guilty to fourth-degree rape of his 3-year-old daughter (originally charged with two counts of second-degree rape carrying a 10-year mandatory minimum each, per secondary reporting). Judge Jan R. Jurden imposed 8 years and suspended it entirely for 8 years' probation; the sentencing order's notes said the defendant “will not fare well” in prison (caution printed with the source: the phrase appears in the order's notes, not as a statement at the hearing, and may reflect defense argument). Delaware AG Beau Biden — himself the son of the then-Vice President — later publicly defended the plea, saying the case was weak, resting on a young child's testimony, with a strong chance of losing at trial. No source documents that Richards's family caused the outcome; the documented stated reasons are prison unsuitability (the order) and evidentiary weakness (the AG).

Brock Turner (California) — tie documented, connection not documented. Stanford swimmer; convicted of three felony sexual-assault counts, facing up to 14 years; prosecutors sought 6 years; the probation office recommended 4–6 months. Judge Aaron Persky sentenced him to 6 months in county jail (3 served) plus 3 years' probation. The documented family involvement is lawful mitigation: his father Dan Turner wrote a character letter to the court (“a steep price to pay for 20 minutes of action”). The judge's stated why: age, character, lack of criminal history, remorse, and that prison would have a “severe impact” on him. The sometimes-made claim that Judge Persky's own Stanford background was a connection is not documented as a connection to the outcome and is not printed as causation. A father's letter is lawful mitigation through art. 37.07 (below) — it is not, without more, a documented connection.

Sources: presidency.ucsb.edu — Statement on the pardon for R. Hunter Biden; en.wikipedia.org — Trial and sentencing of Robert H. Richards IV; abajournal.com — Turner / Persky recall. Read October 29, 2026.

THE STRUCTURAL FINDING — PRINTED AT FULL SIZE

The channel was a purchasable placement, not a relationship. Judge Boyd's own other cases document the structure. Eric Bradlee Miller (Tarrant, Judge Boyd, 2004) — the direct comparator the Star-Telegram itself drew against Couch: age 16, from a poor family (father absent, mother addicted to drugs, living with his grandfather), represented by a court-appointed attorney. He stole a running pickup (a separate felony — the documented distinguishing fact), bought vodka, crashed, and killed a 19-year-old motorist. Boyd sentenced him to 20 years; he was paroled in November 2008. Boyd acknowledged his “sad childhood” at sentencing. No source states Boyd sentenced him more harshly because of his family — and none is printed as motive here.

The unnamed 14-year-old (Tarrant, Judge Boyd, March 2012): admitted manslaughter for a single unprovoked punch that killed Mark Gregory, 40; sentenced to 10 years in juvenile custody. The documented why-it-differs-from-Couch (per the victim's mother/WFAA, via secondary sources, labeled): Boyd tried to give him rehabilitation too, but no treatment program would accept him — whereas Couch's parents could pay for a private placement. Name withheld as a juvenile.

Printed as what it is: documented structure — the court's preferred disposition existed only where a paid placement did — under this record's money-neutrality rail: structure is documented; motive is never imputed. It is not printed as a personal tie, and it is not printed as any judge's motive.

Sources: claimsjournal.com — Miller / Couch comparison; en.wikipedia.org — Jean Hudson Boyd; D Magazine (secondary, labeled) — the unnamed 14-year-old. Read October 29, 2026.

THE TIES CHECK — THE LAWFUL CHANNELS: HOW HISTORY AND CONNECTIONS LEGITIMATELY ENTER OUTCOMES

These are the documented structures, printed separately from any claim of undocumented influence — the doors through which history and character lawfully reach a sentence:

  • Prior-conviction enhancement — Tex. Penal Code §12.42: one prior final felony raises the degree; §12.42(d) habitual status (two sequential final felonies) = 25–99 years or life — the documented mechanism of Antonio Moore's 99 years, and the logic behind Richardson's 50. §49.09 enhances DWI with priors with no 10-year washout, and §49.09(b) elevates intoxication manslaughter to a first-degree felony (5–99 years or life) when the victim is a peace officer, firefighter, or EMS acting in lawful discharge of duty — the entire documented mechanism of Evans's life sentence.
  • Character and mitigation evidence at punishment — Tex. Code Crim. Proc. art. 37.07 §3(a)(1): either side may offer “any matter the court deems relevant to sentencing, including… the prior criminal record of the defendant, his general reputation, his character, an opinion regarding his character, the circumstances of the offense”; art. 37.072 §2 does the same, expressly including background and character mitigation, in capital cases. This statute is the lawful door through which Dan Turner's letter and Dr. Miller's “affluenza” testimony entered their sentencings.
  • Judicial recusal / disqualification — Tex. Const. art. V §11; Code Crim. Proc. art. 30.01: no judge may sit where the accused or the injured party is connected to the judge by consanguinity or affinity within the third degree, or where the judge was counsel or is the injured party. Gov't Code §74.059(c)(3) requires a judge to have another judge assigned to hear a recusal motion against him.
  • Prosecutorial recusal — Code Crim. Proc. art. 2A.105(a): bars a DA who formerly represented the defendant in the same matter. Under Coleman v. State, 246 S.W.3d 76 (Tex. Crim. App. 2008), a DA who is not legally disqualified may voluntarily recuse for good cause — to avoid a conflict or even the appearance of impropriety — and the court appoints an attorney pro tem; a trial court cannot force a DA's recusal. The Toney case (above) is the worked Tarrant example: recusal for withheld evidence.

Sources: statutes.capitol.texas.gov — Penal Code §12.42; law.justia.com — art. 30.01; law.justia.com — Coleman v. State; texas.public.law — art. 37.072. Read October 29, 2026.

THE TIES CHECK — THE SEARCHED-FOR NEGATIVE, AND THE SKEW, PRINTED AS A FINDING

Searched-for negative: no Tarrant County criminal case was found in this research in which a judge or DA recused because of a family relationship to a defendant. The recusals found were for misconduct (Toney) or in unspecified/civil matters.

The skew, printed as a finding: famous defendants get their families named in coverage and ordinary defendants do not; juvenile records are sealed (the Couch and Miller details exist only because of extraordinary coverage); plea reasoning is usually unpublished. So “no documented tie” means no tie in the accessible public record — not proof none existed. A surname-only defendant whose family was never written about cannot be cleared or accused by silence.

Door 2 · Worse, Treated Lighter
Higher BAC or worse facts — and a lighter outcome. The §21 cohort and the §22 ledger now stand here.
DOCUMENTED — §21 COHORT + §22 LEDGER

THE COMPARISON, WHEN IT EXISTS

Researched — October 30, 2026. The county is now supplied (Tarrant County, §00A), and the comparison stands in two documented places on this page: the §21 Tarrant cohort (Side A, Door 21) — the county's own January 2026 disposition numbers and the 23-case named cohort across all offenses, each with its stated reason or REASON NOT STATED — and the §22 ledger (Side A, Door 22): 4 cases, 91 counts, one conviction ending in an unconditional discharge and three cases dismissed, with the documented mechanisms printed. The heavier-charge cases that ended with no conviction at all are tabled in full at Door 1 (Similar), under its rule.

Each case filed here carries its documented WHY — priors, a crash, a plea, a different county, mitigation shown — printed beside the outcome. When no documented reason explains the difference, the gap is printed as a gap: "no documented reason found." That absence is recorded as part of the comparison.

Door 4 · The Money Layer
Known donations by defendants or their families to deciding officials — filed as records. §20's documented doors, and the question put and corrected, now stand here.
§20 ENTERED · INFLUENCE: DOCUMENTED OR NOT DOCUMENTED

§20 — THE DOCUMENTED DOORS, AND THE QUESTION PUT AND CORRECTED

Entered October 30, 2026, from binder §20 (Abuse of Power, Favors, and the Money in Politics — Side A, Door 20). The favor question is answered by this record's ties-check discipline (Door 1): documented count printed there — in the core set, exactly 1 documented family tie and exactly 1 documented connection (Couch), with the widened set adding the Hunter Biden pardon, its documented why stated by the President himself. Purchasable doors are documented (the treatment placement that made probation available to Couch). Influence is printed as documented or NOT DOCUMENTED — for everyone, either direction.

The question, put by Tristan and corrected at his request ("correct me if I'm wrong"): if enough money is paid, is the crime irrelevant — case by case, of course? The corrected answer, as filed in §22: as stated, no. Nowhere in the documented record does a payment buy an outcome from a court; that transaction is itself a crime (§36.02). The 34 convictions in §22 stand — what the office shaped was the punishment (none), not the verdict on the past. What money is documented to buy is the doors, not the verdict: counsel, experts, bond freedom while the case pends, the treatment placement. And running the other way, the fees-to-revocation pipeline (§§17, 23) can only trap someone who can't pay.

The statement the record supports, in his refined frame: the crime is identical on paper either way; the consequences are not — money decides which doors are open, case by case, and the outcomes behind those doors range from probation to decades.

THE CAUSATION RAIL — PRINTED BESIDE EVERY RECORD

A donation is a fact.

A donation beside a lighter outcome is a pattern.

A trade is claimed only where a court has found one.

Texas judges and prosecutors are elected, and their campaign filings are public. The pattern, if one exists, can be built entirely from legally open doors — and it will be printed as what it is, no further.

WHERE THE DATA WILL COME FROM

Public campaign-finance filings: the , county records, and the FEC. Only public filings are used.

Door 5 · THE EXITS BEFORE COURT
Every stage at which a case can end — counted, not only the cases that reached a courtroom. Researched October 29, 2026 against Door 1's bands; early exits print NOT PUBLISHED where no public record exists.
RESEARCHED · EARLY EXITS: NO PUBLIC RECORD FOUND

THE FRAME

A courtroom verdict is the last and smallest stage of a case. Most cases end earlier. Any honest comparison of "how people in similar situations were treated" must count every stage at which a case can end, not only the ones that reached a judge or jury.

THE STAGE LADDER

Each stage below is a counted bucket, with the documented reason recorded where the record gives one:

  • Arrest made, no charge filed — declined at intake by the prosecutor's office.
  • Grand-jury no-bill, where applicable.
  • Charge filed, then dismissed before trial.
  • Charge reduced before trial.
  • Pre-trial diversion or deferred disposition completed and closed.
  • Plea.
  • Trial verdict.

A case enters the comparison at whatever stage it ended, and the stage it ended at is part of the record. The earlier exits are data, not absence of data.

THE HONEST LIMITS — PRINTED AT FULL SIZE

Decisions made before filing are the least publicly documented stage in Texas. Some counties publish intake and dismissal statistics; many do not. Where a count is not published, this door prints NOT PUBLISHED — never zero, never an estimate presented as a count.

Dismissal reasons are recorded only where a public record states one; where no reason is stated, the entry reads REASON NOT STATED.

The sealed and expunged limit already printed on Side B applies with double force here: cases that ended early and were later sealed or expunged vanish from every public set, so this comparison, like all of Side B, describes the cases that remain visible, and says so.

STATUS — RESEARCHED OCTOBER 29, 2026

Researched October 29, 2026, against the comparison universe Door 1 now holds: Tarrant County, BAC 0.20 and above, both sets. Every stage of the ladder was checked, and the full stage-by-stage result is printed in Door 1's pipeline layer. In summary:

  • Populated stages — documented by public sources in Door 1: charge reduced (Davis), pleas (Davis, Moore, Cook, Couch), trial convictions, a trial acquittal (the unnamed 0.21 defendant), and an appellate reversal (Lewis).
  • Empty stages — declined at intake, grand-jury no-bill, dismissed before trial, dismissed at a later stage, and diversion/deferred/program outcomes: NOT PUBLISHED — NO PUBLIC RECORD FOUND for any Tarrant County case in these BAC bands.

Why, printed at full size: pre-court outcomes are the least publicly documented of all. A case declined at intake or dismissed early rarely leaves a public trace carrying a BAC, and dockets cannot be searched by BAC. An empty stage is therefore never printed as zero cases, and never filled by guessing. No counts or percentages are stated here, because no public denominator exists: 4,956 DWI cases were filed in Tarrant County in 2024 alone (DA release, cited at Door 1), and the publicly findable set at these BAC levels is a small, skewed sliver of them.

THE DISMISSALS LAYER — HOW A TEXAS DWI CASE ACTUALLY ENDS ON A DEFECT

Researched October 29, 2026. A Texas prosecutor dismisses by written statement of reasons filed with the court's consent (Tex. Code Crim. Proc. art. 32.02). Many dismissal orders state no reason; where the record gives none, this page prints REASON NOT STATED rather than inferring one. Outcome categories are kept strictly separate throughout this layer: an outright dismissal is not a suppression, is not a reversal on appeal, is not an acquittal, and is not a diversion or deferred completion ending in dismissal. Texas cases break at identifiable joints: the stop, the arrest, the blood draw, the breath/blood machinery and its witnesses, proof of operation, video, SFST foundation, speedy trial, and charging-paper defects. The documented record at each joint follows.

The expunction backdrop, printed because it shapes everything below: dismissed Texas DWIs are generally expunction-eligible (Code Crim. Proc. ch. 55 / art. 55A); DWI convictions are not expungeable. Dismissed cases therefore disappear from the public record by design — one structural reason no count in this layer is a census.

THE DISMISSALS LAYER — THE DOCUMENTED TEXAS CASES, CATEGORIES KEPT SEPARATE

  • State v. Villarreal, 475 S.W.3d 784 (Tex. Crim. App. 2014) — SUPPRESSION AFFIRMED, NOT A DISMISSAL. Nueces County; felony DWI; BAC .16. Blood was drawn without a warrant or consent under Tex. Transp. Code §724.012(b)(3)(B) (two or more priors); the officer testified he “could have” obtained a warrant and relied solely on the statute; no exigency was stipulated. The trial court suppressed; the Court of Criminal Appeals affirmed the suppression, holding the mandatory-draw statute is not itself a warrant exception after Missouri v. McNeely. It is not documented as an outright dismissal, and any source calling Villarreal “a dismissal” is overstating the record.
  • Gonzalez v. State (Tex. App.—El Paso 2018, No. 08-16-00286-CR) — SPEEDY-TRIAL REVERSAL RENDERED AS DISMISSAL. A DWI prosecution left dormant for years; all four Barker v. Wingo factors weighed for the defendant and the State failed to rebut presumed prejudice; the court reversed and rendered a judgment of dismissal. Outcome category: reversal on appeal ending in dismissal — a different animal from a trial-court dismissal.
  • The Bullcoming line — MOSTLY REJECTED IN TEXAS. Texas courts have largely rejected the Melendez-Diaz/Bullcoming attack on substitute technical supervisors. Settlemire v. State, 323 S.W.3d 520 (Tex. App.—Fort Worth 2010, pet. ref'd), relying on Melendez-Diaz's footnote 1, allowed a supervisor who did not personally perform every maintenance step; the Fort Worth court followed that line in a Tarrant County case, Benson v. State (Tex. App.—Fort Worth 2024, No. 02-23-00043-CR, appeal from County Criminal Court No. 6 of Tarrant County), where a retired supervisor had been replaced and the objection was overruled. The door that opens in national cases is, on this record, mostly closed in Texas.
  • Proof of operation — mechanism documented, example MARKETING. Texas has no statutory definition of “operating,” and mere presence in a vehicle is insufficient. A Harris County example (BAC .141, dismissal claimed because the State could not prove operation) comes from a defense firm's own case-result page — MARKETING, not verified, printed as such and never counted.
  • Officer failure to appear; DA intake. A single missed officer appearance in Texas ordinarily produces a reset, not a dismissal (defense-firm summary, labeled). No officially documented Tarrant County intake-declination dataset for DWI was found.

Sources: Villarreal opinion: law.justia.com — State v. Villarreal; Gonzalez: law.justia.com — Gonzalez. Read October 29, 2026.

THE KEY NEGATIVE — PRINTED AT FULL SIZE

THE JANUARY 2026 NUMBERS — TARRANT COUNTY'S OWN PUBLISHED COURT DATA (OCA COMBINED MONTHLY REPORT, JANUARY 2026; VERIFIED AGAINST ITS TOTALS). A Tarrant-County-specific DWI dismissal count is published in the county's own monthly court data — the statement that none exists is superseded by this layer (binder §21). In January 2026: DWI—First Offense — 385 dispositions: 13 dismissed (3.4%), 238 convictions (61.8%), 91 deferred placements (23.6%), the remainder other dispositions. Deferred, not dismissal, is the common non-conviction exit on paper. Across the misdemeanor courts in his court's own court group the same month: 812 dismissals of 2,135 dispositions (38.0%) — DWI—First Offense drew the lowest dismissal share of any major category. Full tables: Side A, Door 21 (§21).

The honest gap, at the same size: across all sources, no individually documented dismissal of an ordinary Tarrant DWI case was found — ordinary dismissals exist in the counts (13 that month) but are individually invisible in every public source. The county's total leniency picture is not publicly knowable, and this record says so instead of faking a census.

Defense-firm Tarrant figures conflict and remain excluded: one defense-marketing page reports a first-DWI "Dismissal 2.2%" (with 26.7% "Other/unresolved" in the denominator); another reports "10 to 14%." Both are marketing, they contradict each other, and neither is counted in this record. A fabricated citation on one defense site — "State v. Smith, No. 123456 (Tex. Dist. Ct. 2022)" — remains flagged as fabricated and excluded.

Source: Texas OCA, Annual Statistical Report FY2023: txcourts.gov — OCA FY2023 (PDF). Read October 29, 2026.

THE LANDMARK DOORS — NATIONWIDE, RIDING WITH THIS LAYER

  • Missouri v. McNeely, 569 U.S. 141 (2013): natural dissipation of alcohol is not a per se exigency; warrantless blood draws are judged case-by-case on the totality of the circumstances. The ruling Villarreal applied in Texas.
  • Birchfield v. North Dakota, 579 U.S. 438 (2016) (with Bernard and Beylund): a warrantless breath test is a lawful search incident to arrest; a warrantless blood test is not. A state may criminalize refusal of a breath test but may not criminalize refusal of a blood test — criminal refusal penalties survive for breath only. Beylund was vacated and remanded because “consent” extracted under threat of an unlawful criminal penalty may not be voluntary.
  • Melendez-Diaz v. Massachusetts, 557 U.S. 305 (2009) and Bullcoming v. New Mexico, 564 U.S. 647 (2011): lab certificates are testimony; the defendant may confront the analyst, and a surrogate analyst cannot substitute for the one who certified the result. Texas's narrowing application — mostly closed — is printed in the Texas layer above.
  • Lost or destroyed evidence — the doctrine, with its bar. California v. Trombetta, 467 U.S. 479 (1984) (itself a breath-sample case) and Arizona v. Youngblood, 488 U.S. 51 (1988): the State must preserve materially exculpatory evidence regardless of good faith; for merely potentially useful evidence — the usual category for lost DUI video — the defendant must prove bad faith. Most lost-video claims fail on the second prong. Printed at full size because it governs Door 7's evidence clock.
  • New Jersey — State v. Chun, 194 N.J. 54 (2008): the Alcotest 7110 was held generally reliable only with safeguards, including a 20-minute continuous observation period; results failing protocol (e.g., mouthpiece-change errors) were excluded case-by-case.
  • Minnesota — source-code litigation: State v. Underdahl (2009) split on source-code discovery; the consolidated litigation ultimately upheld Intoxilyzer 5000EN numerical results (4–3) while “deficient sample” results were excluded; the state replaced the machine with the DataMaster DMT.
  • Massachusetts — the largest documented event. The Office of Alcohol Testing withheld 432 failed-calibration worksheets (of roughly 2,000) in the Commonwealth v. Ananias litigation; Draeger Alcotest 9510 results from June 1, 2011 to April 18, 2019 were presumptively excluded, and a later Supreme Judicial Court ruling (Hallinan, 491 Mass. 730 (2023)) left ~27,000 defendants eligible to seek new trials — printed as ELIGIBILITY, not dismissals. Figures via defense-firm summaries of the SJC litigation, labeled secondary.
  • Philadelphia: 1,147 cases (Sept. 2009–Nov. 2010) were affected when 4 of 8 breath machines were found improperly calibrated; the DA offered new trials.

WHAT'S STILL NEEDED

  • A public record of a pre-court exit carrying a BAC in these bands, if one ever surfaces — it would enter here and in Door 1's pipeline by the same rule: sourced, labeled, never inferred.
Door 6 · VIOLENT VERSUS NON-VIOLENT — DISMISSED ON PROCEDURAL GROUNDS
Violent charges that died on a procedural defect, set beside the non-violent DWI record from the same research — outcomes and stated reasons side by side at the same size, categories kept separate, and no comparative rate manufactured.
DOCUMENTED CASES · NO RATE MANUFACTURED

THE FRAME

Researched October 29, 2026. The same procedural doors exist for violent charges as for DWI — suppression, limitations, speedy trial, withheld evidence — and the documented record shows them opening and closing on both sides of the violent/non-violent line. Cases are printed with their outcome category kept exact: dismissal is not reversal, reversal is not dismissal, and a dismissal reversed on appeal is printed as a door that closed. Where the record gives no reason, the entry reads REASON NOT STATED. This door prints; it does not declare a verdict.

VIOLENT CHARGES — TEXAS

CaseChargeWhat brokeOutcome category
Alfred Dewayne Brown (Harris County; recounted in Tex. 2020 compensation litigation) Capital murder (convicted 2005, death sentence) The prosecutor withheld phone records confirming Brown's alibi — a Brady violation. The Court of Criminal Appeals vacated in November 2014; the State then moved to dismiss because accomplice testimony could not be corroborated. Dismissed June 2015, after 12+ years, ~10 of them on death row. Post-conviction vacatur (Brady) → prosecutorial dismissal
Whitsey v. State (Tex. App. 1993) Aggravated sexual assault Offense January 14, 1985; indictment July 10, 1992 — beyond the 5-year limit (art. 12.01); facially defective, no tolling alleged; the trial court lacked jurisdiction. Reversal, indictment dismissed
State v. Shastid (Tex. App. 1997) Indecency with a child; sexual assault of a child (1981–82) Indicted May 20, 1993; absence from Texas did not toll limitations because no formal accusation existed before the period ran. Trial-court dismissal of indictment AFFIRMED on State appeal
Gallardo v. State (Tex. App. 1989) Aggravated rape of a child (December 1982) The 3-year catch-all limitation then in force; indicted January 20, 1988; later legislative extensions held not retroactive to already-barred cases. Reversal ending in dismissal (error to deny the motion to dismiss)
Tristan Avery Lerma (Bexar County, 2025; news report of court records) Murder; aggravated assault with a deadly weapon; deadly conduct (August 30, 2023 shooting; Michael Molina, 61, died) The judge denied the State's continuance; the DA dropped all charges the next day. Stated reason: “unavailability of several key witnesses”; the case was returned to police. Prosecutorial dismissal, refiling expressly preserved (“may be refiled”)

Sources: Brown: caselaw.findlaw.com — In re Brown; Whitsey: case.law — Whitsey (PDF); Shastid: case.law — Shastid (PDF); Lerma: eltejanorgv.com — murder case dropped in San Antonio. Read October 29, 2026.

VIOLENT CHARGES — NATIONWIDE

  • State v. Moore (Idaho 2022) — suppression → dismissal, affirmed. Second-degree murder (the shooting of Dr. Brian Drake). Moore invoked his right to counsel at least three times; interrogation continued; the confession was suppressed for the Miranda violation. Because the State had relied on the tainted confession at the preliminary hearing, the district court dismissed for insufficient probable cause without it; the Idaho Supreme Court affirmed the dismissal.
  • Curtis Flowers (Mississippi) — procedural reversal → prosecutorial dismissal with prejudice. Capital murder of four; six trials. Flowers v. Mississippi, 588 U.S. (2019), reversed on Batson grounds (across the trials the State struck 41 of 42 Black prospective jurors). The DA recused; on September 4, 2020 the Attorney General dismissed all indictments with prejudice, stating the case could not be retried on unusable witnesses, alternative suspects, and new exculpatory evidence.

Sources: Moore: law.justia.com — State v. Moore (Idaho); Flowers: houstonlawreview.org — Curtis Flowers. Read October 29, 2026.

DOORS THAT CLOSED — PRINTED AT THE SAME SIZE

  • State v. Echols, No. 11-19-00209-CR (Tex. App.—Eastland 2021) — dismissal REVERSED. Aggravated sexual assault of a child (Comanche County). The trial court dismissed with prejudice on speedy-trial grounds (~60 months of counted delay); the court of appeals reversed and remanded — the door opened at trial and closed on appeal.
  • Dragoo v. State, 96 S.W.3d 308 (Tex. Crim. App. 2003) — dismissal REVERSED. Murder (Tarrant County; convicted 1997, life) plus a companion felon-in-possession case. The Fort Worth court of appeals granted a speedy-trial dismissal of the possession case after years of delay; the Court of Criminal Appeals reversed. This is also the only Tarrant County item in this layer — and it is a dismissal reversed, printed as such: no Tarrant County violent-crime dismissal on procedural grounds with a stated reason was verified in this research.
  • Barker v. Wingo, 407 U.S. 514 (1972) — claim DENIED. Murder; 5+ years and 16 continuances; the speedy-trial claim was denied (the defendant had not wanted a speedy trial; no serious prejudice) — though the Court confirmed dismissal is the only remedy when a violation is found.
  • Arizona v. Youngblood, 488 U.S. 51 (1988) — door did NOT open. Sexual-assault evidence degraded by refrigeration failure; no bad faith, no violation, conviction reinstated.
  • State v. Torres (N.J. App. Div. 2000) — dismissal REVERSED. Triple cab-driver murders; the indictment was dismissed on double-jeopardy/fundamental-fairness grounds after a mistrial; reversed, retrial allowed.
  • State v. Moore (Ala. Crim. App. 2006) — dismissal REVERSED. Capital-murder indictment dismissed over prosecutorial misconduct; reversed: the remedy is a new trial; double jeopardy bars retrial only for misconduct intended to provoke a mistrial.

Source: Echols: law.justia.com — State v. Echols. Read October 29, 2026.

BESIDE THEM — THE NON-VIOLENT RECORD FROM THIS SAME RESEARCH

The DWI mechanisms in Door 5's dismissals layer and Door 3's dividing line operate identically in structure — suppression (Villarreal, blood), reversal-ending-in-dismissal (Gonzalez, speedy trial), evidence exclusion without dismissal (Rudd, HGN), error-but-harmless (McRae) — and, on this record, Texas appellate courts closed the door at least as often as they opened it for DWI defendants. The violent and non-violent records sit side by side here at the same size, and the ladder shows itself.

Source: Texas OCA, Annual Statistical Report FY2023: txcourts.gov — OCA FY2023 (PDF). Read October 29, 2026.

BOND — COMPLIANCE, BREACH, AND WHAT FOLLOWED
Bond conditions kept or breached, and what the court did next — counted by the same rule for everybody.
NOT YET RESEARCHED

THE FRAME

Bond conditions are the court's own rules for a person waiting inside a case. Whether a person kept them or breached them, and what the court did next, are documented facts in court records — and they are part of how a person was treated and how their case moved. This door counts them for the like cases in Side B's comparison, by the same rule as every other door.

WHAT GETS COUNTED

Where public records state it, each case in the comparison is entered with:

  • The conditions set — the bond conditions the court imposed in that case.
  • Each documented breach, by type — failure to appear, failed or missed test, interlock violation, new charge, or other condition violation.
  • The documented consequence that followed each breach — bond revoked, bond increased, conditions modified, warning, or NO ACTION RECORDED where the record shows none.

A breach with no recorded consequence is itself a finding, stated as a fact and nothing more.

Compliance is counted with the same care: cases with no documented breach are entered as NO BREACH RECORDED — a record statement, not a character claim.

WHERE THE DATA WILL COME FROM

Public court records only. Sealed records are not used.

THE HONEST LIMITS — PRINTED AT FULL SIZE

Bond records differ in completeness by county. Where a county does not publish them, this door prints NOT PUBLISHED, never zero.

The sealed and expunged limit of Side B applies here as everywhere: sealed and expunged cases vanish from public data, so the set this door builds is the cases that stayed visible, and it will be read with that limit in mind.

STATUS

Structure only — NOT YET RESEARCHED, matching Side B's current state. No cases or counts are stated here.

WHAT'S STILL NEEDED

  • The county.
  • His BAC band.
  • Public bond records for the like cases, when research begins.
Door 7 · TARRANT AGAINST THE OTHER COUNTIES
Tarrant County's courts and jail measured against Harris, Dallas, Travis, and Bexar — the official comparison where one is published, and the gap printed as a gap where none is. Researched October 29, 2026.
OFFICIAL COMPARISON WHERE PUBLISHED · GAPS PRINTED AS GAPS

THE FRAME

Comparison counties: Harris, Dallas, Travis, Bexar. Years are printed with every figure; years are never blended into a timeless ranking. Where a metric is not published by county, it is printed NOT PUBLISHED — never estimated. Custodial deaths print as raw counts, never rates. Allegations print as allegations, at the same size as convictions.

Criminal-case clearance rate (dispositions ÷ filings; above 100% = backlog shrinking) and criminal backlog index, FY2024–25 (September 1, 2024–August 31, 2025), from Tarrant County's published urban-county comparison of court-administration data:
