EFFICIENCY — JAIL: CAPACITY, OCCUPANCY, LENGTH OF STAY, AND THE COST GAP

Capacity (2021, Harris County CJCC comparison, official): Harris 10,566 beds; Dallas 8,746; Bexar 5,108; Tarrant 5,015; Travis 3,095.

Occupancy snapshot (August 2026, TCJS monthly data as compiled by a secondary source, labeled): Tarrant held 4,229 people in 5,015 rated beds (84.3%), and 71.7% of those held had not been convicted of the charge holding them (the pretrial share). Same-month snapshots for the other four counties: NOT PUBLISHED in the sources located in this research.

Average length of stay: Tarrant — 144 days, as reported to the National Institute of Corrections technical-assistance team (2024), with roughly 50,000 admissions and discharges a year. Harris — 160 days, down from a 200-day average, as reported to TCJS in early 2025. Dallas, Travis, Bexar: NOT PUBLISHED in the sources located.

TARRANT COST PER INMATE-DAY: NOT PUBLISHED — printed as the gap it is. What Tarrant publishes is budget totals only: Sheriff Confinement expenditures of $120,099,536 in FY2025 and $130,648,547 approved for FY2027; the NIC team reported a Detention Bureau budget of $113 million including $8 million for behavioral health but excluding medical care. No per-day figure is manufactured from those totals here. For scale, the one published comparator: Dallas — about $95.58/day (March 2026, county Criminal Justice Advisory Board minutes; the figure excludes booking fees and medical expenses), totaling nearly $20 million per month; Dallas's FY2025 total cost per bed was $88.47. Harris, Travis, Bexar per-day: NOT PUBLISHED in the sources located.

Sources: cjcc.harriscountytx.gov — 2021 Jail Comparison by County (PDF); mobettausa.com — Tarrant County (secondary compiler, citing TCJS); NIC technical-assistance report on Tarrant (2024): SO Tarrant County Report (PDF) (link unverified); tcjs.state.tx.us — Commission Meeting Minutes (PDF); dallascounty.org — CJAB minutes (PDF); tarrantcountytx.gov — FY27 budget hierarchy (PDF). Read October 29, 2026.

TREATMENT OF THE HELD — WHAT THE STATE INSPECTORS FOUND

TCJS compliance status (TCJS 2025 Annual Report, official). In calendar 2025 TCJS conducted 347 inspections, all unannounced, and issued 70 notices of noncompliance. As of December 31, 2025, 13 jails were noncompliant. Remedial orders were issued in 2025 to Cherokee, Leon, Rains, Robertson, Bexar, and Runnels; remedial orders for Harris, Liberty, and Falls remained in effect from 2024. TARRANT WAS NOT on the noncompliant list and does not appear among the noncompliant or remedial-order counties in this report — and the Tarrant Detention Bureau states it has passed TCJS certification every year since 1995 (per the county). Per TCJS's 2026 minutes, Harris remained in enhanced enforcement and had passed the first of two inspections required to regain compliance (its cited problem areas were supervision, time held before housing, and life safety; it still held roughly 950 inmates outsourced out of state); Bexar was likewise in enhanced enforcement after passing the first of two required inspections. Harris's 2023 notice of noncompliance cited inmates held in holding cells 63–70 hours against a 48-hour maximum, inmates not seen by medical staff within 48 hours, and a continuing failure to follow physicians' orders and provide medication.

Tarrant specifics (documented). The NIC technical-assistance report recorded 59 in-custody deaths over seven years, 54 of them occurring in hospitals after transfer. The Sheriff's Office's January 13, 2026 release on 2025 statistics states roughly half of the people entering the jail arrive with chronic health conditions and about 65% with a mental-health challenge; medical care is provided by JPS Health Network and behavioral health by MHMR. In February 2026 the Attorney General issued an opinion (requested by the Tarrant DA) reading Gov't Code §511.021(a) to require TCJS to appoint an outside investigating agency on the death of a prisoner in the county jail, but not to mandate appointment where the death occurred outside the jail, e.g., in a hospital — a distinction that matters in a system where most deaths occur after hospital transfer. Public testimony at TCJS meetings alleging dehydration/starvation deaths and uninvestigated deaths at Tarrant is recorded in TCJS minutes as testimony/allegation, not a TCJS finding.

Sources: tcjs.state.tx.us — 2025 Annual Report (PDF); tarrantcountytx.gov — Detention Bureau; tcjs.state.tx.us — Commission Meeting Minutes, Aug. 2026 (PDF); tarrantcountytx.gov — 2025 Custodial Death Stats release (PDF); TCJS notice as republished by a law firm (labeled): deanmalonelawfirm.com — Harris County jail inspection. Read October 29, 2026.

TREATMENT OF THE HELD — CUSTODIAL DEATHS AS RAW COUNTS, NEVER RATES

Texas Jail Project analysis (labeled secondary source; built from TCJS open-records data and Attorney General custodial-death reports):

County202320242025
Harris19919
Bexar181311
Dallas13912
Tarrant996
Travis341

These are raw counts, not rates: the jails differ greatly in size (see capacities above), so the table does not by itself rank the counties by deadliness, and no rate is manufactured here.

The overseer gap, printed beside the counts: a State Auditor review (reported by KERA) found 287 jail deaths statewide in January 2023–December 2024; in all 60 cases reviewed, TCJS was notified within 24 hours and assigned an outside agency, but half of the investigations were still open by early 2025 and three had been open more than two years; required limited inspections were missed for five counties including Bexar and Travis; and TCJS's December 2024 risk-scoring tool miscalculated scores for over 75% of jails reviewed. Part of what “the record” shows about every county is limited by the overseer's own documented gaps.

Sources: Texas Jail Project — Statewide DIC Analysis (PDF, secondary); radio.kttz.org — State audit finds lapses in Texas jail oversight. Read October 29, 2026.

TREATMENT OF VISITORS — THE PEOPLE WHO MUST COME TO THE JAIL

  • Tarrant: in-person visitation exists on a surname-based schedule (A–L Saturdays/Mondays, M–Z Sundays/Tuesdays, 9 a.m.–9 p.m.), per secondary guides. Remote video visitation runs through Smart Communications at 50 cents per minute in 15- or 30-minute blocks, per a secondary roster source — labeled secondary: the county publishes no rate. The county's own inmate-phone page names Smart Communications as the collect-call vendor and tells families they are responsible for the collect-call fee without publishing a per-minute rate — the absence of a published rate on the county's own pages is itself documented.
  • Dallas (official county page): video visitation through SmartInmate; every video visit must be scheduled at least 24 hours in advance; each visit is 15 minutes; on-site video visits are limited to 2 per week, off-site visits unlimited; all video visits are recorded and monitored, and participation constitutes consent.
  • Harris (official HCSO page): attorney video visitation is free, in 30-minute blocks Monday–Friday, and attorney in-person visits are available 24 hours a day. General-public visitation cost/detail was not extracted from the official page in this research — NOT PUBLISHED here.
  • Bexar (secondary): at-the-jail video visitation via IC Solutions; the initial weekly visits at the jail are free.
  • Travis: visitation model and cost NOT PUBLISHED in the sources located in this research.
  • No official per-county compilation of visitor complaints was found; documented complaints about visitation appear, where they appear at all, inside individual lawsuits and advocacy reporting and are not aggregated by any official source located.

Sources: texasinmaterosters.org — Tarrant County Corrections Center (secondary); dallascounty.org — Sheriff: phone/tablet/mail system; harriscountyso.org — Inmate visitation; texasinmaterosters.org — Bexar County Jail (secondary). Read October 29, 2026.

OFFICER BACKGROUNDS — THE LICENSING FLOOR, AND THE TARRANT CASES

The licensing floor is statewide (TCOLE), identical for every county: a jailer must be at least 18, hold a high-school diploma or GED, be fingerprinted with a criminal-history search, and pass a background investigation; a person may not be licensed if ever convicted of an offense above a Class B misdemeanor, of a Class B misdemeanor within the last ten years, or of a family-violence offense. A temporary jailer must enroll in the basic course by day 90 and complete training and pass the licensing exam within 12 months (Tex. Occ. Code §1701.310).

Tarrant — documented cases, labeled by outcome:

  • Rafael Moreno (detention officer) and Joel Garcia (lieutenant): terminated and INDICTED for murder in the April 21, 2024 death of Anthony Johnson Jr., whose death the medical examiner attributed to mechanical and chemical asphyxiation and ruled a homicide. An indictment is an ALLEGATION; no conviction of either man is reported in the sources found. A federal judge's February 7, 2025 order (dismissing the county from the family's civil suit) records the termination and indictment as facts. A county commissioner's official statement records a further documented failure: the two men were returned to the county payroll because the sheriff failed to follow civil-service dismissal rules.
  • Two jailers (names not carried in the sources used here): CONVICTED of falsifying/tampering with government records — cell-check records — in connection with the death of Javonte Myers, who died of an untreated seizure disorder after lying on his cell floor for hours (Star-Telegram reporting, via secondary republication). This is a conviction, not an allegation.
  • Staffing as a documented background fact: the NIC team found 701 custody staff with 196 of ~897 custody positions vacant (2024).

Comparison counties — at the same size: Harris — TCJS minutes (February 2025) record 175 new positions approved (150 detention officers, 25 deputies) against 115 open jailer positions, with a jail population just under 9,700 falling to about 7,500; the jail's TCJS-cited deficiencies (supervision, observation rounds, staffing) are in the treatment layers above. Claims in advocacy coverage that Harris staff “routinely pre-filled safety logs” are ALLEGATIONS from a secondary advocacy source, not a TCJS finding quoted in this research, and are labeled as such. No named indictment or conviction of a Dallas, Travis, or Bexar jailer was verified from the sources located in this research — printed as a search result, not as proof none exists.

DOOR CLOSED BY LAW — printed at full size: no official source publishes, by county, any roster or aggregate of jailer criminal records, terminations, or TCOLE license actions. TCOLE actions are individual, and internal personnel records are largely exempt from public disclosure. A county-by-county comparison of officer backgrounds therefore can only ever be built from the cases that reached indictment, conviction, or reputable reporting — a sample of the prosecuted and the publicized, not of the workforce.

Sources: train.fbctx.gov — TCOLE requirements; lawserver.com — Occ. Code §1701.310; law.justia.com — Johnson federal order (N.D. Tex., Feb. 7, 2025); tarrantcountytx.gov — Statement on fired jailers' return to county payroll; tcjs.state.tx.us — Commission Meeting Minutes (PDF). Read October 29, 2026.

How this page works

The standards this record is kept by.

Built only from what's proven, or honestly labeled

  • His account is labeled HIS ACCOUNT until records confirm it. An unverified fact is labeled UNVERIFIED. A door with nothing behind it yet says so.
  • Everything on this page is proven from legally obtainable records, with no bias in either direction. Facts that support this case and facts that cut against it are entered by the same rule, at the same size, from the same standard of proof. The officer's clean entries stand beside any adverse ones, and his own record stands unpolished beside both.
  • Statuses are dated and can change in either direction as information arrives.
  • Nothing here is legal advice. This page is preparation. His lawyer sees everything first.
  • Side B is built only from public court records and public filings, and it prints its own limits where it uses them.
  • Every law relied on in these doors carries its own record beneath its text: who wrote it; why they wrote it that way — their stated reasons, labeled as stated, or REASON NOT STATED where none is stated; and who it benefits as written and as applied, entered as documented effect. Effects are shown; motives are never invented. This record is kept in a completely unbiased, factual manner, whether it flatters or indicts anyone.

The compass

The bar across the bottom of the screen is the navigation dock — three stacks, each a disc over its pill. In the middle, the compass disc over the pill that reads Navigate: tap it and the navigation panel opens. The bar sits at the bottom by default; Settings, at the right end of the bar, moves it — right, left, top, or bottom — and the page layout moves with it, so nothing is ever covered. The choice is saved on your device only, for this page only.

  • At the left end of the bar, the ?! stack — a disc carrying the ?! over a pill reading What are we missing — fronts this page's own What are we missing section: tap it and you land on that section, open.
  • Inside the navigation panel, Previous part takes you back to the place you were before — going back lives there. The Top and Bottom bookends stand beside it, outside the folds.
  • Go to is two folded faces: This page (every place here, listed — tap one to go straight there; nothing in it leaves this page) and Other pages (the doors to the other pages under this umbrella, the most important first — the Thriving Plan, then Any Legal Case — The Record, then For Veterans — The Record — with the rest grouped below, Tools and For fun on their own shelves). Each of those opens its own page at its own address; your browser's back button brings you right back here.
  • At the foot of the panel, Last place names where you actually were, in plain words. Tap it and the panel simply closes; you stay where you are.
  • The compass is navigation only. Anything that is a setting — the dock position is this page's one — lives in the Settings sheet, behind its own face, never in the compass.

The compass works in portrait and landscape, on any device, and rotating mid-visit loses nothing: your last place, and where you've been, stay put.

Rabbit holes, and the reminder

Throughout the page, named things carry a small round button — a door into a rabbit hole: what the thing is, what it isn't, and how it works, in plain words. Time spent in the rabbit holes is not tracked by the page, so an optional reminder is provided for them.

The reminder is OFF by default. If you turn it on, pick how long you want to spend down the holes — 10 to 60 minutes — and when the time is up, a note appears at the top of the page. Nothing is locked and nothing is lost; it's a tap on the shoulder, not a door closing. It runs in memory only: it is not saved anywhere, and it resets when the page closes or refreshes.

Reminder is off.
